\lxSVG@closescope {{{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\end{aligned}

The vanishing of the generalized gradients
d
and
d\star
alone is insufficient to imply automatic consistency; there must vanish the third generalized gradient corresponding to the Young diagram of the partition
3=2+1
.

For Lorentzian signature
h
the Einstein-Maxwell equations are physical and admit a great diversity of much studied solutions. When
h
is Riemannian it is an observation due to
[
41
]
in the scalar curvature zero case and
[
73
]
in the general constant scalar curvature case that if
(M,h,J)
is a
4
-dimensional Kähler manifold with Kähler form
\omega(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=h(J\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)
constant scalar curvature
\mathit{S}
and Ricci-form
\rho(\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)=\mathit{Ric}(J\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,)
, then
(h,F)
solves (
2.15
) where

(2.21)

\displaystyle F=\omega+\tfrac{1}{2}(\rho-\tfrac{1}{4}\mathit{S}\omega)

is the sum of the Kähler form and half the primitive part of the Ricci form.

In a similar manner it can be shown that there exist nontrivial solutions to the more restrictive equations (
EM1
). By definition, a Kähler structure
(h,J)
has constant holomorphic sectional curvature
4\kappa
if and only if

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathit{Riem}-3\kappa(\omega\cdot\omega)=-\kappa(h\owedge h).

In this case
\kappa=\mathit{S}/24
and:

•

If
\kappa>0
(positive constant holomorphic sectional curvature) then, for
F=\sqrt{3\kappa}\omega
,
(h,F)
solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
EM1
) with
\epsilon=1
;

•

If
\kappa<0
(negative constant holomorphic sectional curvature) then, for
F=\sqrt{-3\kappa}\omega
,
(h,F)
solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
EM1
) with
\epsilon=-1
.

Although it is not the objective here to discuss in detail the equations for
p
-forms (
EM1
)-(
EM3
), it would be interesting to know what are all the solutions of (
EM1
) or (
EM2
), in particular whether in Riemannian signature when
p=2
there are any solutions to (
EM2
) besides the Flaherty-LeBrun solutions.

As mentioned, in some cases the stress-energy tensor remains divergence-free under a relaxation of the coclosed condition. For example, if
n+1=3p
and
c
is a constant the equations

(2.23)

\displaystyle dF=0,

\displaystyle d\star F=cF\wedge F,

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}+\Lambda h=\epsilon\mathcal{T}(F),\iff\mathit{Ric}-\epsilon\operatorname{\rho}(F\cdot F)=\tfrac{\kappa}{n}h,

imply that
\mathcal{T}
is divergence free by (
2.19
). The vanishing of the term
h(\iota(X)F,\operatorname{\delta}(F))
reduces to
\iota(X)(F\wedge F\wedge F)=0
, which is true because
3p>n
. Replacing
d\star F=0
by
d\star F=cF\wedge F
in (
EM1
) and (
EM3
) yields a hierarchy of equations generalizing the hierarchy (
EM1
)-(
EM3
) and specializing to it when
c=0
.

The equations (
2.23
) are a variant of the usual
supergravity equations

[
1
,
34
,
40
,
90
,
39
]
. The cited references use different conventions for the norms and exterior products than those used here, and such differences are not geometrically or physically meaningful, but given such choices fixing a value of
c
fixes a scaling that is consequential. More precisely, for a particular choice of the constant
c
the equations (
2.23
) in the case
p=4
recover the
n=11
supergravity equations as in
[
40
,
90
]
. This particular value of
c
is determined by the requirement of invariance with respect to the Poincare superalgebra; precisely it is necessary for the cocycle condition in
[
40
, Proposition
7
]
. On the other hand, in the discussion surrounding the proof of
[
39
, Theorem
4.1
]
it is shown that certain
11
-dimensional Lorentzian Sasakian manifolds with
F
an appropriate multiple of the pullback of the square of the Kähler form on the base of the characteristic fibration yield a solution of (
2.23
), although for a particular constant
c
in general having numerical value and sign different from those of the usual supergravity equations as in
[
40
]
. More generally still,
[
39
]
considers the modified equation
d\star F-cF\wedge F=p
in which
p
is a closed
8
-form built from the first and second Pontryagin forms of a Lorentzian Sasakian manifold. Under a technical hypothesis on the base of the characteristic fibration the form
p
is a multiple of
F\wedge F
, and together
[
39
, Proposition
4.3
and Theorem
4.5
]
yield more solutions of (
2.23
).

In the just mentioned generalized supergravity setting, as for the Einstein-Maxwell hierarchy or for the hierarchy for trace-free Codazzi tensors described below, the scaling of the auxiliary tensor coupled to the metric can be fixed up to the sign here denoted by
\epsilon
(although in a setting where the auxiliary tensor has some physical meaning such a scaling may already be regarded as inadmissible), but this sign depends on underlying geometric or physical considerations, and properties and existence of solutions depend on it in essential ways.

2.3.
Ingredients of the general scheme

At each level (
GS1
)-(
GS3
) the general scheme consists of a curvature equation and a set of generalized gradients.

The generalized gradients arise as orthogonal projections of
D\omega
.
Those that appear (the set
\mathscr{E}
) cannot be chosen freely because consistency of the couplings forces that they depend on the choice of the quadratic expression
\Phi(\omega,\omega)
. Formally, taking the derivative of
\Phi(\omega,\omega)
yields

(2.24)

\displaystyle D\Phi(\omega,\omega)=2\Phi(\omega,D\omega)=2\Phi(\omega,\sum_{\sigma}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega))

and the curvature equation together with the differential Bianchi identity forces the vanishing of some expression derived from this formal sum, in which there will figure nontrivially certain of the generalized gradient operators. Generally there will be considered a hierarchy of curvature equations that can be deemed full curvature, Einstein, and constraint equations, each obtained formally from the preceding by taking traces. The consistency conditions for the auxiliary equations on
\omega
arise as tautological consequences of the differential Bianchi identities applied to the curvature equations. For example, at the Einstein level the consistency equations derive from the vanishing of the divergence of the Einstein tensor of
h
, and will take a form generalizing the identity (
2.19
). Which generalized gradients can appear may be different depending on the level in the hierarchy. Consistency is easier for the Einstein and constraint equations than for the full curvature equations - as at these levels the consistency equations depend only on the traced differential Bianchi identities - and correspondingly a greater variety of auxiliary constraints are possible at these levels. On the other hand, this remark suggests that the most interesting choices are those that yield consistency at all levels. In these cases the fundamental equation is that at the top level, expressing the projective Weyl tensor of the metric in terms of some quadratic expression
\phi(\omega,\omega)
and the other equations are derived from it by taking traces. The main focus of this article is such a hierarchy for trace-free Codazzi symmetric tensors. On the other hand, for trace-free Killing tensors, a for exterior differential forms, consistency at the top level is not guaranteed simply by the vanishing of certain generalized gradients, although a consistent formulation at the Einstein level is available.

For simplicity the general scheme is described for a Riemannian
n
-manifold
M
with
O(n)
-frame bundle
\mathcal{G}\to M
. With minor modifications most of the discussion can be adapted for oriented manifolds or spin manifolds, in the latter cases replacing
O(n)
by
SO(n)
or
Spin(n)
. Likewise it could be adapted to pseudo-Riemannian manifolds, replacing
O(n)
by an orthogonal group of appropriate signature. In these cases some of the representation theory used in the definite signature case becomes more complicated, with corresponding complications for the general scheme described.

The following background is repeated from Branson’s
[
13
]
where it is explained lucidly.
The associated bundle
(\tau,\mathbb{V})
of the standard representation of
O(n)
is
T^{\ast}M=\mathcal{G}\times_{\tau}\mathbb{V}
. Because each irreducible
O(n)
-module
(\rho,\mathbb{W})
appears as a summand of some tensor power of the standard representation, the associated bundle
\mathscr{W}(\rho)=\mathcal{G}\times_{\rho}\mathbb{W}
is a subbundle of some tensor bundle whose sections are trace-free tensors. (Were
M
oriented and were
O(n)
replaced by
SO(n)
, in some specific cases, e.g. forms of middle dimension, it would be a subbundle of such a bundle.) The covariant derivative
D
maps a section
\omega
of
\mathscr{W}(\rho)
to a section of
T^{\ast}M\otimes\mathscr{W}(\rho)
, which is the bundle associated to the tensor product
O(n)
-module
(\tau\otimes\rho,\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{W})
. This tensor product decomposes into a direct sum
\oplus_{\sigma\in\mathscr{M}(\rho)}\mathscr{W}(\sigma)
of vector bundles associated with irreducible
O(n)
-modules each appearing with multiplicity one (see
[
13
]
; this follows from the Pieri rule). The orthogonal projection
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)
of
D\omega
on one of the summands is a
generalized gradient
or
Stein-Weiss operator
in the sense of
[
13
,
19
,
99
]
. The generalized gradients corresponding to a given Young diagram are indexed by the Young diagrams obtained from the given one by adding to it a single box or deleting from it a single box in such a way as to obtain a new Young diagram. (While this is correct for
O(n)
-modules, for
SO(n)
-modules there are further possibilities because on a manifold of even dimension
n
, the module of exterior differential forms of rank
n/2
splits as a sum of self-dual and anti-self-dual forms.)

In order to analyze what conditions can be imposed in terms of generalized gradients, it is necessary to examine the possibilities for the quadratic map
\Phi
appearing in (
2.24
). This is possible separately at the different levels in the hierarchy. Here it will be studied at the most complicated top level, the Ricci curvature level being similar but simpler.

Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{W}\times\mathbb{W}\to\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
is a symmetric bilinear
O(n)
-module map. Because
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\phi(\omega,\omega))
is an
O(n)
-invariant quadratic form on the irreducible
O(n)
-module
\mathbb{W}
, it must be constant multiple of
|\omega|^{2}
. It is convenient to normalize
\phi
by requiring that
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\phi(\omega,\omega))=h(\omega,\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Gamma(\mathscr{W})
.

Given a pseudo-Euclidean metric
h
on a tensor module
\mathbb{W}
,
\mathbb{W}_{0}
denotes the
O(h)
-submodule comprising elements of
\mathbb{W}
for which there vanish all traces and contractions with
h_{ij}
and
h^{ij}
. The convention here is that
\mathbb{V}_{0}
equals
\mathbb{V}
. For any tensor module
\mathbb{W}
with determined symmetries and valency,
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\in\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{W})
denotes the
h
-orthogonal projection onto the submodule
\mathbb{W}_{0}\subset\mathbb{W}
comprising completely trace-free tensors of the same symmetries and valency.

The choice of
\phi
is an important piece of data for the equations to be described. In general the equations will be most interesting when the trace-free part
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\phi(\omega,\omega)
need not vanish, so yields a nontrivial map
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\phi:\mathbb{W}\times\mathbb{W}\to\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
into the irreducible (if
n>4
)
O(n)
-module
\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})=\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\cap\ker\operatorname{\rho}
of Weyl curvature tensors (trace-free curvature tensors). To know that there are nontrivial possibilities it needs to be that the dimension of the space of
O(n)
-equivariant symmetric bilinear maps
\mathbb{W}\times\mathbb{W}\to\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
is positive (at the Einstein level one would consider instead
O(n)
-equivariant symmetric bilinear maps
\mathbb{W}\times\mathbb{W}\to S^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
). The problem of calculating this dimension is a special case of the problem of calculating what are called the triple multiplicities for the orthogonal group
O(n)
, namely the the dimensions of the invariant subspaces of threefold tensor products of irreducible
O(n)
-modules. There is a huge literature on this and related problems, for example
[
6
,
77
]
, which yields methods in principle adequate for calculating the dimension in the specific case of interest here, but which remain complicated. As even in the case of successful realization their development would require considerable space, and the objective here is only to argue that the data
\phi
appearing in the equations described later is in general nontrivial, only some special cases are described. On the other hand, to implement the scheme there is needed a basis of the space of such equivariant maps, not only the dimension of the space, and it is not clear how effective is the available general machinery in this regard.

In general, if
\omega_{i_{1}\dots,i_{k}}
has some specified symmetries, contracting it with itself on some subset of
k-2
indices and applying a linear combination of these two maps yields a map
\phi
of the desired sort, although proving its nontriviality may require some work. A precise statement follows. Let
\mathbb{V}
be the standard representation of
O(n)
and let
\mathbb{W}\subset\otimes^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
be a nontrivial irreducible
O(n)
-module. If
k\geq 2
, there is a nontrivial
O(n)
-equivariant symmetric bilinear map
\phi:\mathbb{W}\times\mathbb{W}\to\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
. The elements of
\mathbb{W}
can be identified with covariant tensors
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
, trace-free with respect to the
O(n)
invariant metric
h
on
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, having the symmetries encoded by a filling by the indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
of a Young diagram with nonincreasing row lengths given by a partition of
k
. The tensors of
\mathbb{W}
are antisymmetric in the indices filling any given column of the Young diagram, while there vanishes their antisymmetrization over a set of indices comprising the indices in a column and any index in a box to the right of the given column. If
\mathbb{W}
corresponds to a Young diagram with more than one row, then, after an isomorphism given by reordering the indices, it can be supposed that its constituent tensors are antisymmetric in their first two indices,
\omega_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-2}}=\omega_{[ij]i_{1}\dots i_{k-2}}
. The product

(2.25)

\displaystyle(\alpha\cdot\beta)_{ijkl}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{k[i}\,^{A}\beta_{j]lA}-\alpha_{l[i}\,^{A}\beta_{j]kA}-\alpha_{ij}\,^{A}\beta_{klA}-\beta_{ij}\,^{A}\alpha_{klA}\right),

in which
A
abbreviates
a_{1}\dots a_{k-2}
, satisfies
\operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\cdot\beta)_{ij}=\alpha_{(i}\,^{p_{1}\dots p_{k-1}}\beta_{j)p_{1}\dots p_{k-1}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\cdot\beta)=h(\alpha,\beta)
. By the nondegeneracy of
h
this shows that if
\alpha\neq 0
there exists
\beta\in\mathbb{W}
such that
\alpha\cdot\beta\neq 0
. It is not obvious, and this argument does not show, that
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\phi
is nontrivial, but in many cases this is in fact true provided
k\geq 2
and
n\geq 4
.

The preceding argument omits the case most relevant here,
\mathbb{W}=S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. For
k\geq 2
, define a symmetric bilinear
generalized Kulkarni-Nomizu product

\owedge:S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\times S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\to\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
by

(2.26)

\displaystyle(\alpha\owedge\beta)_{ijkl}=\alpha_{k[i}\,^{A}\beta_{j]lA}-\alpha_{l[i}\,^{A}\beta_{j]kA}.

in which
A
abbreviates
a_{1}\dots a_{k-2}
as in (
2.25
). (When
k=2
the map
\owedge
is half what is usually called the Kulkarni-Nomizu product.)
It is immediate from the definition that
(\alpha\owedge\beta)_{[ijk]l}=0
, so that
\alpha\owedge\beta\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
. There hold

(2.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\owedge\beta)_{ij}&=\alpha_{(i}\,^{p_{1}\dots p_{k-1}}\beta_{j)p_{1}\dots p_{k-1}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha_{ij}\,^{p_{1}\dots p_{k-2}}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\beta)_{p_{1}\dots p_{k-2}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{ij}\,^{p_{1}\dots p_{k-2}}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\alpha)_{p_{1}\dots p_{k-2}},\\
\mathit{S}(\alpha\owedge\beta)&=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle-\langle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}\alpha,\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}\beta\rangle,\end{aligned}

which yield the corresponding quadratic expressions needed for the lower levels of the hierarchy.
If
k\geq 2
, the restricted product
\owedge:S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}\times S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}\to\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
can be taken as
\phi
and by (
2.27
) satisfies the normalization
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)=h(\omega,\omega)
.
Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
show that if
\dim\mathbb{V}^{\ast}\geq 4
, then the orthogonal projection of
\omega\owedge\omega
onto the space of trace-free curvature tensors is nontrivial and any such
O(n)
-equivariant product is built from this map and its traces. These lemmas give an alternative and more concrete proof of the more abstract Lemma
2.2
which, while far from an optimal statement, serves to illustrate what needs to be proved in general and how it might be proved.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be an
n
-dimensional Euclidean vector space. Let
\mathbb{W}_{\lambda}\subset\otimes^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
be an irreducible
O(n)
-module of
h
-trace-free tensors with symmetries corresponding with a filling of a Young diagram whose row lengths are given by an integer partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1}^{m_{1}}\dots\lambda_{r}^{m_{r}})
of length
r=|\lambda|
. If
2\leq k
and
r\leq n/4
, there is a nontrivial
O(n)
-equivariant symmetric bilinear map
\phi:\mathbb{W}_{\lambda}\times\mathbb{W}_{\lambda}\to\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
, and such a map is unique up to scale in the case of a Young diagram of one row corresponding with
\mathbb{W}_{(k)}=S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
.

Proof.

By
[
69
, Theorem
2.5.3
]
or
[
76
, Section A.
6
]
(which refer to
SO(n,\mathbb{C})
modules) and that standard relation between irreducibles of a simple complex Lie group and its compact form, the multiplicity of
\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
as a summand in the decomposition of
\mathbb{W}_{\lambda}\times\mathbb{W}_{\lambda}
into
O(n)
-irreducibles is given by the Newell-Littlewood number
N_{\lambda,\lambda,(2,2)}
in the
stable range
where
2r=2|\lambda|\leq n/2
. By standard arguments For partitions
\mu,\nu,\tau
of
n
, the Newell-Littlewood number
N_{\mu,\nu,\tau}
is defined by

(2.28)

\displaystyle N_{\mu,\nu,\tau}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}c_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mu}c_{\beta,\gamma}^{\nu}c_{\gamma,\alpha}^{\tau},

where the sum runs over partitions of
n
and
c_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mu}
is the usual Littlewood-Richardson number (the structure constants for the ring of symmetric functions with respect to the Schur basis)
[
53
,
52
]
. In private correspondence, Alexander Yong explained to me a proof, using the formula (
2.28
), the nonnegativity of the Littlewood-Richardson numbers, properties of skew-Schur functions, and the usual Pieri rule, that
N_{\nu,\nu,(2,2)}\geq 1
if and only if
\nu\neq(1)
(the partition with one box) with equality if and only if
\nu
is a row or a column. Applied in the current setting this shows that
N_{\lambda,\lambda,(2,2)}\geq 1
with equality if and only if
\lambda=(k)
or
\lambda=(1^{k})
. Because
|(k)|=1
, this gives the desired conclusion when
|\lambda|\leq n/4
. By
[
69
, Theorem
2.5.3
]
this restriction is sufficient to conclude that the desired multiplicities are given by the Newell-Richardson numbers.
∎

There is a large literature calculating the triple multiplicities for threefold tensor products of irreducibles for classical groups as in
[
6
]
. Outside the stable range there are formulas that in principle serve to calculate the desired multiplicities available in
[
69
,
76
,
77
,
88
]
and other sources, but developing them here sufficiently to prove the desired claim would require too much space. The basic issue is that outside the stable range partitions do not uniquely determine
O(n)
-irreducibles - some columns are longer than
n/2
, and applying the Hodge star over the antisymmetric indices in a such columns yields via the diagram folding procedure described precisely in
[
69
]
an equivalent irreducible. Such equivalencies can result in hidden cancellations that complicate calculating multiplicities. In indefinite signature there are additional complications generated by nonpositivity. Nonetheless, it seems likely that the claim of Lemma
2.2
remains true with only very minor conditions provided the diagram of the partition
\lambda
contains at least two boxes.

Qualifications aside, the preceding serves on the one hand to support that nontrivial choices of
\phi
exist quite generally, and on the other hand to show that for
\mathbb{W}=S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, the normalization
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)=h(\omega,\omega)
determines the trace-free part of
\phi
uniquely as the trace-free part of
\omega\owedge\omega
.

To write down the hierarchy of equations to be considered, there are needed, in addition to
\phi
:

•

a set
\mathscr{E}\subset\mathscr{M}(\rho)
parametrizing the generalized gradients and depending on
(\rho,\mathbb{W})
and
\phi
; and

•

a stress-energy tensor associated with
\mathbb{W}
and
\phi
and having the form

(2.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\omega)=\operatorname{\rho}(\phi(\omega,\omega))+\tfrac{1}{2r}h(\omega,\omega)h,

where the nonzero constant
r
is determined by
(\rho,\mathbb{W})
and the choice of
\phi
via the requirement that the divergence of
\mathcal{T}(\omega)
vanish.

The sign and value of the nonzero integer
r
depend on the number of rows and columns in the Young diagram corresponding with
\mathbb{W}
and also the set
\mathscr{E}
specifying summands in the decomposition of
\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{W}
into irreducibles. Initially it can seem surprising that for
\omega
having a particular symmetry type there may be more than one choice for
\mathcal{T}(\omega)
, but this occurs already for trace-free symmetric tensors, for which there are two possible stress-energy tensors, one corresponding with the Codazzi operator, the other with the Killing operator. See (
3.39
) for the precise values of
r
when
\mathbb{W}=S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and
\phi=\owedge
for the two corresponding sets
\mathscr{E}
.

The hierarchy of equations (
GS1
)-(
GS3
) specializes to the usual constant curvature hierarchy when
\omega\equiv 0
. Each equation comprises two parts, a
curvature equation
and equations on the auxiliary tensor
\omega
.
The hierarchy is written so that formally tracing the curvature equation at each level yields the curvature equation at the subsequent level. This means that any choice at one level fixes the choices at lower levels. It is possible as well to consider the two-step truncated hierarchy beginning at the Einstein level; in this case the data that needs to be chosen is not
\phi
but an
O(n)
-equivariant symmetric bilinear map
\mathbb{W}\times\mathbb{W}\to S^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
.

In each equation of the hierarchy, that
(h,\omega)
satisfy the curvature equation need not imply that
\omega
satisfy the equations
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)=0
,
\sigma\in\mathscr{E}
, but in general it does imply some weaker equations, in the Einstein case via an identity like (
2.30
) derived from the condition
\operatorname{\delta}\mathcal{T}(\omega)=0
and in the full curvature case via the differential Bianchi identity for the Levi-Civita connection. This means that the consistency of the curvature equations and the equations for
\omega
is not automatic and forces some conditions on the set
\mathscr{E}
. The form of such conditions is most easily seen at the Einstein level.

At the full curvature level, the curvature equation in (
GS1
) implies that
\phi(\omega,\omega)
is in the kernel of the differential Bianchi operator. Because
\phi(\omega,\omega)
is quadratic in
\omega
and the differential Bianchi operator is first order, working out what it means for the former to be in the kernel of the latter yields via (
2.24
) the vanishing of a sum of generalized gradients of
\omega
paired in various ways with
\omega
.

As shown by Lemma
3.10
below, in the particular case of completely symmetric trace-free tensors and the Codazzi type generalized gradients the differential Bianchi identity follows from the vanishing of the generalized gradients so that the coupled equations are automatically consistent even at the level of the full curvature tensor. This automatic consistency is a distinguishing feature of the completely symmetric case.

Likewise, for the consistency of the coupled Einstein equations, there must be a set
\mathscr{E}\subset\mathscr{M}(\rho)
, depending on
(\rho,\mathbb{W})
and possibly
\phi
, such that, for some constants
\beta_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{R}
(that also depend on
(\rho,\mathbb{W})
),

(2.30)

\displaystyle h(X,\operatorname{\delta}\mathcal{T}(\omega))=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathscr{E}}\beta_{\sigma}h(\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}}(X)(\omega),\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)).

in which
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathcal{A}}(X)(\omega)
is the symbol of the first-order differential operator
\mathcal{A}
in the direction
X\in\Gamma(TM)
.

As is apparent from (
2.30
), for (
GS1
) and (
GS2
) to imply (
GS3
) it is not necessary that
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)
vanish for
\sigma\in\mathscr{E}
, rather only that some linear combination of
h(\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}}(X)(\omega),\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega))
vanish for
\sigma\in\mathscr{E}
. The previously mentioned example of the supergravity equations is an interesting example in which this extra generality applies. With such a condition in place of the vanishing of the
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)
the auxiliary equations of the hierarchy will be said to be
relaxed
.

All of the preceding can be formulated in more generality. First, the orthogonal group can be replaced by the orthogonal group of a metric of indefinite signature. Second, it can be supposed that
M
has a spin structure and there can be used sections of the bundles associated with irreducible
Spin(n)
-modules and the associated generalized gradients (these include, for example, the Rarita-Schwinger operators). Third, equations of the form (
GS1
)-(
GS3
) can be considered in which in place of
\epsilon\phi(\omega,\omega)
there appears a sum of terms of this same form but for tensors of various symmetry types.

Apart from consistency considerations there are also solvability considerations. For a general metric
h
, there may be no
\omega
solving the equations
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)=0,\sigma\in\mathscr{E}
. For differential forms, with
\mathcal{A}
being
d
or
d^{\ast}
, the exterior differential and its adjoint, the space of solutions is described by Hodge theory. Although for other symmetries there is no comparably powerful general theory yielding existence there are general vanishing theorems that describe obstructions to solvability. Such results at least suggest where to look for nontrivial existence results.

When the metric of a solution is Riemannian signature, strong results on the behavior of nontrivial solutions will be available when the sum of the Laplacians
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}^{\ast}\mathcal{A}_{\mu}
as
\mathcal{A}_{\mu}
runs over the generalized gradients
not
occurring in
\mathscr{E}
is elliptic. In this case qualitative properties of solutions can be deduced via the Bochner/Weitzenböck/Kato machinery that goes back to Calabi and Yau in particular. In Lorentzian signature the same calculations are generally valid, but the resulting partial differential equations are hyperbolic and their behavior and solvability has an entirely different character that, although worth exploring, escapes the purview of the current paper.

The Bochner Laplacian
D^{\ast}D
on
\Gamma(\mathscr{W}(\rho))
is a sum of Laplacian operators of the form
\mathcal{A}^{\ast}{\sigma}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}
as
\sigma
runs over all the set
\mathscr{M}(\rho)
associated with
(\rho,\mathbb{W})
. Which sums of operators of the form
\mathcal{A}^{\ast}_{\sigma}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}
are elliptic is described by Branson in
[
13
, Theorem
1.4
]
.

In the cases where this general hierarchy described above has been seen to be interesting, the complementary operator
\sum_{\sigma\in\mathscr{M}(\rho)\setminus\mathscr{E}}=D^{\ast}D-\oplus_{\sigma\in\mathscr{E}}\mathcal{A}^{\ast}_{\sigma}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}
is elliptic. This means that a solution of (
GS1
) satisfies an elliptic second order PDE with zeroth order terms quadratic in the curvature tensor of the metric. This is exactly a Weitzenböck formula in the sense of Semmelmann-Weingart
[
93
]
, where such Weitzenböck formulas are studied in the generality necessary for this general scheme. The equations (
GS1
) means that the zeroth order terms quadratic in the curvature tensor are reexpressible in terms of
\phi
as an expression quartic in
\omega
. Depending on the sign
\epsilon
and the value of
\Lambda
, such a formula can either be integrated, yielding Simons style integral estimates, or it can be bounded using refined Kato inequalities in the sense of
[
14
,
19
]
to yield a differential inequality that can be used to obtain bounds on the growth of
\omega
in the style of Calabi-Cheng-Yau.

A special feature of the exterior differential on differential forms is its diffeomorphism equivariance. This feature fails for other choices of generalized gradients.

For the general scheme, the coupled projective flatness equations formally resemble the usual Hitchin equations for Higgs fields. The conditions
\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}(\omega)=0,\sigma\in\mathscr{E}
are something like holomorphicity of the auxiliary field, while the equation
\mathit{Riem}-\epsilon\phi(\omega,\omega)=-\tfrac{\kappa}{n(n-1)}h\owedge h
says that a quadratic modification of the curvature determined by the auxiliary field is a multiple of the metric (the identity). In the special case of trace-free symmetric tensors on surfaces, that the symmetric
k
-tensor
\omega
be trace-free and Codazzi means it is the real part of a holomorphic
k
-differential
[
44
, Lemma
3.5
]
, and the coupled projective flatness equations (which collapse to the coupled Einstein equations on a surface) are in fact a special case of the abelian Higgs equations, as is explained (using somewhat different terminology) in
[
44
, Section
8
]
.

3.
Completely symmetric tensors

This section describes the particularization of the general scheme of Section
2.3
for trace-free symmetric tensors. The simplest instantiations of the general scheme occur when the number of generalized gradients is minimal, namely in the cases corresponding to antisymmetric tensor and symmetric tensors (Young diagrams with one column or row). The first case leads to the generalized Einstein-Maxwell hierarchy for
p
-forms already described. The second case leads to hierarchies for conformal Killing and trace-free Codazzi tensors. This is presented in Section
3.3
after some background material is described in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
.

3.1.
Algebra of symmetric tensors and curvature operators

Fix a
metrized vector space

(\mathbb{V},h)
. The graded vector space
S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})=\oplus_{k\geq 0}S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
of finite linear combinations of completely symmetric covariant tensors on
\mathbb{V}
is an algebra graded by degree with the product given by the symmetrized tensor product
(\alpha\odot\beta)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+l}}=\alpha_{(i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\beta_{i_{k+1}\dots i_{k+l})}
for
\alpha\in S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
and
\beta\in S^{l}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
. Using
h
,
S(\mathbb{V})
and
S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
are identified by index raising and lowering, where for
X\in\Gamma(S^{k}\mathbb{V})
,
X^{\flat}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=X^{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}h_{i_{1}j_{1}}\dots h_{i_{k}j_{k}}\in\Gamma(S^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
, and, for
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
,
\omega^{\sharp}\in\Gamma(S^{k}\mathbb{V})
is defined dually.

Let
\iota
denote the bilinear pairing
S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\times S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\to S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
defined, for
\alpha\in S^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and
\beta\in S^{l}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
with
k\leq l
, by

(3.1)

\displaystyle\iota(\alpha)\beta=\alpha_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\beta^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\,_{i_{k+1}\dots i_{l}}.

For
X\in\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
the degree
-1
and
1
operators
\iota(X)
and
\mathsf{s}(X)\in\operatorname{End}(S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast}))
defined by
\mathsf{s}(X)\omega=(k+1)X\odot\omega
for
\omega\in S^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
satisfy the relation
[\iota(X),\mathsf{s}(Y)]=h(X,Y)
. Graded linear operators
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}},\mathsf{h}\in\operatorname{End}(S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast}))
of degrees
-2
and
2
are defined by

(3.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\omega)=\iota(h)\omega,

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(\omega)=h\odot\omega,

\displaystyle\omega\in S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast}).

By convention,
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\omega)=0
when
k=1
. These operators are adjoints, meaning that
\langle\alpha,\mathsf{h}(\beta)\rangle=\langle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\alpha),\beta\rangle
for
\alpha\in S^{k}(\mathbb{V})
and
\beta\in S^{k-2}(\mathbb{V})
. In particular, the orthogonal complement in
S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
of the image of
\mathsf{h}
is the subspace
S_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})=\oplus_{k\geq 0}S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
of trace-free elements of
S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
, where, by convention,
S^{1}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}=\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
.

The degree
2
,
-2
, and
0
graded linear operators
\mathsf{X}_{+},\mathsf{X}_{-},\mathsf{Z}\in\operatorname{End}(S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast}))
defined by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}_{+}(\omega)=\tbinom{k+2}{2}h\odot\omega,

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}_{-}(\omega)=-\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}\omega,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Z}(\omega)=\tfrac{n+2k}{2}\omega,

\displaystyle\omega\in S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast}).

satisfy

(3.4)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{+},\mathsf{X}_{-}]=\mathsf{Z},

\displaystyle[\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X}_{+}]=2\mathsf{X}_{+},

\displaystyle[\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{X}_{-}]=-2\mathsf{X}_{-},

so generate an action of
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
on
S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
.
The operators
\iota
and
\mathsf{s}
satisfy the relations

(3.5)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{+},\iota(X)]=-2\mathsf{s}(X),

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{-},\mathsf{s}(X)]=-\iota(X),

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{-},\iota(X)]=0,

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{+},\mathsf{s}(X)]=0.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 4
. Suppose
k\geq 3
. For
\alpha\in S^{k-2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and
\beta\in S^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{X}_{+}(\alpha)\owedge\beta+(k-2)(k-3)\alpha\owedge\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta)&=h\owedge\iota(\alpha)\beta,\\
\operatorname{\rho}(\mathsf{X}_{+}(\alpha)\owedge\beta)+(k-2)(k-3)\operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\owedge\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta))&=\tfrac{2-n}{2}\iota(\alpha)\beta+(n-2)\langle\alpha,\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta)\rangle h,\\
\mathit{S}(\mathsf{X}_{+}(\alpha)\owedge\beta)+(k-2)(k-3)\mathit{S}(\alpha\owedge\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta))&=2(n-1)\langle\alpha,\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta)\rangle.\end{aligned}

(The term
\alpha\owedge\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta)
is zero if
k=3
.)

Proof.

A straightforward computation shows

(3.7)

\displaystyle 2\tbinom{k}{2}(h\odot\alpha)_{kiA}\beta_{jl}\,^{A}=\tbinom{k-2}{2}\alpha_{kiB}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\beta)_{jl}\,^{B}+(k-2)(\alpha_{kB}\beta_{ijl}\,^{B}+\alpha_{iB}\beta_{jkl}\,^{B})+h_{ki}\alpha_{B}\beta_{jl}\,^{B},

in which the uppercase Latin letters
A
and
B
denote the contracted multiindices. Antisymmetrizing (
3.7
) in
ij
and in
kl
yields

(3.8)

\displaystyle\tbinom{k}{2}(h\odot\alpha)\owedge\beta=\tbinom{k-2}{2}\alpha\owedge\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\beta)+h\owedge\iota(\alpha)\beta.

Rewriting (
3.8
) yields the first equation of (
3.6
). For
\sigma\in S^{2}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
there hold

(3.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rho}(\sigma\owedge h)=\tfrac{2-n}{2}\left(\sigma+\tfrac{1}{n-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\sigma)h\right),

\displaystyle\mathit{S}(\sigma\owedge h)=(1-n)\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}\sigma.

Applied to the first equation of (
3.6
) with
\sigma=\iota(\alpha)\beta
, the identities (
3.9
) yield the remaining equations of (
3.6
).
∎

Corollary 3.2
.

Suppose
k\geq 3
. For
\alpha\in S^{k-2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and
\beta\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\mathsf{X}_{+}(\alpha)\owedge\beta)=\mathsf{X}_{+}(\alpha)\owedge\beta-h\owedge\iota(\alpha)\beta.

Proof.

From (
3.9
) it follows that the trace-free part
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\mathscr{Y})
of
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
is given by

(3.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\mathscr{Y})&=\mathscr{Y}+\tfrac{2}{n-2}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})\owedge h-\tfrac{1}{(n-2)(n-1)}\mathit{S}(\mathscr{Y})h\owedge h=\mathscr{Y}+\tfrac{2}{n-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y}))\owedge h+\tfrac{1}{n(n-1)}\mathit{S}(\mathscr{Y})h\owedge h.\end{aligned}

Because
\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta)=0
, combining (
3.11
) and (
3.6
) yields (
3.10
).
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n
.

(1)

If
n\geq 2
, for all
k\geq 2
there are
\alpha,\beta\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
such that
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\owedge\beta)\in S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is nonzero.

(2)

If
n\geq 4
, for all
k\geq 2
there are
\alpha,\beta\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
such that
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\alpha\owedge\beta)\in\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})=\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\cap\ker\operatorname{\rho}
is nonzero.

Proof.

Choose nonzero
X\in\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and nonzero
\sigma\in S^{k-1}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
such that
\iota(X)\sigma=0
(for example,
\sigma
can be taken as the extension to
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
of a trace-free symmetric
(k-1)
-tensor on the orthocomplement of
X
). By (
3.5
),
\mathsf{s}(X)\sigma\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. Because
\iota(X)\sigma=0
, there holds
\operatorname{\rho}((\mathsf{s}(X)\sigma)\owedge(\mathsf{s}(X)\sigma))=|\sigma|^{2}X\otimes X
, which has nonzero trace-free part. This shows (
1
).

For
X,Y,Z,W\in\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there hold

(3.12)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 8&(X\odot Y)\owedge(Z\odot W)\\
&=(Y\wedge Z)\otimes(X\wedge W)+(X\wedge W)\otimes(Y\wedge Z)+(Y\wedge W)\otimes(X\wedge Z)+(X\wedge Z)\otimes(Y\wedge W),\\
4&\operatorname{\rho}((X\odot Y)\owedge(Z\odot W))=-2h(X,Y)Z\odot W-2h(Z,W)X\odot Y\\
&\quad h(X,Z)Y\odot W+h(X,W)Y\odot Z+h(Y,Z)X\odot W+h(Y,W)X\odot Z.\end{aligned}

Supose
\{X,Y,Z,W\}
is an orthonormal set (this requires
n\geq 4
). By (
3.12
),
(X\odot Y)\owedge(Z\odot W)
is nonzero and is contained in
\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
. If
k=2
there is nothing more to show, so suppose
k>2
. Because
n\geq 4
there is nonzero
\sigma\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
such that
\iota(X)\sigma=0=\iota(Y)\sigma
(extend any trace-free symmetric tensor on the orthocomplement of the span of
X
and
Y
). Because
\iota(X)\sigma=0=\iota(Y)\sigma
,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\tbinom{k+2}{2}^{2}(X\odot Y\odot\sigma)\owedge(Z\odot W\odot\sigma)=|\sigma|^{2}(X\odot Y)\owedge(Z\odot W)

is a nonzero element of
\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
. However,
Z\odot W\odot\sigma
need not be trace-free.
The tensors
\alpha=\tbinom{k+2}{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(Z\odot W\odot\sigma)
and
\beta=\tbinom{k+2}{2}X\odot Y\odot\sigma
are in
S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. There is
\mu\in S^{k-2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
such that
Z\odot W\odot\sigma=\alpha+\mathsf{X}_{+}(\mu)
.
By (
3.10
),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\sigma|^{2}(X\odot Y)\owedge(Z\odot W)&=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\left(|\sigma|^{2}(X\odot Y)\owedge(Z\odot W)\right)=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\left((\alpha+\mathsf{X}_{+}(\mu))\owedge\beta\right)\\
&=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\alpha\owedge\beta)+\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\left(h\owedge\iota(\mu)\beta\right)=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\alpha\owedge\beta),\end{aligned}

the last equality by (
3.11
). This shows that
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\alpha\owedge\beta)
is nonzero, so shows (
2
).
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 4
. Let
k\geq 2
. For any
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
not all zero, the
O(n)
-equivariant bilinear map
\phi:S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)\times S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)\to\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
defined by

(3.15)

\displaystyle\phi(\alpha,\beta)=a\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\alpha\owedge\beta)+b(\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\owedge\beta))\owedge h+ch(\alpha,\beta)h\owedge h,

is nontrivial and every nontrivial such map has this form for some
a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}
not all zero.

Proof.

By
[
9
, Lemma
4.3
]
the
O(n)
-module
S^{2}(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
has exactly one irreducible factor isomorphic to each of
\mathscr{MC}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
,
S^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
, and
\mathbb{R}
. Since a map of the form (
3.15
) is
O(n)
-equivariant, it suffices to prove that such a map is nontrivial provided at least one of
a
,
b
, and
c
is nonzero. For nonzero
a
or
b
this follows from Lemma
3.3
, while for nonzero
c
it is simply the nondegeneracy of
h
.
∎

Lemma
3.4
justifies that for symmetric tensors there is a nontrivial coupling
\phi
as in (
GS1
). Lemma
3.10
shows that there is a choice of such a coupling that is automatically consistent.

3.2.
Generalized gradients for symmetric tensors: trace-free Codazzi and conformal Killing tensors

This section describes the generalized gradients for trace-free completely symmetric tensors. Most of the material recounted in this section was presented in
[
43
, section
6
]
. Much of this material has been obtained before or since by others in various equivalent forms; see in particular
[
57
,
58
,
94
,
100
]
.

For
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
there are three generalized gradients,
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{T}
, and
\operatorname{\delta}
as indicated schematically in (
3.16
) for the case
k=4
.

(3.16)

\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.89 -18.1 M 4.89 -18.1 C 4.52 -20.18 3.67 -21.56 2.44 -21.56 L 2.44 -21.56 C 1.22 -21.56 0.37 -22.95 0 -25.02 C -0.37 -22.95 -1.22 -21.56 -2.44 -21.56 L -2.44 -21.56 C -3.67 -21.56 -4.52 -20.18 -4.89 -18.1}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\end{aligned}

These generalized gradients are defined in what follows.

Because the fibers of
TM
and
T^{\ast}M
carry canonically dual flat centroaffine structures, constructions, such as those of Sectin
3.1
, applicable to dual vector spaces
\mathbb{V}
and
\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
apply fiberwise to
TM
and
T^{\ast}M
without change.
The graded vector space
S(TM)=\oplus_{k\geq 0}\Gamma(S^{k}(TM))
(respectively
S_{0}(TM)=\oplus_{k\geq 0}\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(TM)))
comprising finite linear combinations of (trace-free) completely symmetric tensors is a graded Poisson algebra with the fiberwise symmetrized tensor product
\odot
and the Poisson bracket defined by

(3.17)

\displaystyle\{X,Y\}^{i_{1}\dots i_{k+l-1}}=kX^{p(i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}\nabla_{p}Y^{i_{k}\dots i_{k+l-1})}-lY^{p(i_{1}\dots i_{l-1}}\nabla_{p}X^{i_{l}\dots i_{k+l-1})},

for
X\in\Gamma(S^{k}(TM))
and
Y\in\Gamma(S^{l}(TM))
and any torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
. Since index raising and lowering using
h
induce commutative algebra isomorphisms
\flat:S(TM)\to S(T^{\ast}M)
and
\sharp:S(T^{\ast}M)\to S(TM)
, there results on
S(T^{\ast}M)
the Poisson bracket
\{\alpha,\beta\}
defined by
\{\alpha,\beta\}=\{\alpha^{\sharp},\beta^{\sharp}\}^{\flat}
.

Given a metric
h
, the connection in (
3.17
) may be taken to be its Levi-Civita connection
D
and a degree one operator
\mathscr{S}:S(T^{\ast}M)\to S(T^{\ast}M)
is defined as a multiple of the symmetrized covariant derivative by

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}}=\tfrac{k+1}{2}\{h,\omega\}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}}=(k+1)D_{(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k+1})},

\displaystyle\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M)).

The formal adjoint of
\omega\to-\tfrac{1}{2}\{h,\omega\}
is the divergence operator
\operatorname{\delta}:S(T^{\ast}M)\to S(T^{\ast}M)
defined by (
2.5
).
Straightforward computations show
\mathscr{S}
and
\operatorname{\delta}
span a two-dimensional irreducible
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
-module, meaning

(3.19)

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{+},\mathscr{S}]=0,

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{-},\mathscr{S}]=-\operatorname{\delta},

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{+},\operatorname{\delta}]=-\mathscr{S},

\displaystyle[\mathsf{X}_{-},\operatorname{\delta}]=0,

\displaystyle[\mathsf{Z},\mathscr{S}]=\mathscr{S},

\displaystyle[\mathsf{Z},\operatorname{\delta}]=-\operatorname{\delta}.

A
rank
k
Killing tensor
is a symmetric tensor
X\in\Gamma(S^{k}(TM)))
such that
\{h,X^{\flat}\}=0
; equivalently,
\mathscr{S}(X)=0
.

Recall that
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)\subset S^{k}(T^{\ast}M)
is the subbundle of trace-free tensors. Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}:\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
be the
h
-orthogonal projection onto the completely trace-free part and define the degree one
conformal Killing
operator
\mathscr{L}:S_{0}(T^{\ast}M)\to S_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
by

(3.20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathscr{L}(\omega)&=\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\{h,\omega\}=\tfrac{1}{2}\{h,\omega\}-\tfrac{k}{n+2(k-1)}h\odot\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=\tfrac{1}{k+1}\left(\mathscr{S}-\tfrac{2}{n+2(k-1)}\mathsf{X}_{+}\operatorname{\delta}\right)(\omega),\end{aligned}.

More explicitly, the conformal Killing operator
\mathscr{L}
is given by

(3.21)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}}=D_{(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k+1})}-\tfrac{k}{n+2(k-1)}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)_{i_{3}\dots i_{k+1})}.

If
X\in\Gamma(TM)
, then
\mathscr{L}(X^{\flat})
is the Lie derivative of the conformal structure
[h]
along
X
, for

(3.22)

\displaystyle 2\mathscr{L}(X^{\flat})_{ij}=2\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(DX^{\flat})=2D_{(i}X_{j)}-\tfrac{2}{n}D_{p}X^{p}h_{ij}=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\mathfrak{L}_{X}h)_{ij}=(\mathfrak{L}_{X}[h])_{ij}.

The identity (
3.22
) motivates use of the notation,
\mathscr{L}
, resembling that for the Lie derivative.

The
trace-free Codazzi operator

\mathscr{K}
on
\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))
is defined to be the trace-free part of the Codazzi operator
\mathscr{C}(\omega)
defined in (
2.6
). For trace-free
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathscr{K}&(\omega)_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=\mathscr{C}(\omega)_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}-\tfrac{1}{n+k-3}\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}h_{i_{s}[i}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)_{j]i_{1}\dots\hat{i}_{s}\dots i_{k-1}}.\\
\end{aligned}

By definition,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{[ij]i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{ij(i_{1}\dots i_{k-1})},

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{[ijk]i_{1}\dots i_{k-2}}=0.

Note that, for
\omega\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)=\Gamma(S^{1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
,
\mathscr{K}
is half the exterior differential,
\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{ij}=D_{[i}\omega_{j]}=\tfrac{1}{2}d\omega_{ij}
.

A
rank
k
Codazzi tensor
is a completely symmetric tensor
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{C}
. Equivalently,
D\omega
is also completely symmetric. The divergence of a trace-free Codazzi tensor vanishes, for
D^{p}\omega_{pi_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=D_{i_{1}}\omega^{p}\,_{pi_{2}\dots i_{k-1}}=0
, so the space of rank
k
trace-free Codazzi tensors is
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}=\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\mathscr{C}
. Properties of trace-free Codazzi tensors have been studied in
[
94
,
100
,
101
]
.

A
rank
k
conformal Codazzi tensor
is a tensor
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))
such that
\mathscr{K}(\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\omega)=0
. By definition a symmetric tensor is conformal Codazzi if and only if its trace-free part is conformal Codazzi. Trace-free Codazzi tensors and divergence-free conformal Codazzi tensors are the same thing.

Example 3.5
.

Let
h
be a metric on a manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
and suppose
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(T^{\ast}M)
satisfyies the differential Bianchi identity. Define
\sigma(\mathscr{Y})=\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})-\tfrac{1}{2(n-1)}\mathit{S}(\mathscr{Y})h
, so that
\sigma(\mathit{Riem})
is the Schouten tensor of
h
. Straightforward computations using (
3.11
) and Lemma
2.1
show

(3.25)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y}))=\tfrac{n-2}{2n}d\mathit{S}(\mathscr{Y}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\mathscr{Y})=\tfrac{2(3-n)}{n-2}\mathscr{C}(\sigma(\mathscr{Y}))=\tfrac{2(3-n)}{n-2}\mathscr{K}(\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})).

It follows that if
n>3
, then the following are equivalent:
\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})
is conformal Codazzi;
\sigma(\mathscr{Y})
is Codazzi; and
\mathscr{Y}
is divergence free. In particular, if
h
is conformally flat and
n\geq 3
, then the Schouten tensor
\sigma(\mathit{Riem})
is a Codazzi tensor, and if
n\geq 4
, a metric
h
has harmonic Weyl tensor (meaning
\operatorname{\delta}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\mathit{Riem}=0
) if and only if its Ricci curvature is conformal Codazzi.

For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
define

(3.26)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}(\omega)_{ii_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\tfrac{2k}{k+1}\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{i(i_{1}\dots i_{k})},

which is completely trace-free and satisfies
\mathscr{T}(\omega)_{i(i_{1}\dots i_{k})}=\mathscr{T}(\omega)_{ii_{1}\dots i_{k}}
and
\mathscr{T}(\omega)_{(i_{1}\dots i_{k+1})}=0
. Using

(3.27)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{[iji_{1}]i_{2}\dots i_{k-1}}=0

it can be checked that
\mathscr{T}(\omega)_{[ij]i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}
, so that each of
\mathscr{K}
and
\mathscr{T}
determines the other on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
. The preceding implies that the kernels of
\mathscr{K}
and
\mathscr{T}
on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
are the same.

The linear map
\mathsf{i}_{h}:\Gamma(S^{k-1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(T^{\ast}M\otimes S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
defined by

(3.28)

\displaystyle\mathsf{i}_{h}(\omega)_{ii_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}h_{i(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})}+\tfrac{k(1-k)}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})i},

(for
f\in C^{\infty}(M)=\Gamma(S^{0}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
,
\mathsf{i}_{h}(f)_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{n}fh_{ij}
)
is characterized by the properties that its image is contained in
\Gamma(T^{\ast}M\otimes S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
and that the nontrivial traces of
\mathsf{i}_{h}(\omega)
equal
\omega
. In particular,
\mathsf{i}_{h}
is injective.

Recall the definition (
3.26
) of the operator
\mathscr{T}
.
The identity (
3.30
) of Lemma
3.6
is a special case of
[
12
, Lemma
2.24
]
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
there hold

(3.29)

\displaystyle D\omega

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}(\omega)+\mathscr{T}(\omega)+\mathsf{i}_{h}(\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)),

(3.30)

\displaystyle|D\omega|^{2}

\displaystyle-|\mathscr{L}(\omega)|^{2}-\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}|\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)|^{2}=|\mathscr{T}(\omega)|^{2}=\tfrac{2k}{k+1}|\mathscr{K}(\omega)|^{2}.

Proof.

Substituting the definitions of
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{T}
, and
\mathscr{K}
into
D_{i}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=D_{(i}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k})}+\tfrac{2}{k+1}\sum_{s=1}^{k}D_{[i}\omega_{i_{s}]i_{1}\dots\hat{i}_{s}\dots i_{k}}
and simplifying the trace terms yields (
3.29
). Pairing (
3.29
) with itself yields (
3.30
).
∎

Symmetrizing (
3.29
) recovers (
3.20
).
It is immediate from (
3.30
) that
\ker D\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))=\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.

The identity (
3.29
) of Lemma
3.6
justifies that
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{T}
, and
\operatorname{\delta}
are the generalized gradients on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
. Together with the equivalence of
\mathscr{K}
and
\mathscr{T}
as in previous paragraph this justifies regarding
\mathscr{K}
as a generalized gradient or, at any rate, using it instead of
\mathscr{T}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

On an
n
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold
(M,h)
, the operators
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{K}
, and
\operatorname{\delta}
are conformally invariant in the sense that for
0<f\in C^{\infty}(M)
there hold

(3.31)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{fh}(f^{k}\omega)=f^{k}\mathscr{L}_{h}(\omega),

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}_{fh}(f^{(k-1)/2}\omega)=f^{(k-1)/2}\mathscr{K}_{h}(\omega),

\displaystyle f\operatorname{\delta}_{fh}(f^{(2-n)/2}\omega)=f^{(2-n)/2}\operatorname{\delta}_{h}(\omega).

Proof.

The Levi-Civita connections
\tilde{D}
and
D
of conformally related pseudo-Riemannian metrics
fh_{ij}
and
h_{ij}
are related by
\tilde{D}-D=2\sigma_{(i}\delta_{j)}\,^{k}-h_{ij}h^{kp}\sigma_{p}
with
2\sigma_{i}=d\log f_{i}
and
\sigma^{i}=h^{ip}\sigma_{p}
. Write
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)
for the symbol of
\mathscr{L}
applied to
Z\in\Gamma(TM)
and
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
, and similarly for
\mathscr{K}
and
\operatorname{\delta}
.
For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
,
0<f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, there hold

(3.32)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathscr{L}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)=f^{\alpha}\left(\mathscr{L}_{h}(\omega)+2(\alpha-k)\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(\sigma^{\sharp})(\omega)\right),&\\
&\mathscr{K}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)=f^{\alpha}\left(\mathscr{K}_{h}(\omega)+(2\alpha+1-k)\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(\sigma^{\sharp})(\omega)\right),\\
&f\operatorname{\delta}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)=f^{\alpha}\left(\operatorname{\delta}_{h}(\omega)+(n-2+2\alpha)\iota(\sigma^{\sharp})\omega\right),\end{aligned}

from which (
3.31
) follows.
∎

Define
\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(TM))\to\Gamma(S^{k+1}_{0}(TM))
by
\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}(X)=\mathscr{L}(X^{\flat})^{\sharp}
. Then (
3.31
) implies the invariance
f\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}_{\tilde{h}}(X)=\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}_{h}(X)
, so that while
\mathfrak{L}^{\sharp}
depends on
h
, the subspace
\ker\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(TM))
does not.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
X\in\Gamma(S^{k}(TM))
,
\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}(\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}X)=0
if and only if
\{h,X^{\flat}\}\in\operatorname{Im}\mathsf{X}_{+}
.

Proof.

Write
X^{\flat}=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(X^{\flat})+h\odot\alpha=\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(X)^{\flat}+h\odot\alpha
for
\alpha\in\Gamma(S^{k-2}(T^{\ast}M))
. By (
3.20
),

(3.33)

\displaystyle\{h,X^{\flat}\}

\displaystyle=\{h,\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(X)^{\flat}\}+\{h,h\odot\alpha\}=2\mathscr{L}(\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(X)^{\flat})+h\odot\left(\tfrac{2k}{n+2(k-1)}\operatorname{\delta}(\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(X)^{\flat})+\{h,\alpha\}\right),

from which the claimed equivalence is apparent.
∎

A
rank
k
conformal Killing tensor
is a symmetric tensor
X\in\Gamma(S^{k}(TM)))
satisfying the equivalent conditions of Lemma
3.8
. The definition means that the complete symmetrization of the covariant derivative of
X
is contained in the Poisson module generated by the bivector dual to the metric. By definition, a trace-free symmetric tensor is conformal Killing if and only if it is in
\ker\mathscr{L}^{\sharp}
.
Via metric duality conformal Killing tensors are identified with elements of
\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))
whose trace-free part is contained in
\ker\mathscr{L}
. Lemma
3.8
recovers the observation from
[
58
]
that a symmetric tensor is conformal Killing if and only if its trace-free part is conformal Killing. By (
3.33
), a trace-free conformal Killing tensor is a Killing tensor if and only if it is also divergence free. For explicit constructions of Killing tensors of rank higher than two see
[
21
,
56
]
.

Remark 3.9
.

For a Riemann surface, conformal Killing and trace-free Codazzi tensors are exactly the real parts of holomorphic sections of powers of the canonical line bundle and for a compact Riemann surface their existence is controlled by the genus via the Riemann-Roch theorem and linked to curvature via the Gauss-Bonnet theorem
[
44
, section
3
]
. This remark provides one justification for treating such tensors simultaneously. Another is that when the covariant derivative of a trace-free symmetric tensor is decomposed by symmetries, the generalized gradients that appear are the divergence operator and the Codazzi and conformal Killing operators.

3.3.
The hierarchy of curvature equations for trace-free symmetric tensors

Lemma
3.10
shows that for a Codazzi tensor
\omega
the curvature tensor
\omega\owedge\omega
automatically satisfies the differential Bianchi identity.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
(M,h)
be an
n
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold and suppose
k\geq 2
. For any
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}T^{\ast}M)
such that
\mathscr{C}(\omega)=0
there holds
D_{[m}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij]kl}=0
.

Proof.

A straightforward computation shows that for
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}T^{\ast}M)
,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} D_{[m}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij]kl}&=2\omega_{k[i}\,^{A}D_{m}\omega_{j]lA}-2\omega_{l[i}\,^{A}D_{m}\omega_{j]kA}=\omega_{k[i}\,^{A}\mathscr{C}(\omega)_{mj]lA}-2\omega_{l[i}\,^{A}\mathscr{C}(\omega)_{mj]kA},\end{aligned}

where
A
is shorthand for
a_{1}\dots a_{k-2}
. The claim follows.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
(M,h)
be an
n
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold and suppose
k\geq 2
. The map
\phi:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\times\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\mathscr{MC}(T^{\ast}M)
defined by

(3.35)

\displaystyle\phi(\omega,\omega)=A\omega\owedge\omega+B\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)\owedge h+C\mathit{S}(\omega\owedge\omega)h\owedge h

satisfies

(3.36)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} D_{[m}\phi(\omega,\omega)_{ij]kl}&=A\left(\omega_{k[i}\,^{A}\mathscr{C}(\omega)_{mj]lA}-2\omega_{l[i}\,^{A}\mathscr{C}(\omega)_{mj]kA}\right)\\
&\quad+B\left(h_{k[i}\mathscr{C}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))_{mj]l}-h_{l[i}\mathscr{C}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))_{mj]k}\right)+Cd_{[m}\mathit{S}(\omega\owedge\omega)(h\owedge h)_{ij]kl},\end{aligned}

for all
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. In particular
D_{[m}\phi(\omega,\omega)_{ij]kl}=0
for all
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{C}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
if
B=C=0
.

Proof.

The identity (
3.36
) follows from Lemma
3.10
via a straightforward computation. By (
3.36
) if
B=C=0
and
\mathscr{C}(\omega)=0
, then
D_{[m}\phi(\omega,\omega)_{ij]kl}=0
.
∎

Together with Lemma
3.4
and Corollary
3.11
imply that any multiple of the coupling
\phi(\omega,\omega)=\omega\owedge\omega
has nontrivial trace-free part and is automatically consistent. Although the details are omitted because the claim is not needed, with a bit more work it can be shown that for
\phi
as in (
3.35
),
D_{[m}\phi(\omega,\omega)_{ij]kl}=0
for all
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{C}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
if and only if
B=C=0
, so that up to scale
\phi(\omega,\omega)=\omega\owedge\omega
is the unique choice of coupling that is automatically consistent.

By Lemmas
2.1
and
3.10
, if
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}T^{\ast}M)
is Codazzi, then
\mathscr{G}(\omega\owedge\omega)
is divergence-free. The quadratic map
\mathscr{T}^{+}:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M)
defined by

(3.37)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega)&=\begin{cases}(\omega\otimes\omega)-\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega|^{2}h&k=1,\\
\mathscr{G}(\omega\owedge\omega)=\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)-\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega|^{2}h&k\geq 2,\end{cases}\end{aligned}

determines the stress-energy tensor associated with a trace-free Codazzi tensor. This is justified by the identity

(3.38)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(X,\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega)))&=-2h(\mathscr{K}(\omega),\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(X)(\omega))+\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-3}h(\mathsf{\sigma}_{\operatorname{\delta}}(X)(\omega),\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)),\end{aligned}

having the form (
2.30
) and proved in Theorem
3.12
(in (
3.38
),
\mathsf{\sigma}
denotes the symbol of a differential operator).

When
\omega
vanishes identically, the following hierarchy of equations for a pair
(h,\omega)
and a constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
comprising a pseudo-Riemannian metric
h
and a trace-free symmetric tensor
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
specializes to the usual hierarchy of constant sectional curvature (projectively flat), Einstein, and constant scalar curvature:

(TFC1)

\displaystyle\emph{Coupledprojectiveflatness}:

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\mathit{Riem}-c(\omega\owedge\omega)=-\tfrac{\kappa}{n(n-1)}h\owedge h,

(TFC2)

\displaystyle\emph{CoupledEinsteinequations}:

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{S}h+\tfrac{n-2}{2n}\kappa h=c\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega),

(TFC3)

\displaystyle\emph{Constraintequations}:

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle D_{i}(\mathit{S}-c|\omega|^{2})=0.

•

Because
\omega
is trace-free the pair of equations
\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0
and
\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=0
is equivalent to the single equation
\mathscr{C}(\omega)=0
, but it seems better to write the pair because it conforms with the general scheme and because it makes clearer that each of the two equations can be relaxed individually.

•

Because
\omega
is a trace-free Codazzi tensor, from the differential Bianchi identity it follows that for a solution of (
TFC2
) the function
\kappa=\mathit{S}-c|\omega|^{2}
is constant (provided
n>2
). Consequently, for a trace-free Codazzi tensor
\omega
, (
TFC2
) is equivalent to

(TFC2b)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}-c\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)=\tfrac{\kappa}{n}h,

from which it is evident that a solution of (
TFC1
) solves (
TFC2
).

•

In all three systems (
TFC1
)-(
TFC3
), the absolute value of
c
can always be absorbed into
\omega
, but its sign cannot.
Both the existence and behavior of solutions depend strongly on the sign of
c
, as well as the signature of
h
, as is illustrated partly by Theorems
5.3
and
5.5
.

•

The special case of (
TFC3
) with
k=2
and
c
positive also includes the special case of the constraint equations of general relativity for a a mean curvature zero spacelike hypersurface in a spacetime solving the Einstein field equations
[
25
]
, and this motivates calling (
TFC3
) the
coupled constraint
equations.

•

If
c
has the wrong sign, the solutions of (
3.59
) may not admit a physical interpretation, as physical considerations focus attention on solutions of the Einstein field equations for which the energy momentum tensor satisfies some energy condition, such as the
weak energy condition
that
x^{i}x^{j}\mathscr{T}_{ij}\geq 0
for all timelike vector fields
x^{i}
, where
x^{i}
is
timelike
if
h(x,x)<0
. (Such a condition is vacuous if
h
is Riemannian.) Changing the sign of
c
destroys such a condition. On the other hand, examples coming from the study of submanifolds show that the equations (
TFC1
)-(
TFC3
) have mathematical interest even without such an energy condition (for example, see Example
4.1.1
). As was already remarked, similar observations apply to the Einstein-Maxwell hierarchy and its supergravity generalizations.

•

By Lemma
3.10
the generalized gradient equations of (
TFC1
) imply the consistency of the curvature equation of (
TFC1
). This is what was meant earlier by the automatic consistency of (
TFC1
). Note that the analogous claim for (
EM1
) is not true. Such a claim depends on the Young type of the symmetries of the coupled tensor and the particular generalized gradients considered.

The equations (
TFC1
) and (
TFC2
) are special cases of more general equations (
3.60
) and (
3.59
) defined in Section
3.3
, in which the conditions stating that
\omega
be trace-free Codazzi,
\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0
and
\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)=0
, are replaced by the weaker condition, analogous to that for exterior forms in the supergravity-like equations (
2.23
), that there vanish the right-hand side of (
3.38
).

In the rest of this section, the corresponding equations coupling a metric with a trace-free Killing tensor is described as well, although only at the Einstein/Ricci level. Because conformal Killing tensors generally do not exist on negatively curved manifold, these equations are likely of most interest when the background metric has nonnegative curvature in some sense. These equations can be seen also as a natural generalization of conditions coupling a metric on an oriented surface to holomorphic sections of powers of the canonical line bundle as in
[
44
, Section
8
]
.

Define quadratic maps
\mathscr{T}^{\pm}:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M)
by

(3.39)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega)&=\begin{cases}\omega\otimes\omega-\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega|^{2}h&k=1,\\
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)-\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega|^{2}h&k\geq 2,\end{cases}&&\mathscr{T}^{-}(\omega)&=\begin{cases}(\omega\otimes\omega)+\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega|^{2}h&k=1,\\
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)+\tfrac{1}{2k}|\omega|^{2}h&k\geq 2.\end{cases}\end{aligned}

Note the asymmetry in the definitions; there is a
k
in the definition of
\mathscr{T}^{-}
absent in the definition of
\mathscr{T}^{+}
.
The
\pm
in the notation is motivated by thinking of sections of
S^{k}_{0}TM
as sections of
S^{-k}_{0}T^{\ast}M
, so corresponding to negative integers. Although this has no sense in general, it makes sense on oriented surfaces, for which conformal Killing and trace-free Codazzi tensors are exactly the real parts of holomorphic differentials
[
44
, Section
3
]
.

By definition,

(3.40)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega)=\tfrac{2-n}{2}|\omega|^{2},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}\mathscr{T}^{-}(\omega)=\tfrac{n+2k}{2k}|\omega|^{2}.

The operators
\mathscr{T}^{\pm}
depend on
h
and, when necessary, this dependence is indicated by a subscript,
\mathscr{T}^{\pm}_{h}
. For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
and
0<f\in C^{\infty}(M)
,

(3.41)

\displaystyle\omega\owedge_{fh}\omega=f^{2-k}\omega\owedge\omega,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rho}_{fh}(\omega\owedge_{fh}\omega)=f^{1-k}\operatorname{\rho}_{h}(\omega\owedge\omega),

\displaystyle|\omega|_{fh}^{2}=f^{-k}|\omega|^{2}.

It follows that the operators
\mathscr{T}^{\pm}
are conformally invariant in the sense that

(3.42)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}^{\pm}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)=f^{2\alpha+1-k}\mathscr{T}^{\pm}_{h}(\omega),

\displaystyle\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)).

For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
define one-forms by

(3.43)

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{K}_{h}(\omega),X\rangle=\langle\omega,\iota(X)\mathscr{K}_{h}(\omega)\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L}_{h}(\omega),X\rangle=\langle\omega,\iota(X)\mathscr{L}_{h}(\omega)\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{D}_{h}(\omega),X\rangle=\langle\iota(X)\omega,\operatorname{\delta}_{h}(\omega)\rangle,

for
X\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)
.
By (
3.31
), with
\sigma=\tfrac{1}{2}d\log f
,

(3.44)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f\mathsf{K}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)&=f^{2\alpha+1-k}\left(\mathsf{K}_{h}(\omega)+\tfrac{2\alpha+1-k}{2}\left(|\omega|^{2}\sigma_{i}-\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}\iota(\sigma^{\sharp})\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)\right)\right),\\
f\mathsf{L}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)&=f^{2\alpha+1-k}\left(\mathsf{L}_{h}(\omega)+\tfrac{2(\alpha-k)k}{k+1}\left(|\omega|^{2}\sigma_{i}+\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+2(k-1)}\iota(\sigma^{\sharp})\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)\right)\right),\\
f\mathsf{D}_{fh}(f^{\alpha}\omega)&=f^{2\alpha+1-k}\left(\mathsf{D}_{h}(\omega)+(n-2+2\alpha)\iota(\sigma^{\sharp})\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)\right).\end{aligned}

Theorem
3.12
describes the identities of the form (
2.30
) for trace-free symmetric tensors.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
M
be a manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
and let
h
be a pseudo-Riemannian metric. For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
and
X\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)
there hold

(3.45)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(X,\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega)))&=-2h(X,\mathsf{K}(\omega))+\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-3}h(X,\mathsf{D}(\omega)),\\
&=-2h(\iota(X)\mathscr{K}(\omega),\omega)+\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-3}h(\iota(X)\omega,\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)),\\
&=-2h(\mathscr{K}(\omega),\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(X)(\omega))+\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-3}h(\mathsf{\sigma}_{\operatorname{\delta}}(X)(\omega),\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)),\end{aligned}

(3.46)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} h(X,\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{-}(\omega)))&=\tfrac{k+1}{k}h(X,\mathsf{L}(\omega))+\tfrac{n+2k}{n+2(k-1)}h(X,\mathsf{D}(\omega))\\
&=\tfrac{k+1}{k}h(\iota(X)\mathscr{L}(\omega),\omega)+\tfrac{n+2k}{n+2(k-1)}h(\iota(X)\omega,\operatorname{\delta}(\omega))\\
&=\tfrac{k+1}{k}h(\mathscr{L}(\omega),\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(X)(\omega))+\tfrac{n+2k}{n+2(k-1)}h(\mathsf{\sigma}_{\operatorname{\delta}}(X)(\omega),\operatorname{\delta}(\omega))).\end{aligned}

Proof.

Suppose
k\geq 2
and let
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.
Using (
3.29
) yields

(3.47)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{2}D_{i}|\omega|^{2}&=\omega^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}D_{i}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\tfrac{2k}{k+1}\mathsf{K}(\omega)_{i}+\mathsf{L}(\omega)_{i}+\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i},\end{aligned}

which shows

(3.48)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\delta}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))_{i}&=-\tfrac{2}{k+1}\mathsf{K}(\omega)_{i}+\mathsf{L}(\omega)_{i}+\left(1+\tfrac{n-2}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\right)\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i}\\
&=-\tfrac{2}{k+1}\mathsf{K}(\omega)_{i}+\mathsf{L}(\omega)_{i}+\tfrac{(n+k-2)(n+2(k-2))+2(n-2)}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i},\end{aligned}

Contracting
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
with
D_{i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k}i}
and using (
3.29
) yields

(3.49)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \omega^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}D_{i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k}i}&=-\tfrac{2}{k+1}\mathsf{K}(\omega)_{i}+\mathsf{L}(\omega)_{i}+\tfrac{2(1-k)}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i}.\end{aligned}

Differentiating
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij}=\omega_{ia_{1}\dots a_{k-1}}\omega_{j}\,^{a_{1}\dots a_{k-1}}
yields

(3.50)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} w^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}D_{i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k}i}&=\operatorname{\delta}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))_{i}-\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i}.\end{aligned}

Substituting (
3.50
) in (
3.49
) yields

(3.51)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\delta}(|\omega|^{2}h)_{i}&=\tfrac{1}{2}D_{i}|\omega|^{2}=\tfrac{2k}{k+1}\mathsf{K}(\omega)_{i}+\mathsf{L}(\omega)_{i}+\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i}.\end{aligned}

Taking linear combinations of (
3.48
) and (
3.51
) in different ways and substituting the result in (
3.39
) yields

(3.52)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega))_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\delta}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))_{i}-\tfrac{1}{2}D_{i}|\omega|^{2}=-2\mathsf{K}(\omega)_{i}+\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-3}\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i},

(3.53)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{-}(\omega))_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\delta}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))_{i}+\tfrac{1}{2k}D_{i}|\omega|^{2}=\tfrac{k+1}{k}\mathsf{L}(\omega)_{i}+\tfrac{n+2k}{n+2(k-1)}\mathsf{D}(\omega)_{i},

where, when
k=1
, (
3.52
)-(
3.53
) have sense if
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij}
is interpreted as
\omega_{i}\omega_{j}
.
The
k=1
cases of (
3.52
) and (
3.53
) follow from

(3.54)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\delta}(\gamma\otimes\gamma-\tfrac{1}{2}|\gamma|^{2}h)_{i}&=\operatorname{\delta}(\gamma)\gamma_{i}-2\gamma^{p}D_{[i}\gamma_{p]}=\operatorname{\delta}(\gamma)\gamma_{i}-2\gamma^{p}\mathscr{K}(\gamma)_{ip},\\
\operatorname{\delta}(\gamma\otimes\gamma+\tfrac{1}{2}|\gamma|^{2}h)_{i}&=\operatorname{\delta}(\gamma)\gamma_{i}+2\gamma^{p}D_{(i}\gamma_{p)}=\tfrac{n+2}{2}\operatorname{\delta}(\gamma)\gamma_{i}+2\gamma^{p}\mathscr{L}(\gamma)_{ip},\end{aligned}

valid for any
\gamma\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)
.
Pairing
X
with (
3.52
) and (
3.53
) yields (
3.45
) and (
3.46
)
∎

Remark 3.13
.

Among the possible linear combinations of (
3.48
) and (
3.51
), the combinations on the right-hand sides of (
3.52
) and (
3.53
) are distinguished by the absence of
\mathsf{L}(\omega)
and
\mathsf{K}(\omega)
. This is relevant because, by (
3.44
), the conformal scaling of
\mathsf{K}(\omega)
,
\mathsf{L}(\omega)
, and
\mathsf{D}(\omega)
is different. The first two rescale in a way dependent on
k
, while the last rescales in a way dependent only on dimension. This means that given a conformal structure
[h]
it makes sense to impose that either
\mathsf{K}(\omega)
or
\mathsf{L}(\omega)
vanish, but does not make sense to require that both vanish. The vanishing of
\mathsf{D}(\omega)
can be treated as a condition selecting within the conformal class.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
(M,h)
be a pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
of dimension
n\geq 3
. Consider sequences of tensors
\{\omega(k)\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)):k\geq 1\}
and
\{\gamma(k)\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)):k\geq 1\}
such that

•

\omega^{(k)}\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathsf{D}\cap\ker\mathsf{K}
,

•

\gamma^{(k)}\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathsf{D}\cap\ker\mathsf{L}
, and

•

the tensors
\mathscr{T}^{\text{Cod}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega^{(k)})
and
\mathscr{T}^{\text{Kill}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}b_{k}\mathscr{T}^{-}(\gamma^{(k)})
converge pointwise to smooth sections of
S^{2}(T^{\ast}M)
for some sequences
\{a_{k}\in\mathbb{R}:k\geq 1\}
and
\{b_{k}\in\mathbb{R}:k\geq 1\}
.

Then the equations

(3.55)

\displaystyle\mathit{Ric}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{S}h+\tfrac{n-2}{2n}\kappa h=\mathscr{T}^{\text{Cod}}+\mathscr{T}^{\text{Kill}}

are consistent and

(3.56)

\displaystyle\kappa=\mathit{S}-\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}|\omega^{(k)}|^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\tfrac{n+2k}{k(n-2)}b_{k}|\gamma^{(k)}|^{2}

is a constant.
If
n>2
the equations (
3.55
) hold for some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if

(3.57)

\displaystyle\mathit{Ric}-a_{1}\omega^{(1)}\otimes\omega^{(1)}-\sum_{k\geq 2}a_{k}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega^{(k)}\owedge\omega^{(k)})-b_{1}\gamma^{(1)}\otimes\gamma^{(1)}-\sum_{k\geq 2}b_{k}\operatorname{\rho}(\gamma^{(k)}\owedge\gamma^{(k)})=\tfrac{\kappa}{n}h,

in which case
\kappa
has the form (
3.56
).

Proof.

By Theorem
3.12
,
\mathscr{T}^{\text{Cod}}
and
\mathscr{T}^{\text{Kill}}
are divergence free. Taking the divergence of both sides of (
3.55
) shows that
\kappa
must be constant if (
3.55
) is to admit solutions. The form (
3.56
) for
\kappa
follows by tracing both sides of (
3.55
). Evidently (
3.55
) implies (
3.57
). If there holds (
3.57
), taking the divergence of (
3.57
) and using the traced differential Bianchi identity
2\operatorname{\delta}(\mathit{Ric})=d\mathit{S}
and Theorem
3.12
shows

(3.58)

\displaystyle 0=\tfrac{n-2}{n}\left(\mathit{S}-\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}|\omega^{(k)}|^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\tfrac{n+2k}{k(n-2)}b_{k}|\gamma^{(k)}|^{2}\right),

so that, since
n>2
,
\kappa
as in (
3.56
) is constant. With (
3.57
) this implies (
3.55
).
∎

Remark 3.15
.

The hypotheses of Lemma
3.14
are satisfied provided
\omega^{(k)}
is a rank
k
trace-free Codazzi tensor and
\gamma^{(k)}
is a rank
k
Killing tensor.

Remark 3.16
.

The equations (
3.55
) make sense for
h
of any signature, although for indefinite signature
h
the notation
|\omega^{(k)}|^{2}
is misleading as this expression need not be positive.

Corollary 3.17
.

On an
n
-manifold
M
a pair
(h,\omega)
comprising a pseudo-Riemannian metric
h
and a tensor
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
solves the system

(3.59)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega))=-2\mathsf{K}(\omega)+\tfrac{(n-2)}{(n+k-3)}\mathsf{D}(\omega),\\
\mathit{Ric}&-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{S}h+\tfrac{n-2}{2n}\kappa h=c\left(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)-\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega|^{2}h\right)=c\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega),\end{aligned}

for some real constants
\kappa
and
c
if and only if
h
solves the Einstein field equations with energy momentum tensor
c\mathscr{T}^{+}(\omega)
and cosmological constant
\Lambda=\tfrac{n-2}{2n}\kappa=\tfrac{n-2}{2n}\left(\mathit{S}-c|\omega|^{2}\right)
.

The system (
3.59
) has been written so as to make readily apparent its formal resemblance to the Einstein-Maxwell system for a metric and a two-form.

Example 3.18
.

In the case
k=1
, for a solution
(h,\omega)
of (
3.59
), the one-form
\omega
is
h
-harmonic.
If, moreover,
\omega=d\phi
for some
\phi\in C^{\infty}(M)
, then
\phi
solves the wave equation
\Delta_{h}\phi=0
, and in Lorentzian signature can be interpreted as a massless scalar field, so the pair
(h,d\phi)
satisfies the Einstein scalar field equations. In the Riemannian case, were
M
compact, then
\phi
would be harmonic, so constant, and
\omega
would vanish identically, but if
\omega
is not required to be exact, there can still be interesting solutions in Riemannian signature.

Thinking of
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
as a
S^{k-1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
-valued one-form,
(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ijkl}
can be viewed as a curvature term.
Lemma
3.19
shows that a metric and
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}
such that the modified curvature
\mathit{Riem}-\tfrac{1}{4}(\omega\owedge\omega)
is projectively flat, meaning it is a multiple of
h\owedge h
, yield a solution of (
3.59
).

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
h
be a pseudo-Riemannian metric on a manifold
M
of dimension
n\geq 3
. Suppose
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
satisfies
\mathsf{K}(\omega)=0
and that there is
\kappa\in C^{\infty}(M)
such that the curvature of
h
satisfies

(3.60)

\displaystyle\mathit{Riem}-c(\omega\owedge\omega)=-\tfrac{\kappa}{n(n-1)}h\owedge h,

for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
h
and
\omega
solve the equations (
3.59
). In particular,
\kappa
is a constant.

If
n=3
, then
(h,\omega)
solves (
3.60
) for
c\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if it solves (
3.59
).

Proof.

By (
2.27
), tracing (
3.60
) yields
\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}=\tfrac{\kappa}{n}h+c\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)
. Tracing this yields
\mathit{S}=\kappa+c|\omega|^{2}
, and calculating the trace-free part of
\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}
yields the last equation of (
3.59
). From Lemma
3.14
it follows that
\kappa
is a constant.
If
(h,\omega)
solves (
3.59
), then, by (
3.11
) and (
3.59
),

(3.61)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathit{Riem}-c(\omega\owedge\omega)=\mathscr{W}-c\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)-\tfrac{2}{n-2}\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\left(\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}-c\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)\right)\owedge h-\tfrac{1}{n(n-1)}\left(\mathit{S}-c|\omega|^{2}\right)h\owedge h\\
&=\mathscr{W}-c\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)-\tfrac{\kappa}{n(n-1)}h\owedge h,\end{aligned}

where the conformal Weyl tensor
\mathscr{W}
of
h
is the trace-free part of
\mathit{Riem}
. If
n=3
, then there vanish
\mathscr{W}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)
, and (
3.61
) shows that
h
and
\omega
solve (
