3.60
).
∎

4.
Examples of solutions to the coupled projectively flat and coupled Einstein equations

This section records examples of solutions of the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) and coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
). There is no pretense at completeness. Rather, the goals are to illustrate the abundance of solutions and the diverse nature of possible constructions. The different subsections are essentially independent, and also this entire section is independent of Section
5
.

4.1.
Examples related with submanifold geometry

This section records examples of solutions of the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) coming from minimal immersions. These example arise from consideration of classes of hypersurfaces in special ambient spaces and characterized by conditions on their second fundamental forms.

The
second fundamental form
of an immersion
i:M\to N
of a manifold
M
in a manifold
N
equipped with a projective structure
[\widehat{\nabla}]
is a normal bundle valued symmetric two-tensor on
M
,
\mathbb{\Pi}\in\Gamma(S^{2}(T^{\ast}M)\otimes i^{\ast}(TN)/TM)
. By definition
\mathbb{\Pi}(X,Y)
is the projection of
\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Y
onto the normal bundle for
X,Y\in\Gamma(T^{\ast}M)
and any torsion-free representative
\widehat{\nabla}\in[\widehat{\nabla}]
. Each nonvanishing section
W
over
U\subset M
of the normal bundle of a hypersurface immersion determines an identification of the second fundamental form with a symmetric tensor
\Pi
on
U
defined by
\mathbb{\Pi}(X,Y)=\Pi(X,Y)W
. Different such sections determine conformally related tensors. The tensor
\Pi
is said to be the second fundamental form with respect to the transversal
W
and
\widehat{\nabla}
.

4.1.1.
Examples from mean curvature zero immersions in space forms

Theorem
4.1
yields solutions of the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) with
c
having either sign.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(N,g)
be an
(n+1)
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian space form with scalar curvature
R_{g}
and let
i:M\to N
be an immersion of an
n
-dimensional hypersurface
M
such that
h=i^{\ast}(g)
is nondegenerate. Let
\Pi
be the second fundamental form of the immersion
i
defined with respect to a unimodular transverse vector field
Z
orthogonal to
i(M)
and satisfying
\epsilon=|Z|^{2}_{g}\in\{\pm 1\}
.
If
g
has constant curvature equal to
-\tfrac{R_{g}}{n(n+1)}g\owedge g
, and
i(M)
has mean curvature zero, then
(h,\Pi)
solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) with
c=-\epsilon
and
\kappa=\mathit{S}+\epsilon|\Pi|^{2}=\tfrac{n-1}{n+1}R_{g}
, where
\mathit{S}
is the scalar curvature of
h
.

Proof.

By definition, for
X,Y\in\Gamma(TM)
,
\Pi(X,Y)=\epsilon h(SX,Y)
, where
S
is the shape operator with respect to the normal
Z
. The Gauss-Codazzi equations and the assumption that
g
have constant sectional curvature imply that
\mathscr{C}(\Pi)=0
and that the curvature tensor of
g
satisfies
\mathit{Riem}+\epsilon(\Pi\owedge\Pi)=-\tfrac{R_{g}}{n(n+1)}(h\owedge h)
. If the immersion has mean curvature zero, then
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}(\Pi)=0
, so
\operatorname{\delta}(\Pi)=0
and
\Pi\in\Gamma(S^{2}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}
.
∎

In the case where
M
is a spacelike mean curvature zero hypersurface in a
4
-dimensional Lorentzian spacetime solving the Einstein field equations, the equations
\operatorname{\delta}(\Pi)=0
and
\kappa=R_{h}-|\Pi|^{2}=0
are the
constraint equations
of general relativity
[
5
,
25
,
32
,
33
,
63
]
. Their solvability is a necessary condition for the existence of solutions to (
TFC2
).

4.1.2.
Examples from mean curvature zero Legendrian immersions

Let
(N,\Omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional symplectic manifold and let
i:M\to N
be a Lagrangian immersion. Tensors on
M
and
N
are labeled with lowercase and uppercase Latin indices, respectively.
A
(para/pseudo)-Kähler structure
is a triple
(G,A,\Omega)
comprising a (pseudo-)Riemannian metric
G
, an integrable (para)complex structure,
A
, and a symplectic form
\Omega
, which are
compatible
in the sense that, for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(TN)
,
A(AX)=\epsilon X
,
\Omega(X,Y)=G(AX,Y)
,
\Omega(AX,Y)=\epsilon G(X,Y)
, where, when
\epsilon=1
, the qualifier
para
applies, and, when
\epsilon=-1
, the qualifier
pseudo
is used if
G
has indefinite signature.

By definition, a (para/pseudo)-Kähler manifold
(N,G,A)
has
constant (para)-holomorphic sectional curvature
4\hat{c}
if its metric curvature tensor
\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}
has the form

(4.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}=-\hat{c}(G\owedge G+3\epsilon\Omega\cdot\Omega),

where
\owedge
and
\cdot
are the bilinear maps defined in (
2.26
) and (
2.16
).
In this case, its Ricci and scalar curvatures equal
2(n+1)\hat{c}G
and
4n(n+1)\hat{c}
, respectively.

Let
(N,G,A,\Omega)
be a
2n
-dimensional (para/pseudo)-Kähler manifold with canonical (Levi-Civita) connection
\widehat{\nabla}
. An immersion
i:M\to N
is
nondegenerate
if the induced tensor
h=i^{\ast}(G)
is nondegenerate. Let
i:M\to N
be a nondegenerate Lagrangian immersion. The second fundamental form equals the projection of
\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y)
onto the normal bundle of
M
and determines the tensor
\Pi\in\Gamma(S^{3}T^{\ast}M)
defined by

(4.2)

\displaystyle\Pi(X,Y,Z)=\Omega(\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y),Ti(Z))=G(A\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y),Ti(Z))=-G(\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y),ATi(Z)).

The tensor
\Pi
is symmetric because
\widehat{\nabla}
is torsion-free and the immersion is Lagrangian. Define
\Pi^{\sharp}\in\Gamma(S^{2}T^{\ast}M\otimes TM)
by
h(\Pi^{\sharp}(X,Y),Z)=\Pi(X,Y,Z)
. Although
\Pi^{\sharp}
is not the second fundamental form as such, as it is twisted by the (para)-complex structure, it carries the same information as the second fundamental form. In particular, by the complete symmetry of
\Pi
, the Lagrangian immersion has mean curvature zero (by definition meaning
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}(\Pi)=0
) if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(\Pi^{\sharp})=0
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
i:M\to N
be a nondegenerate mean curvature zero Lagrangian immersion of the
n
-manifold
M
in the
2n
-dimensional (para/pseudo-)Kähler manifold
(N,G,\Omega,A)
with constant (para)-holomorphic curvature
4\hat{c}
. Let
h=i^{\ast}(G)
be the induced metric and let
\Pi\in\Gamma(S^{3}T^{\ast}M)
be the tensor defined from the second fundamental form of
i
by (
4.2
). Then
(h,\Pi)
solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) with
c=\epsilon
and
\kappa=\hat{c}n(n-1)
.

Proof.

Because
i
is nondegenerate,
Ti(TM)
and
ATi(TM)
are transverse, so the projection onto
Ti(TM)
along
ATi(TM)
,
P\in\Gamma(\operatorname{End}(i^{\ast}(TN)))
, is well-defined. The torsion-free connection
D
on
M
defined by
Ti(D_{X}Y)=P\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y)
is the Levi-Civita connection of
h
.
By the definition of
\Pi^{\sharp}
, for all
X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(TM)
there holds

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Omega(\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y),Ti(Z))&=h(\Pi^{\sharp}(X,Y),Z)=G(Ti(\Pi^{\sharp}(X,Y)),Ti(Z))=\epsilon\Omega(ATi(\Pi^{\sharp}(X,Y)),Ti(Z)),\end{aligned}

and by the nondegeneracy of
\Omega
this shows the first identity of

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\widehat{\nabla}_{X}Ti(Y)=Ti(D_{X}Y)+\epsilon ATi(\Pi^{\sharp}(X,Y)),&&\widehat{\nabla}_{X}ATi(Y)=ATi(D_{X}Y)+Ti(\Pi^{\sharp}(X,Y)).\end{aligned}

The second identity of (
4.4
) follows from the first because
A
is
\widehat{\nabla}
-parallel.

Let
\hat{R}_{IJK}\,^{L}
be the curvature of
\widehat{\nabla}
so that
\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}_{IJKL}=\hat{R}_{IJK}\,^{P}G_{PL}
is the curvature tensor of
G
and let
\mathit{Riem}
be the metric curvature tensor of
h
.
From (
4.4
) it follows that there are tensors
\mathscr{T}
and
\mathscr{N}
on
M
of metric curvature tensor type and such that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\hat{R}(X,Y)Ti(Z)=Ti(\mathscr{T}^{\sharp}(X,Y)Z)+\epsilon ATi(\mathscr{N}^{\sharp}(X,Y)Z)

for all
X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(TM)
, where
h(\mathscr{T}^{\sharp}(X,Y)Y,W)=\mathscr{T}(X,Y,Z,W)
and similarly for
\mathscr{N}^{\sharp}
. Straightforward computations using (
4.4
) show that

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}=\mathit{Riem}-2\epsilon\Pi\owedge\Pi,

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}=2\mathscr{C}(\Pi).

Because there is supposed
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}(\Pi)=0
, tracing (
4.6
) yields

(4.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{T})=\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}-\epsilon\operatorname{\rho}(L\owedge L),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{T})=\mathit{S}-\epsilon|L|^{2},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{N})=\operatorname{\delta}(\Pi),

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{N})=0.

Together (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) implies
\mathscr{N}=\mathscr{K}(\Pi)
.
Define
(\iota(A)\widehat{\mathit{Riem}})_{IJKL}=A_{I}\,^{P}\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}_{PJKL}
.
If
G
has constant (para)-holomorphic sectional curvature
\hat{c}
, then
\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}
has the form (
4.1
) and it follows that

(4.8)

\displaystyle(\iota(A)\widehat{\mathit{Riem}})_{IJKL}=2\hat{c}(G_{K[I}\Omega_{J]L}+\Omega_{K[I}G_{J]L}+G_{IJ}\Omega_{KL})

It follows from (
4.5
) together with (
4.1
) and (
4.8
) that pulling back
\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}
and
\iota(A)\widehat{\mathit{Riem}}
to
M
via
i
yields

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathscr{T}=i^{\ast}(\widehat{\mathit{Riem}})=-\hat{c}h\owedge h,&&\mathscr{N}=i^{\ast}(\iota(A)\widehat{\mathit{Riem}})=0,\\
&\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{T})=\hat{c}(n-1)h,&&\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{T})=\hat{c}n(n-1).\end{aligned}

Together (
4.6
), (
4.7
), and (
4.9
) show that
\Pi\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
and that
(h,\Pi)
satisfies
\mathit{Riem}=-\hat{c}h\owedge h+2\epsilon\Pi\owedge\Pi
and so also
\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}=\hat{c}(n-1)h+\epsilon\operatorname{\rho}(\Pi\owedge\Pi)
and
\mathit{S}=\hat{c}n(n-1)+\epsilon|\Pi|^{2}
.
∎

4.2.
Einstein statistical structures, flat projective structures, and cubic Codazzi tensors

The notions described next are special cases of more general ones introduced in
[
43
,
44
,
45
]
and described more completely in
[
51
, Sections
8-10
]
.

A
statistical structure

(\nabla,h)
is a pair comprising a pseudo-Riemannian metric
h
and a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
satisfying
\nabla_{[i}h_{j]k}=0
. This means that the
cubic form

\mathit{L}_{ijk}=\nabla_{i}h_{jk}
is symmetric. The torsion-free
conjugate
connection
\bar{\nabla}
defined by
\bar{\nabla}=\nabla+h^{kp}\nabla_{i}h_{jp}
satisfies
\bar{\nabla}_{i}h_{jk}=-\nabla_{i}h_{jk}=-\mathit{L}_{ijk}
, so also
\bar{\nabla}_{[i}h_{j]k}=0
. Hence
(\bar{\nabla},h)
is a statistical structure with cubic form
\bar{\mathit{L}}_{ijk}=-\mathit{L}_{ijk}
. The conjugate connection of
\bar{\nabla}
is
\nabla
, so conjugacy is an involution on the space of statistical structures. A statistical structure is self-conjugate if and only if
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
h
.

Remark 4.3
.

For background on statistical structures see
[
2
,
3
]

Statistical structures are also called
Codazzi structures

[
95
]
and are essentially the same as what I have called
exact AH structures

[
44
]
. The
statistical
terminology originates in the work of Amari
[
2
]
applying differential geometric methods to study parametric statistics. While from a purely geometric point of view the terminology may seem poorly motivated, it has become a popular way of referring to these objects.

A statistical structure is
special
if
\nabla_{i}\det h=0
. Because the conjugate connection
\bar{\nabla}
satisfies
\bar{\nabla}_{i}\det h=-\nabla_{i}\det h
, the conjugate of a special statistical structure is special. Define
\ell_{i}=\mathit{L}_{ip}\,^{p}
, so that
(\nabla,h)
is special if and only if
\ell_{i}=0
.

By definition, the curvature of a statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
is the curvature
R_{ijk}\,^{l}
of
\nabla
. Its Ricci curvature is
R_{ij}=\operatorname{\rho}(R)_{ij}=R_{pij}\,^{p}
and its scalar curvature is
r=h^{ij}R_{ij}
.
Given a statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
, the difference tensor of
\nabla
and the Levi-Civita connection
D
of
h
is
\nabla-D=-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{L}_{ij}\,^{k}
. Similarly
\bar{\nabla}=D+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{L}_{ij}\,^{k}
. More generally, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the connection
\nabla^{t}=D-t\mathit{L}_{ij}\,^{k}
constitutes with
h
a statistical structure having cubic form
2t\mathit{L}_{ijk}
and for which the conjugate statistical structure is
(\nabla^{-t},h)
.

Let
\mathscr{R}_{ijk}\,^{l}
be the curvature of
D
. The curvature
R^{t}_{ijkl}=R^{t}_{ijk}\,{}^{p}h_{pl}
of
(\nabla^{t},h)
satisfies

(4.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{t}_{ijkl}&=R^{t}_{ijk}\,{}^{a}h_{al}=h_{al}\left(\mathscr{R}_{ijk}\,^{a}-2tD_{[i}\mathit{L}_{j]k}\,^{a}+2t^{2}\mathit{L}_{p[i}\,^{a}\mathit{L}_{j]k}\,^{p}\right)\\
&=\mathscr{R}_{ijkl}-2t\mathscr{C}(\mathit{L})_{ijkl}-t^{2}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})_{ijkl},\end{aligned}

(4.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\rho}(R^{t})_{ij}&=\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{R})_{ij}-t(\operatorname{\delta}(\mathit{L})_{ij}-D_{i}\ell_{j})-t^{2}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})_{ij},\end{aligned}

(4.12)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} r^{t}&=\mathit{S}-t^{2}|\mathit{L}|^{2},\end{aligned}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{t}_{(ij)}-\tfrac{1}{2}r^{t}h_{ij}&=G_{ij}-t(\operatorname{\delta}(\mathit{L})_{ij}-\mathscr{L}(\ell)_{ij})-t^{2}(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathit{L}|^{2}h_{ij}),\end{aligned}

(4.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{t}_{(ij)}-\tfrac{1}{n}r^{t}h_{ij}&=\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{R})_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{n}\mathit{S}h_{ij}-t^{2}(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{n}|\mathit{L}|^{2}h_{ij}),\end{aligned}

where
n=\dim M
. From (
4.10
) there follow

(4.15)

\displaystyle R^{t}_{ijlk}=-R^{-t}_{ijkl},

\displaystyle R^{t}_{ij(kl)}=-2t\mathscr{C}(\mathit{L})_{ijkl},

(4.16)

\displaystyle R^{t}_{ip}\,{}^{p}\,_{j}=\operatorname{\rho}(R^{-t})_{ij},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rho}(R^{-t})_{(ij)}-\operatorname{\rho}(R^{t})_{(ij)}=2t\operatorname{\delta}(\mathit{L})_{ij}.

A statistical structure has
self-conjugate
curvature if its curvature tensor equals the curvature tensor of the conjugate statistical structure. More generally, a tensor associated with a statistical structure is
self-conjugate
if it equals the corresponding tensor associated with the conjugate statistical structure. For example, it follows from (
4.12
) that the scalar trace of the curvature of a statistical structure is self-conjugate, and by (
4.16
) a statistical structure has self-conjugate Ricci tensor if and only if its cubic form is divergence free.

Lemma 4.4
.

The following are equivalent for a statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
:

(1)

It has self-conjugate curvature.

(2)

Its cubic form is a Codazzi tensor.

(3)

Its curvature tensor satisfies
R_{ij(kl)}=0
, so has the symmetries of a metric curvature tensor.

A special statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
has self-conjugate Ricci curvature if and only if its cubic form is divergence free.

Proof.

The equivalence of the three statements follows from (
4.10
) and (
4.15
) with
t=\pm 1/2
.
It follows from (
4.11
) that a special statistical structure has self-conjugate Ricci curvature if and only if its cubic form is divergence free.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For a statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
on a manifold of dimension
n
there holds

(4.17)

\displaystyle\tfrac{n-2}{n}dr_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{p}d\ell_{pi}-h_{ia}h^{pq}(\nabla_{p}S_{q}\,^{a}+\bar{\nabla}_{p}\bar{S}_{q}\,^{a})=\mathit{L}^{abc}R_{i(abc)}=\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{+}(\mathit{L}))_{i}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{L}_{i}\,^{pq}\operatorname{\delta}(\mathit{L})_{pq},

in which
S_{ij}=R_{(ij)}-\tfrac{r}{n}h_{ij}
is the trace-free part of the symmetrized Ricci tensor of
(\nabla,h)
, and
\bar{S}_{ij}
is the trace-free part of the symmetrized Ricci tensor of the conjugate statistical structure.

Proof.

By definition
R_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{n}rh_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{4}d\ell_{ij}+S_{ij}
. Differentiating this and
antisymmetrizing the result yields

(4.18)

\displaystyle 2\nabla_{[i}R_{j]k}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{n}dr_{[i}h_{j]k}-\tfrac{1}{4}\nabla_{k}d\ell_{ij}+2\nabla_{[i}S_{j]k}.

Contracting (
4.18
) with
h^{jk}
yields

(4.19)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2h^{jk}\nabla_{[i}R_{j]k}&=\tfrac{n-1}{n}dr_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}h^{pq}\nabla_{p}d\ell_{qi}+h^{pq}\nabla_{i}S_{pq}-h^{pq}\nabla_{p}S_{qi}\\
&=\tfrac{n-1}{n}dr_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}h^{pq}\nabla_{p}d\ell_{qi}-S_{pq}\nabla_{i}h^{pq}-h^{pq}(h_{ai}\nabla_{p}S_{q}\,^{a}+S_{q}\,^{a}\nabla_{p}h_{ai})\\
&=\tfrac{n-1}{n}dr_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}h^{pq}\nabla_{p}d\ell_{qi}-h^{pq}h_{ai}\nabla_{p}S_{q}\,^{a}.\end{aligned}

in which the last inequality follows from
\nabla_{i}h^{jk}=-\mathit{L}_{i}\,^{jk}
and that
S_{ij}
is trace-free.

By (
4.16
),
R_{ip}\,^{p}\,{}_{j}=\bar{R}_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{n}rh_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{4}d\ell_{ij}+\bar{S}_{ij}
.
Using this together with the differential Bianchi identity yields

(4.20)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2h^{jk}\nabla_{[i}R_{j]k}&=h^{jk}\nabla_{p}R_{ijk}\,^{p}=\nabla_{p}R_{ia}\,^{ap}-R_{ijk}\,^{p}\nabla_{p}h^{jk}\\
&=\tfrac{1}{n}dr_{i}-\tfrac{1}{4}\nabla_{p}d\ell_{i}\,^{p}+\nabla_{p}\bar{S}_{i}\,^{p}+\mathit{L}^{abc}R_{ibca}\\
&=\tfrac{1}{n}dr_{i}+\tfrac{1}{4}h^{pq}\nabla_{p}d\ell_{qi}-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{q}d\ell_{qi}+h_{ia}h^{pq}\bar{\nabla}_{p}\bar{S}_{q}\,^{a}+\mathit{L}^{abc}R_{iabc}.\end{aligned}

in which the last equality follows from
\nabla_{p}\bar{S}_{i}\,^{p}=h^{pq}h_{ia}\bar{\nabla}_{p}\bar{S}_{q}\,^{a}
and the complete symmetry of
L^{abc}
. Combining (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) yields the first equality of (
4.17
).

Differentiating
\mathscr{T}^{+}(L)
yields
\operatorname{\delta}(\mathscr{T}^{+}(L))_{i}=\mathit{L}_{i}\,^{pq}-2\mathit{L}^{abc}\mathscr{C}(\mathit{L})_{iabc}
. Together with (
4.15
) this yields the second equality of (
4.17
).
∎

The following definition is a special case of one made in
[
51
]
, specializing a more generl definition from
[
43
,
44
]
.
A special statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
is
Einstein
if it satisfies the following two conditions:

•

(
Naive Einstein
)
\operatorname{\rho}(R)
and
\operatorname{\rho}(\bar{R})
are multiples of
h
.

•

(
Conservation
) The scalar curvature
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(R)=\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}_{h}\operatorname{\rho}(\bar{R})
is constant.

Lemma 4.6
.

On manifold of dimension
n>2
, a special statistical structure with self-conjugate curvature that is naive Einstein is Einstein.

Proof.

Two different proofs are available. By Lemma
4.4
, that a special statistical structure have self-conjugate curvature means its cubic form is trace-free Codazzi. By (
4.13
) in this case
R_{(ij)}-\tfrac{1}{2}rh_{ij}=G_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{4}(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{2}|\mathit{L}|^{2}h_{ij})
and by Theorem
3.12
this is divergence free. If the special statistical structure is naive Einstein, this implies
(n-2)dr_{i}=0
. Alternatively, for a special statistical structure with self-conjugate curvature, (
4.17
) implies
(n-2)dr_{i}=nh_{ia}h^{pq}(\nabla_{p}S_{q}\,^{a}+\bar{\nabla}_{p}\bar{S}_{q}\,^{a})
, and the naive Einstein condition implies the vanishing of the last expression. In either case,
(n-2)dr_{i}=0
, so
r
is constant, because
n>2
.
∎

A statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
is
projectively flat
if
\nabla
is projectively flat. A statistical structure is
conjugate projectively flat
if the conjugate statistical structure is projectively flat.

Lemma 4.7
.

For a special statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
on an
n
-manifold the following are equivalent:

(1)

The curvature of
(\nabla,h)
satisfies
R_{ijkl}=\alpha(h\owedge h)_{ijkl}
for a constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

(\nabla,h)
is projectively flat and has self-conjugate curvature.

(3)

(\nabla,h)
is projectively flat and Einstein.

If there hold (
1
)-(
3
), then
-n(n-1)\alpha=r
. If
n>2
, the conditions (
1
)-(
3
) are equivalent to (
4
).

(4)

(\nabla,h)
is projectively flat and conjugate projectively flat.

Proof.

A special statisical structure has symmetric Ricci tensor and, because the conjugate statistical structure is also special, symmetric conjugate Ricci tensor. Consequently, its projective Weyl tensor is
B_{ijk}\,^{l}=R_{ijk}\,^{l}+2\delta_{[i}\,^{l}P_{j]k}-2\delta_{k}\,^{l}P_{[ij]}
where
(1-n)P_{ij}=R_{ij}-\tfrac{2}{n+1}R_{[ij]}=R_{ij}
.

If there holds (
1
), then, by (
4.16
),
\bar{R}_{ijkl}=-R_{ijlk}=-\alpha(h\owedge h)_{ijlk}=\alpha(h\owedge h)_{ijkl}=R_{ijkl}
, so
(\nabla,h)
has self-conjugate curvature. Tracing yields
R_{ij}=(1-n)\alpha h_{ij}
and
r=-n(n-1)\alpha
, so that
(\nabla,h)
is Einstein. Consequently
-n(n-1)P_{ij}=rh_{ij}
and
B_{ijkl}=R_{ijkl}+2h_{l[i}P_{j]k}=R_{ijkl}-\tfrac{2r}{n(n-1)}h_{l[i}h_{j]k}=0
. If
n>2
this shows
\nabla
is projectively flat. If
n=2
, then
2\nabla_{[i}P_{j]k}=-dr_{[i}h_{j]k}=0
because
\alpha
is constant by assumption. This shows the vanishing of the projective Cotton tensor and hence that
\nabla
is projectively flat. The preceding shows (
1
) implies (
2
).

Let
(\nabla,h)
be a projectively flat special statistical structure. That
\nabla
be projectively flat means that
R_{ijk}\,^{l}=-2\delta_{[i}\,^{l}P_{j]k}
where
(1-n)P_{ij}=R_{ij}
. Tracing this in
jk
yields
(1-n)\bar{R}_{ij}=(1-n)R_{ip}\,^{p}\,{}_{j}=R_{ij}-rh_{ij}
. If the curvature of
\nabla
is self-conjugate it follows that
n\bar{R}_{ij}=nR_{ij}=rh_{ij}
, so that
(\nabla,h)
is naive Einstein. If
n>2
this implies
(\nabla,h)
is Einstein by Lemma
4.6
. If
n=2
, that
(\nabla,h)
be projectively flat means
0=2\nabla_{[i}P_{j]k}=-dr_{[i}h_{j]k}
. Tracing this in
jk
yields
dr_{i}=0
, so that
r
is constant and
(\nabla,h)
is Einstein. This shows that (
2
) implies (
3
).

If
(\nabla,h)
is projectively flat and Einstein, then, as in the preceding paragraph,
R_{ijkl}=\tfrac{2}{n-1}h_{l[i}R_{j]k}
. If
(\nabla,h)
is naive Einstein, then this last expression equals
-\tfrac{r}{n(n-1)}r(h\owedge h)_{ijkl}
. If
(\nabla,h)
is moreover Einstein, then
r
is constant. This shows that (
3
) implies (
1
).

For a special statistical structure,

(4.21)

\displaystyle B_{ijkl}=R_{ijkl}+2h_{l[i}P_{j]k}=R_{ijkl}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}h_{l[i}R_{j]k}.

By (
4.16
) and (
4.21
) applied to the conjugate special statistical structure,

(4.22)

\displaystyle\bar{B}_{ijkl}=\bar{R}_{ijkl}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}h_{l[i}\bar{R}_{j]k}=-R_{ijlk}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}h_{l[i}\bar{R}_{j]k}=-B_{ijlk}-\tfrac{2}{n-1}(h_{k[i}R_{j]l}+h_{l[i}\bar{R}_{j]k}).

If there holds (
3
), then the right-hand side of (
4.22
) vanishes, so
\bar{B}_{ijkl}
vanishes. If
n>2
this implies
(\nabla,h)
is conjugate projectively flat. This shows (
3
) implies (
4
) if
n>2
. If
(\nabla,h)
is both projectively flat and conjugate projectively flat, then (
4.22
) implies
h_{k[i}R_{j]l}+h_{l[i}\bar{R}_{j]k}=0
. Tracing this in
il
and in
jk
and relabeling the results yields
(n-1)\bar{R}_{ij}+R_{ij}=rh_{jk}=(n-1)R_{ij}+\bar{R}_{ij}
. If
n>2
this implies
\bar{R}_{ij}=R_{ij}
and
nR_{ij}=rh_{ij}=n\bar{R}_{ij}
, so that
(\nabla,h)
is naive Einstein. In (
4.21
) and (
4.22
), this shows
R_{ijkl}=-\tfrac{r}{n(n-1)}(h\owedge h)_{ijkl}=\bar{R}_{ijkl}
and, because
n>2
, by Lemma
4.6
, this shows
r
is constant. This proves (
4
) implies (
1
) when
n>2
.
∎

Definition 4.8
.

A special statistical structure has
constant curvature
if it satisfies the equivalent conditions (
1
)-(
3
) of Lemma
4.7
.

Theorem
4.9
shows that for a special statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
with cubic form
\mathit{L}
and self-conjugate curvature the hierarchy of coupled equations for
(h,\mathit{L})
corresponds with a geometrical natural hierarchy of equations for
(\nabla,h)
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
(\nabla,h)
be a special statistical structure with cubic form
\mathit{L}
and scalar curvature
r
on a manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
.

(1)

(\nabla,h)
has constant curvature if and only if
(h,\mathit{L})
solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) with constants
c=\tfrac{1}{4}
and
\kappa=r
;

(2)

(\nabla,h)
has self-conjugate curvature and is Einstein if and only if
(h,\mathit{L})
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) with constants
c=\tfrac{1}{4}
and
\kappa=r
;

(3)

(\nabla,h)
has self-conjugate curvature and constant scalar curvature if and only if
(h,\mathit{L})
solves the coupled constraint equations (
TFC3
) with constants
c=\tfrac{1}{4}
and
\kappa=r
.

Proof.

Because the cubic form of a special statistical structure is trace-free, by Lemma
4.4
, the cubic form of a special statistical structure is a trace-free Codazzi tensor if and only if the special statistical structure has self-conjugate curvature.

By the preceding and (
4.10
)-(
4.12
), for a special statistical structure with self-conjugate curvature:

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{ijkl}-\tfrac{1}{4}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})=R_{ijkl},

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{ij}-\tfrac{1}{4}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathit{L}\owedge\mathit{L})_{ij}=R_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathit{S}-\tfrac{1}{4}|\mathit{L}|^{2}=r.

Claim (
3
) is immediate from (
4.23
) and (
TFC3
) and claim (
2
) is immediate from (
4.23
) and (
TFC2
) if one notes that for an Einstein special statistical structure having self-conjugate curvature
r
is constant by assumption.

By Lemma
4.7
, if a special statistical structure having self-conjugate curvature is projectively flat, then its curvature satisfies
R_{ijkl}=-\tfrac{r}{n(n-1)}r(h\owedge h)_{ijkl}
with
r
constant. By Lemma
4.7
, in this case
r
is constant, so with (
4.23
) this shows
(h,\mathit{L})
solves (
TFC1
) with
c=\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\kappa=r
. Conversely, if
(h,\mathit{L})
solves (
TFC1
) with
c=\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\kappa=r
, then (
4.23
) shows
R_{ijkl}=-\tfrac{r}{n(n-1)}r(h\owedge h)_{ijkl}
, so by Lemma
4.7
,
(\nabla,h)
is projectively flat.
∎

The following paragraph summarizes the approach to the affine differential geometry of hypersurfaces detailed in
[
43
,
44
]
.
An immersed hypersurface
M
in
(n+1)
-dimensional flat affine space
\mathcal{A}
is nondegenerate if its second fundamental form is nondegenerate. Equivalently the conormal Gauss map
\gamma:M\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{\ast})
that associates with
p\in M
the annihilator of the tangent space
T_{p}M
, viewed as an element of the projectivization
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{\ast})
of the vector space
\mathcal{A}^{\ast}
dual to
\mathcal{A}
, is an immersion. In this case, the pullback via
\gamma
of the flat projective structure on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{\ast})
induces on
M
a flat projective structure
[\bar{\nabla}]
. A coorientation of
M
means an orientation of its normal bundle and such identifies the second fundamental form of
M
with a conformal structure
[h]
on
M
. Any choice of vector field
W
transverse to
M
and consistent with the given coorientation determines a symmetric two tensor
h
representing the second fundamental form with respect to the trivialization of the normal bundle determined by
W
, and
h
is a metric by the assumed nondegeneracy of
M
. The metrics corresponding with different choices of cooriented
W
are conformal. Each choice of
W
determines a torsion-free affine connection
\nabla
on
M
by projecting the flat affine connection on
\mathcal{A}
along
W
. The resulting
\nabla
is unchanged if
W
is rescaled, but varies when the direction of
W
is changed. The affine normal line bundle is the line field transverse to
M
determined by the requirement that for any local section
W
the induced
\nabla
satisfying
h^{pq}\nabla_{i}h_{pq}=nh^{pq}\nabla_{p}h_{qi}
with respect to any
h\in[h]
. If a parallel volume form
\Psi
on
\mathcal{A}
is fixed, a unique cooriented transversal
W
called the affine normal vector field is determined by requiring that it span the affine normal line bundle and that the volume densities on
M
determined by
\iota(W)\Psi
and
|\det h|^{1/2}
agree, where
h\in[h]
corresponds with
W
. The resulting
h
is called the equiaffine or Blaschke metric of
M
. Because
\Psi
is determined only up to multiplication by a constant by the requirement that it be parallel,
h
is determined only up to positive homothety. Together
h
and the connection
\nabla
induced via the affine normal
W
constitute a special statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
. The conjugate special statistical structure
(\bar{\nabla},h)
is such that
\bar{\nabla}
represents the flat projective structure
[\bar{\nabla}]
induced on
M
via the conormal Gauss map.

In summary, a cooriented nondegenerate immersed hypersurface
M
in flat affine space acquires a pair of conjugate special statistical structures one of which is necessarily projectively flat.

The shape operator of a cooriented nondegenerate immersed hypersurface
M
in flat affine space means the shape operator with respect to its affine normal
W
. Such a hypersurface is an
affine sphere
if its affine normal line bundles all meet in a point (its center) or are all parallel (center at infinity). In the first case the affine sphere is
proper
, in the second it is
improper
. Equivalently, its affine shape operator is a (constant) multiple of the identity. It is an elementary matter to check that this is the case if and only if one of, and hence both, the induced special statistical structures on
M
have constant curvature in the sense of Definition
4.8
; in particular they determine a solution of the coupled projectively flat equations, as was observed with different terminology in
[
43
]
; see also
[
44
,
46
]
.

These claims make sense whatever the signature of
[h]
, but more can be said when
[h]
has definite signature. When
M
is locally strictly convex so that
[h]
has Riemannian signature, then a proper affine sphere is
elliptic
or
hyperbolic
as its center lies on the side to which
W
points or on the side away from which
W
points. In this setting improper affine spheres are called
parabolic
. If the equiaffine metric of an elliptic affine sphere is complete, then it is the Fubini-Study metric on an ellipsoid
[
18
]
. If the equiaffine metric of a parabolic affine sphere is complete, then the affine sphere is an elliptic paraboloid and its equaffine metric is flat
[
65
,
16
]
. If the equiaffine metric of a hyperbolic affine sphere is complete, then it has nonpositive Ricci curvature
[
17
, Theorem
5.1
]
, and the cone over the boundary of the closure of its image in projective space is a pointed convex cone with vertex at the center of the affine sphere and to which the affine sphere is asymptotic, and all hyperbolic affine spheres with complete equiaffine metric arise in this way. These last affirmations summarize deep results due to Cheng and Yau, in particular in
[
29
]
; they are described in the form used here in the articles of J. Loftin
[
78
,
79
,
80
]
and further variants and discussion can be found in
[
46
,
74
,
66
]
.

In general signature there are not available similarly general existence and characterization results but many examples are known. See for example
[
47
,
48
,
59
,
60
]
.

Theorem 4.10
.

On an
n
-manifold
M
that carries a properly convex flat real projective structure
[\nabla]
, there is a complete Riemannian metric
h
, unique up to homothety, that with the unique
\nabla\in[\nabla]
satisfying
\nabla\det h=0
, constitutes a special statistical structure having constant sectional curvature in the sense of Definition
4.8
. In particular, the pair
(h,\mathit{L})
determined by
D-\nabla=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathit{L}_{ijp}h^{kp}
, where
D
is the Levi-Civita connection of
h
, solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) with
c=\tfrac{1}{4}
and a nonpositive constant
\kappa
. Moreover, the metric
h
has nonpositive Ricci curvature.

Proof.

The following sketch reformulates work of J. Loftin summarized in
[
78
,
80
]
based on earlier work of E. Calabi and S. T. Yau and S. Y. Cheng.
Let
\mathbb{V}
be an
n
-dimensional vector space. That
M
be a properly convex flat real projective
n
-manifold means that it is the quotient of a properly convex domain
\Omega
(having nonempty interior) in flat projective space
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
by a free and properly discontinuous action of a group
\Gamma\subset PGL(\mathbb{V})
on
\Omega
. That
\Omega
be properly convex means that it is convex and its closure is contained in some affine chart; in particular it contains no projective line. The cone over
\Omega
means the union
\hat{\Omega}=\cup_{p\in\Omega}\pi^{-1}(p)
where
\pi:\mathbb{V}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{V})
is the defining projection. The closure of either of the two connected components of
\hat{\Omega}
is a pointed convex cone in
\mathbb{V}
with vertex at the origin. By a theorem of S. T. Yau and S. Y. Cheng there is a unique foliation of its interior by complete hyperbolic affine spheres. Any one of these affine spheres is diffeomorphic to
\Omega
via
\pi
and their equiaffine metrics with respect to a parallel volume are all positively homothetic, so they determine on
\Omega
a positive homothety class of complete Riemannian metrics. The connections determined on these affine spheres via their affine normals all descend to the same connection on
\Omega
and with any one of these metrics it forms a special statistical structure having constant sectional curvature. That these metrics have nonpositive Ricci curvature follows from
[
17
, Theorem
5.1
]

∎

4.3.
Solutions from invariant polynomials on compact simple Lie algebras

There seems to be known no general existence result applicable to the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) for the case
k>3
. Here there are given some specific examples based on algebraic constructions.

Example 4.11
.

Let
G
be a connected compact simple Lie group of dimension greater than
3
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, let
h=-B
be the biinvariant metric determined by the negative of the Killing form of
\mathfrak{g}
, and note that
h
is Einstein with Ricci curvature
\tfrac{1}{4}h
.

Let
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\ast})
be the complete polarization of a homogeneous degree
k
polynomial
P
invariant under the adjoint action of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}
. More precisely, suppose that
P
is one of a set of homogeneous generators of the ring of invariant polynomials on
\mathfrak{g}
and that
k\geq 3
. If
\mathfrak{g}
has rank
l
then the ring of invariant polynomials on
\mathfrak{g}
is generated by
l
algebraically independent homogeneous elements
[
10
, section VIII.
8
]
. The degrees
2=u_{1}<\dots<u_{l}
of the homogeneous generators are given in terms of the exponents
m_{1}<\dots<m_{l}
of the Weyl group
W
of
\mathfrak{g}
by
u_{i}=m_{i}+1
, and satisfy
u_{1}\cdot\dots\cdot u_{l}=|W|
and
2(u_{1}+\dots+u_{l})=\dim\mathfrak{g}+l
(always,
u_{1}=2
).
Since
G
acts on
\mathfrak{g}
orthogonally, the
h
-Laplacian is invariant under the
G
-action, so
\Delta_{h}P
is again an invariant polynomial. Let
E
be the invariant polynomial corresponding to
h
. The harmonic part
Q
of
P
is obtained by subtracting from
P
a linear combination of terms of the form
E^{s}\Delta_{h}^{s}P
(
s>0
). Since each of these terms is
G
-invariant, so is
Q
. Since the homogeneous generators of the ring of invariant polynomials are algebraically independent, it cannot be that
P
is a linear combination of powers of
E
, so
Q
is not null. It follows that it may be supposed from the beginning that
P
is
\Delta_{h}
-harmonic, or, equivalently, that
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
is trace-free. In particular, this shows that on
\mathfrak{g}
there exists a
\Delta_{h}
-harmonic homogeneous
G
-invariant polynomial of degree at least
3
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
G
be a connected compact simple Lie group of dimension greater than
3
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
and let
h=-B
be the biinvariant metric on
G
determined by the negative of the Killing form
B
of
\mathfrak{g}
. Suppose
k\geq 3
and let
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\ast})
be the extension as a left-invariant tensor of the complete polarization of a
\Delta_{h}
-harmonic homogeneous
G
-invariant polynomial
P
of degree
k
.
The pair
(h,\omega)
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) on
G
.

Proof.

Let
D
be the Levi-Civita connection of
h=-B
and let
c_{ij}\,^{k}
be the structure tensor of the Lie algebra (so
x^{i}y^{j}c_{ij}\,^{k}=[x,y]^{k}
).
The invariance of
P
means that
0=kc_{i(i_{1}}\,^{p}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})p}=-2D_{i}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
, so that
\omega
is parallel, and so in the kernel of
\mathscr{K}
. Let
\sigma_{ij}=\omega_{ii_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}\omega_{j}\,^{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}
. Then

(4.24)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} c_{ij}\,^{p}\sigma_{pk}&=-c_{ji}\,^{p}\omega_{pi_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}\omega_{k}\,^{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=(k-1)c_{j(i_{1}}\,^{p}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k-1})pi}\omega_{k}\,^{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}\\
&=(k-1)c_{j(p}\,^{i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k-1})ki_{1}}\omega_{i}\,^{pi_{2}\dots i_{k-1}}=-c_{jk}\,^{i_{1}}\omega_{i_{1}pi_{2}\dots i_{k-1}}\omega_{i}\,^{pi_{2}\dots i_{k-1}}=-c_{jk}\,^{p}\sigma_{ip},\end{aligned}

showing that
\sigma_{ij}
is an invariant bilinear form. By the simplicity of
\mathfrak{g}
there is a constant
\kappa
such that
\sigma_{ij}=\kappa h_{ij}
, and tracing this equality shows that
\kappa=|\omega|^{2}/\dim\mathfrak{g}
. It follows that
(h,\omega)
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
).
∎

One expects that in general, and in some cases it can be proved, that the solutions of the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) obtained via Theorem
4.12
are not solutions of the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
). The simplest case in which this is easily shown is the following with
G=SU(n)
for
n>2
. Identify the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(n)
with the real vector space of
n\times n
trace-free Hermitian matrices. The Killing form
B
is
B(X,Y)=2n\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(XY)
where
\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}
is the usual matrix trace. Let
h
be the biinvariant metric determined on
G
by
-B
. The commutative product
X\circ Y=\mathsf{i}(XY+YX-\tfrac{2}{n}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(XY)I)
determines a completely symmetric trilinear form
\chi(X,Y,Z)=h(X\circ Y,Z)=-2n\mathsf{i}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(XYZ+YXZ)
with associated cubic form
P(X)=-4n\mathsf{i}\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(X^{3})
that is evidently invariant under the adjoint action of
G
. The reason for assuming
n>2
is that
P(X)
vanishes identically when
n=2
. On the other hand, when
n>2
,
P
is nontrivial.

By Theorem
4.12
, for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
the pair
(h,2t\chi)
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) on
G
. Define
L:\mathfrak{su}(n)\to\operatorname{End}(\mathfrak{su}(n))
by
L(X)Y=X\circ Y
. The pair
(h,2t\chi)
determines the special statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
with
\nabla^{t}=D-tL
where
D
is the Levi-Civita connection of
h
. Explicitly,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\nabla^{t}_{X}Y=(\tfrac{1}{2}\text{ad}(X)-tL(X))Y.

The curvature
R^{t}(X,Y)Z
of
\nabla^{t}
can be computed using the invariance of
L
,
[\text{ad}(X),L(Y)]=L([X,Y])
, and that

(4.26)

\displaystyle[L(X),L(Y)]Z=X\circ(Y\circ Z)-Y\circ(X\circ Z)=-\text{ad}([X,Y])Z+\tfrac{2}{n^{2}}(h(X,Z)Y-h(Y,Z)X),

and the result is

(4.27)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} R^{t}(X,Y)Z&=-\tfrac{1}{4}\text{ad}([X,Y])Z+t^{2}[L(X),L(Y)]Z\\
&=-(\tfrac{1}{4}+t^{2})[[X,Y],Z]+\tfrac{2t^{2}}{n^{2}}(h(X,Z)Y-h(Y,Z)X).\end{aligned}

so that

(4.28)

\displaystyle h(R^{t}(X,Y)Z,W)=-(\tfrac{1}{4}+t^{2})h([X,Y],[Z,W])-\tfrac{2t^{2}}{n^{2}}(h(Y,Z)h(X,W)-h(X,Z)h(Y,W))

from which it is apparent that the statistical structure
(\nabla,h)
does not have constant curvature, so, by Theorem
4.9
,
(h,2t\chi)
does not solve the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
). The special statistical structures
(\nabla^{\pm t},h)
are conjugate and by (
4.27
) they have self-conjugate curvature.
Because

(4.29)

\displaystyle R^{t}(\,\cdot\,\,,X)Y=-(\tfrac{1}{4}+t^{2})\text{ad}(Y)\text{ad}(X)+\tfrac{2t^{2}}{n^{2}}(X\otimes h(Y,\,\cdot\,\,)-h(X,Y)\operatorname{Id}_{\mathfrak{su}(n)}),

the Ricci curvature of
\nabla^{t}
is

(4.30)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathit{Ric}}^{t}(X,Y)=\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}R^{t}(\,\cdot\,\,,X)Y=(\tfrac{1}{4}+(\tfrac{4}{n^{2}}-1)t^{2})h(X,Y),

which is positive if
t^{2}<\tfrac{n^{2}}{4(n^{2}-4)}
, zero when
t^{2}=\tfrac{n^{2}}{4(n^{2}-4)}
, and negative otherwise.

The one-parameter family of biinvariant affine connections
\nabla^{t}
was found by T. Laquer who determined all biinvariant affine connections on compact Lie groups
[
71
]
. Laquer proved that the space of biinvariant affine connections on compact simple real Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is one-dimensional except when
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{su}(n)
and
n\geq 3
, in which case there is a two-dimensional family of biinvariant connections (the case
\mathfrak{so}(6)
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{su}(4)
). Among these connections the torsion-free ones are exactly the connections
\nabla^{t}
. The geometric significance of these connections was not apparent from Laquer’s results. The preceding results show that
(\nabla^{t},h)
is a one-parameter family of Riemannian signature Einstein special statistical structures having self-conjugate curvature but not having constant curvature (except when
t=0
). Moreover they exist on a compact manifold. In particular, these examples are not locally equivalent to the special statistical structures induced on an affine hypersurface because they are neither projectively nor conjugate projectively flat.

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
G
be a connected compact simple Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of dimension at least
3
. Suppose that
\nabla
is a left-invariant affine connection that together with the Riemannian metric
h=-B
determined by the negative of the Killing form generates a left-invariant Einstein special statistical structure having self-conjugate curvature. Then either
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
h
, or
\mathfrak{g}
has type
A_{l}
for some
l\geq 2
and
(\nabla,h)
is locally isomorphic to one of the one-parameter family of biinvariant Einstein special statistical structures
(\nabla^{t},h)
on
SU(n)
defined by (
4.25
).

In interpreting the conclusion of Corollary
4.13
, recall that Dynkin diagrams of types
A_{3}
and
D_{3}
coincide, corresponding to the fact that
SU(4)
is the connected double cover of
SO(6)
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in S^{3}(\mathfrak{g}^{\ast})
be the cubic torsion of a left-invariant Einstein special statistical structure having self-conjugate curvature,
(\nabla,h)
. By Theorem
4.9
,
(h,\chi)
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
), so
\operatorname{\delta}(\chi)=0
and
\mathscr{C}(\chi)=0
. On the other hand,
2D_{i}\chi_{jkl}=c_{i(j}^{p}\chi_{kl)p}
, so
D_{(i}\chi_{jkl)}=0
. Hence
c_{i(j}^{p}\chi_{kl)p}=2D_{i}\chi_{jkl}=0
, which shows the invariance of the cubic polynomial
P
on
\mathfrak{g}
whose complete polarization is
\chi_{ijk}
. The only cases in which there is an invariant homogeneous polynomial of degree
u_{2}=3
are when
\mathfrak{g}
has type
A_{l}
and
l\geq 2
and when
\mathfrak{g}
has type
D_{3}
(which is redundant since
D_{3}=A_{3}
). Note that it follows as well that in the cases where there is a nonzero invariant cubic polynomial on
\mathfrak{g}
it is unique up to scale. It also must be trace-free, for its trace corresponds to the
h
-Laplacian of
P
; since
G
acts on
\mathfrak{g}
orthogonally and the Laplacian is orthogonally invariant,
\Delta_{h}P
is invariant, but there are no nonzero invariant homogeneous linear forms on
\mathfrak{g}
, so
\Delta_{h}P=0
. Similarly, the invariance of
\chi_{ijk}
implies the invariance of
\chi_{ip}\,^{q}\chi_{jq}\,^{p}
, and the simplicity of
\mathfrak{g}
then forces that
\chi_{ip}\,^{q}\chi_{jq}\,^{p}
is a multiple of
h_{ij}
. That
(\nabla,h
must be locally isomorphic to one of the examples on
SU(n)
defined by (
4.25
) follows from the uniqueness up to scale of the invariant cubic polynomial
P
.
∎

In
[
72
]
, Laquer determined the space of left-invariant affine connections on a compact irreducible Riemannian symmetric space. Examples like that just described occur for the spaces
SU(n)/SO(n)
,
(SU(n)\times SU(n))/SU(n)
,
SU(2n)/Sp(n)
, all with
n\geq 3
, and
E_{6}/F_{4}
. The case
(SU(n)\times SU(n))/SU(n)
is that already described. The connections are given essentially as in the preceding example, with the Levi-Civita connection of the underlying Riemannian symmetric space perturbed by adding to it a multiple of a commutative product that can be described as the trace-free Jordan part of the Jordan product on the algebra of
n\times n
Hermitian matrices over one of the real Hurwitz algebras,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{H}
, or
\mathbb{O}
(in the last case, of the octonions,
n=3
). These yield further examples of compact Riemannian signature solutions of the coupled Einstein equations that are not coupled projectively flat. These same connections arise in the classification of totally real parallel submanifolds in complex projective space obtained by H. Naitoh
[
87
]
; in that context they arise as in Example
4.1.2
. An argument like that proving Corollary
4.13
will show that these are essentially the unique left-invariant examples. The essence of the proof of this claim is contained in the proof of
[
87
, Theorem
4.5
]
. Because the details are formally quite similar to those given above, but writing them would take considerable space, they are omitted.

4.4.
Solutions of other algebraic origins

When the metric
h
is flat, the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) admit purely algebraic solutions. Inwhat follows,
h
is a flat Riemannian metric on an
n
-dimensional vector space
\mathbb{V}
, although many of the claims make sense in other signatures. Let
x^{i}
denote the radial Euler vector field. Let
E(x)=|x|^{2}
. Let
\Delta=\Delta_{h}
. If
F(x)
is a function on
\mathbb{V}
, let
F_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=D_{i_{1}}\dots D_{i_{k}}F
. Sometimes it is convenient to write
D^{(k)}F
for the
k
-fold covariant derivative of
F
(for example
D^{(2)}F
is the Hessian of
F
). If
F
is a polynomial homogeneous of degree
g
then
x^{i_{1}}\dots x^{i_{j}}F_{i_{1}\dots i_{j}}=g(g-1)\dots(g-j+1)F
. In this case
F_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
is a constant tensor, so parallel.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
F
be an
h
-harmonic polynomial homogeneous of degree
g\geq 2
on the
n
-dimensional Euclidean vector space
(\mathbb{V},h)
. Let
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}=F_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
. The pair
(h,\omega)
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
) if and only if there is
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
F
solves any one of the following equivalent equations:

(4.31)

\displaystyle 0=F_{ip_{1}\dots p_{g-1}}F_{j}\,^{p_{1}\dots p_{g-1}}-ch_{ij},

\displaystyle 0=D_{i}D_{j}\left(|D^{(g-1)}F|^{2}-cE\right),

\displaystyle 0=|D^{(g-1)}F|^{2}-cE.

In this case
nc=|D^{(g)}F|^{2}
.

Proof.

The equivalence of the first two equations of (
4.31
) follows from the vanishing of
D^{(g+1)}F
. Because
|D^{(g-1)}F|^{2}-cE
is a homogeneous quadratic polynomial, its Hessian vanishes if and only if it vanishes identically, so these equations are equivalent to the last equation of (
4.31
).
∎

Example 4.15
.

A hypersurface in a Riemannian space form is
isoparametric
if its principal curvatures are constant. The question of classifying isoparametric hypersurfaces was posed and partially solved by E. Cartan in
[
23
,
24
,
22
]
. See
[
31
,
96
,
102
]
for background. In
[
85
,
84
]
it is shown that for an isoparametric hypersurface in a constant curvature
(n-1)
-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\{x\in\mathbb{V}:E(x)=1\}
:

•

the number
g
of distinct principal curvatures satisfies
g\in\{1,2,3,4,6\}
;

•

if the distinct principal curvatures are ordered
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>\dots>\lambda_{g}
, the multiplicities,
m_{i}
, of the
\lambda_{i}
satisfy
m_{i}=m_{i+2}
(indices modulo
6
), so that there are at most two distinct multiplicities
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
(moreover, if
g<4
then
m_{1}=m_{2}
always); and

•

every such hypersurface arises as a level set of the restriction to the sphere of a polynomial
P:\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{R}
homogeneous of degree
g
and satisfying the equations

(4.32)

\displaystyle|dP|^{2}=g^{2}E^{g-1},

\displaystyle\Delta P=\tfrac{m_{2}-m_{1}}{2}g^{2}E^{\tfrac{g}{2}-1}.

A polynomial
P
solving (
4.32
) is called a
Cartan-Münzner polynomial
. Examples of solutions for which the resulting hypersurfaces are not extrinsically homogeneous are known when
g=4
; see for example
[
38
]
.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
P
be a Cartan-Münzner polynomial homogeneous of degree
g\geq 2
and having multiplicities
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
on the
n
-dimensional Euclidean vector space
(\mathbb{V},h)
. Then the trace-free part
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
of
P_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
solves (
4.31
), so the pair
(h,\omega)
solves the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
).

Proof.

It suffices to show that
P
solves an equation of the form (
4.31
). The following identity is needed. For any polynomial
F
homogeneous of degree
g
there holds

(4.33)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta(E^{i}F)&=2i(n+2(g+i-1))E^{i-1}F+E^{i}\Delta F.\end{aligned}

In particular, the special case
f=1
yields
\Delta E^{i}=2i(n+2(i-1))E^{i-1}
.

In the case
m_{1}=m_{2}
, the polynomial
P
is harmonic and so
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}=P_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
. In this case applying
\Delta^{g-2}
to the first equation of (
4.32
) and simplifying the result using (
4.33
) yields

(4.34)

\displaystyle 2^{g-2}g(g!)(n+2(g-2))\dots(n+2)E=\Delta^{g-2}(g^{2}E^{g-1})=\Delta^{g-2}|dP|^{2}=2^{g-2}|D^{(g-1)}P|^{2}.

Differentiating this yields

(4.35)

\displaystyle D_{i}D_{j}|D^{(g-1)}P|^{2}=2g(g!)(n+2(g-2))\dots(n+2)h_{ij},

which suffices to show that
P
solves (
4.31
). The argument in the general case is similar, but more involved. Since
m_{1}=m_{2}
, if
g<4
, it can be supposed that
g\in\{4,6\}
. In particular,
g
is even. Let
P=\sum_{i=0}^{g/2}E^{i}Q^{(g-2i)}
be the Lefschetz decomposition of
P
into its harmonic components. Here
Q^{(g-2i)}
is a harmonic polynomial homogeneous of degree
g-2i
, and the decomposition is uniquely determined. Applying
\Delta
to both sides and using (
4.33
) yields

(4.36)

\displaystyle\tfrac{m_{2}-m_{1}}{2}g^{2}E^{\tfrac{g}{2}-1}=\Delta P=\sum_{i=1}^{g/2}2i(n+2(g-i-1))E^{i-1}Q^{(g-2i)}.

By the uniqueness of the Lefschetz decomposition, this implies
Q^{(g-2i)}=0
if
0<i<g/2
. Hence

(4.37)

\displaystyle P=Q+\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})g}{2(n+g-2)}E^{g/2}

where
Q
is a harmonic polynomial homogeneous of degree
g
. Note that the desired tensor
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
equals
Q_{i_{1}\dots,i_{g}}
. Calculating the differential of (
4.37
) using the homogeneity of
Q
yields

(4.38)

\displaystyle g^{2}E^{g-1}=|dP|^{2}=|dQ|^{2}+\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})g^{3}}{(n+g-2)}E^{g/2-1}Q+\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})^{2}g^{2}}{(n+g-2)^{2}}E^{g-1},

so that

(4.39)

\displaystyle 0=|dQ|^{2}+g^{3}\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})}{(n+g-2)}E^{g/2-1}Q+g^{2}\left(\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})^{2}}{(n+g-2)^{2}}-1\right)E^{g-1}.

Applying
\Delta^{g-2}
to both sides of (
4.39
) and simplifying using (
4.33
) yields

(4.40)

\displaystyle 0=2^{g-2}\left(|D^{(g-1)}Q|^{2}+\left(\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})^{2}}{(n+g-2)^{2}}-1\right)g(g!)(n+2(g-2))(n+2(g-3))\dots(n+2)E\right).

Hence

(4.41)

\displaystyle Q_{ip_{1}\dots p_{g-1}}Q_{j}\,^{p_{1}\dots p_{g-1}}=\left(1-\tfrac{(m_{2}-m_{1})^{2}}{(n+g-2)^{2}}\right)g(g!)(n+2(g-2))(n+2(g-3))\dots(n+2)h_{ij}.

Because
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{g}}
is parallel, this suffices to prove the claim.
∎

Example 4.17
.

Let
E=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
be an enumeration of the edge set of a finite
k
-regular graph with vertex set
V
. The partial Steiner system
\mathcal{B}
determined by the incidence of edges in the given graph is the collection of
k
-element subsets (blocks) of
\bar{n}=\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}\in\mathcal{B}
if and only if the edges
e_{i_{1}},\dots,e_{i_{k}}
are incident at some vertex in
V
. Let
\mathbb{V}
be the
n
-dimensional real vector space generated by
E
and equip
\mathbb{V}
with the flat Riemannian metric
h
with respect to which
E
is an ordered orthonormal basis. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be the coordinates of
x\in\mathbb{V}
with respect to the ordered basis
E
. The quadratic form
Q(x)
determined by
h
is
Q(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}
. Associate with
I\in\mathcal{B}
the monomial
x_{I}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}
. Let
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{B}}
, so that
\epsilon_{I}\in\{\pm 1\}
for each
I\in\mathcal{B}
.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the partial Steiner system determined by the incidence of edges in a finite
k
-regular graph. For the homogeneity
k
polynomial
P(x)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{B}}\epsilon_{I}x_{I}

associated with the
k
-regular graph and any choice of signs
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}^{\mathcal{B}}
, the pair
(h,D^{(k)}P)
solves the coupled Einstein equation (
TFC2
).

Proof.

The component
\tfrac{\partial^{k-1}P}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k-1}}}
is nonzero if and only if
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1}\}
is contained in some block
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}
in
\mathcal{B}
(because any
k-1
edges meet at most one vertex there is at most one such block). In this case
\tfrac{\partial^{k-1}P}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\dots\partial x_{i_{k-1}}}=\epsilon_{I}x_{i_{k}}
. Since there are
(k-1)!
orderings of the distinct indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1}
and since the index of a given edge appears in exactly two blocks there results
|D^{(k-1)}P|^{2}=2(k-1)!Q
. Because no variable
x_{i}
appears in any monomial of
P
with a power higher than one,
P
is
h
-harmonic. By Lemma
4.14
this shows that the pair
(h,D^{(k)}P)
solves (
TFC2
).
∎

5.
Properties of solutions

This section describes qualitative properties of solutions to the coupled hierarchies for completely symmetric tensors. Section
5.2
states the main theorems to be proved, Theorems
5.3
and
5.5
. The preliminary section
5.1
gives some definitions needed in their formulations and proofs. The subsequent sections give the technical material needed for their proofs: Section
5.3
reviews Weitzenböck formulas; Section
5.4
presents refined Kato inequalities; Section
5.5
describes bounds on quantities such as
|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}
; and Section
5.6
assembles this material to give the proofs of Theorems
5.3
and
5.5
.

5.1.
Metric curvature tensors as operators

Any
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
determines a self-adjoint endomorphism of
S^{2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
defined by
a_{ij}\in S^{2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}\to\mathscr{Y}_{ipjq}a^{pq}\in S^{2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, but this endomorphism need not preserve the subspace
S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. The modified endomorphism
a_{ij}\to a^{pq}(\mathscr{Y}_{ipjq}+\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p(i}h_{j)q})
restricts to a self-adjoint endomorphism of
S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. This is the
k=2
special case of the following construction that goes back to A. Lichnerowicz
[
75
, section
10
]
.

Lemma 5.1
.

For
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
, the degree
0
graded linear operator
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}:S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})\to S(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
defined by

(5.1)

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=k\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})}\,^{p}-k(k-1)\mathscr{Y}_{p(i_{1}i_{2}}\,^{q}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})q}\,^{p},

\displaystyle\omega\in S^{k}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast}),

commutes with the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
- triple
\{\mathsf{X}_{+},\mathsf{X}_{-},\mathsf{Z}\}
. In particular
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(h^{\odot k})=0
for all
k\geq 1
, and
[\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}},\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}]=0
, so
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}
restricts to an endomorphism of
S_{0}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
.

Proof.

It is claimed that

(5.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p(i_{1}}\mathsf{X}_{+}(\omega)_{i_{2}\dots i_{k+2})}\,^{p}&=\tbinom{k+1}{2}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})^{p}\,_{i_{3}}\omega_{i_{4}\dots i_{k+2})p}+(k+1)\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k+2})},\\
\mathscr{Y}^{p}\,_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\,{}^{q}\mathsf{X}_{+}(\omega)_{i_{3}\dots i_{k+2})pq}&=\tbinom{k}{2}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\mathscr{Y}^{p}\,_{i_{3}i_{4}}\,{}^{q}\omega_{i_{5}\dots i_{k+2})pq}+\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k+2})}.\end{aligned}

Combining (
5.2
) using (
5.1
) yields
[\mathsf{X}_{+},\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}]=0
. The validity of (
5.2
) is shown as follows. Write

(5.3)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathsf{X}_{+}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}p}&=h_{pi_{k+1}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k}}+kh_{p(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})i_{k+1}}+kh_{i_{k+1}(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})p}+\tbinom{k}{2}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})i_{k+1}p}.\end{aligned}

Contracting (
5.3
) with
\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+2}}\,^{p}
yields

(5.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+2}}\,^{p}\mathsf{X}_{+}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}p}&=\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+1}i_{k+2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k}}+k\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+2}(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})i_{k+1}}\\
&+k\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+2}}\,^{p}h_{i_{k+1}(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})p}+\tbinom{k}{2}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+2}}\,^{p}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})i_{k+1}p}.\end{aligned}

Symmetrizing (
5.4
) over the uncontracted indices yields the first identity in (
5.2
). Relabeling
i_{k+1}
as
q
in (
5.3
) and contracting the result with
\mathscr{Y}^{p}\,_{i_{k+1}i_{k+2}}\,{}^{q}
yields

(5.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathscr{Y}^{p}\,_{i_{k+1}i_{k+2}}\,{}^{q}\mathsf{X}_{+}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}pq}\\
&=\mathscr{Y}^{p}\,_{i_{k+1}i_{k+2}}\,{}^{q}\left(h_{pq}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k}}+kh_{p(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})q}+kh_{q(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})p}+\tbinom{k}{2}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})pq}\right)\\
&=\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{i_{k+1}i_{k+2}}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}-k\mathscr{Y}_{i_{k+2}}\,^{q}\,{}_{i_{k+1}(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})q}\\
&\quad-k\mathscr{Y}_{i_{k+1}}\,^{q}\,{}_{i_{k+2}(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{2}\dots i_{k})q}+\tbinom{k}{2}\mathscr{Y}^{p}\,_{i_{k+1}i_{k+2}}\,{}^{q}h_{(i_{1}i_{2}}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})pq}\end{aligned}

Symmetrizing (
5.5
) over the uncontracted indices yields the second identity in (
5.2
). Similarly,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathsf{X}_{-}(\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(\omega))_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-2}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}h^{i_{k-1}i_{k-2}}\left(k\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p(i_{1}}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k})}\,^{p}+k(1-k)\mathscr{Y}_{p(i_{1}i_{2}}\,^{q}\omega_{i_{3}\dots i_{k})q}\,^{p}\right)\\
&=-\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})^{pq}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-2}pq}+(k-2)\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p(i_{1}}\mathsf{X}_{-}(\omega)_{i_{2}\dots i_{k-2})}\,^{p}\\
&\quad+\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p}\,^{q}\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-2}q}\,^{p}-2\tbinom{k-2}{2}\mathscr{Y}_{p(i_{1}i_{2}}\,^{q}\mathsf{X}_{-}(\omega)_{i_{3}\dots i_{k-2})q}\,^{p}\\
&=(k-2)\left(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})_{p(i_{1}}\mathsf{X}_{-}(\omega)_{i_{2}\dots i_{k-2})}\,^{p}+(3-k)\mathscr{Y}_{p(i_{1}i_{2}}\,^{q}\mathsf{X}_{-}(\omega)_{i_{3}\dots i_{k-2})q}\,^{p}\right)=\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(\mathsf{X}_{-}(\omega))_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-2}},\end{aligned}

which shows
[\mathsf{X}_{-},\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}]=0
.
That
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(h^{\odot k})=0
follows from
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(h)=0
and (
3.3
) by induction.
Because
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}
commutes with the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
-triple
\{\mathsf{X}_{+},\mathsf{X}_{-},\mathsf{Z}\}
, it preserves the decomposition of symmetric tensors into their primitive parts. In particular, it commutes with
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}
.
∎

It follows from (
5.1
) and (
2.27
) that for
\alpha,\beta\in S^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \langle\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(\alpha),\beta\rangle&=\tbinom{k}{2}\langle\alpha\owedge\beta,\mathscr{Y}\rangle+k\langle\operatorname{\rho}(\alpha\owedge\beta),\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})\rangle-k\left(\langle\iota(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y}))\alpha,\mathsf{X}_{-}(\beta)\rangle+\langle\mathsf{X}_{-}(\alpha),\iota(\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y}))\beta\rangle\right).\end{aligned}

If
\alpha,\beta\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
then the last two terms in (
5.7
) vanish.

Because the operator
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}
associated with
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
is self-adjoint it determines on any
\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}
-invariant subspace
E\subset\otimes^{k}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
a quadratic form defined by
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{Y}}(\omega)=\langle\omega,\wideparen{\mathscr{Y}}(\omega)\rangle
for
\omega\in E
. Taking
\beta=\alpha=\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
in (
5.7
) yields
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{Y}}(\omega)=k\langle\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega),\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})\rangle+\tbinom{k}{2}\langle\omega\owedge\omega,\mathscr{Y}\rangle
.

Example 5.2
.

The metric curvature tensor
\mathscr{H}=-\tfrac{1}{n(n-1)}h\owedge h
determines an operator
\wideparen{\mathscr{H}}
and an associated quadratic form
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{H}}
. The coefficient is chosen so that
\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{H})=\tfrac{1}{n}h
and
\mathit{S}(\mathscr{H})=1
. That a metric
h
have constant sectional curvature
\mathit{S}
is equivalent to the statement
\mathit{Riem}=\mathit{S}\mathscr{H}
. By (
5.7
) there hold

(5.8)

\displaystyle\wideparen{\mathscr{H}}(\omega)=\tfrac{k(n+k-2)}{n(n-1)}\omega,

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{H}}(\omega)=\tfrac{k(n+k-2)}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{2},

\displaystyle\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)).

In particular,
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{H}}
is positive definite on
S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. Note that
\mathscr{H}
is
negative
definite when viewed as an endomorphism of
\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
.

5.2.
Constraints on solutions: Calabi-Cheng-Yau growth estimates and Simons style pinching

This section describes a priori constraints on the growth of solutions of the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) when
h
is Riemannian. It would be interesting to extend, even partially, such constraints to solutions of the coupled Einstein equations (
TFC2
).

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
M
be a manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
. Suppose
h
is a complete Riemannian metric which with a trace-free Codazzi tensor
\omega
solves the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) for
c>0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
.

(1)

If
\kappa\geq 0
then
\omega
is identically zero, and
h
is a metric of constant sectional curvature.

(2)

If
\kappa<0
then

•

If
\kappa<0
and
k\geq 3
, then
\sup_{M}|\omega|^{2}\leq-\kappa/c
, so the scalar curvature
\mathit{S}=c|\omega|^{2}+\kappa
of
h
is nonpositive.

•

If
\kappa<0
and
k=2
, then
\sup_{M}|\omega|^{2}\leq-\tfrac{\kappa}{c(n-1)}
, so the scalar curvature
\mathit{S}
of
h
satisfies
\mathit{S}=c|\omega|^{2}+\kappa\leq\tfrac{n-2}{n-1}\kappa
(which is strictly negative if
n>2
).

Remark 5.4
.

In the special case corresponding to the context of the cubic form of a complete hyperbolic affine sphere, Calabi
[
17
]
showed the nonpositivity of the Ricci curvature. It seems plausible that, perhaps with some additional conditions, the nonpositivity of the scalar curvature in Theorem
5.3
can be improved to nonpositivity of the Ricci curvature. The remaining technical issue is that certain tensorial identities used in Calabi’s argument for
k=3
are special to that case, so some new ingredient is needed to extend these results to
k>3
.

The proof of Theorem
5.3
requires the Weitzenböck formulas described in Section
5.3
, the refined Kato inequalities described in Section
5.4
, and a theorem of Cheng-Yau on the growth of solutions to a differential inequality of the form
\Delta u\geq Bu^{1+\sigma}-Au
recalled as Theorem
5.38
.

These results can be understood as generalizations of results for holomorphic tensors on surfaces and as a counterparts to classical vanishing theorems for holomorphic symmetric tensors due to Kobayashi
[
67
,
68
]
.

For
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there holds

(5.9)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{k}\langle\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega),\omega\rangle=\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}

by (
5.7
). The proofs of Theorem
5.3
and Corollary
5.5
below depend on estimating (
5.9
) from above and below. Because the unit sphere in
S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is compact, the expression (
5.9
) assumes its maximum and minimum values on the sphere, so there are constants
\Lambda_{n,k},\lambda_{n,k}\in\mathbb{R}
defined by

(5.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Lambda_{n,k}&=\max\{\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}:\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast},|\omega|^{2}=1\},\\
\lambda_{n,k}&=\min\{\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}:\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast},|\omega|^{2}=1\},\end{aligned}

and for all
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, there hold

(5.11)

\displaystyle\lambda_{n,k}|\omega|^{4}\leq\tfrac{1}{k}\langle\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega),\omega\rangle=\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\leq\Lambda_{n,k}|\omega|^{4}.

Note that
\Lambda_{n,k}
and
\lambda_{n,k}
depend only on the homothety class of the chosen Euclidean metric. A basic technical issue is to obtain the values of
\Lambda_{n,k}
and
\lambda_{n,k}
and to characterize the
\omega
for which there holds either of the equalities in (
5.11
). In Section
5.5
it will be apparent that this is easier for
\lambda_{n,k}
than for
\Lambda_{n,k}
.

Let
E
and
F
be bundles of tensors on
M
. A metric
h
on
M
determines a pairing
\left(\alpha,\beta\right)=\int_{M}\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\,d\operatorname{vol}_{h}
of sections
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(E)
, at least one of which is compactly supported. Write
\lVert\omega\rVert^{2}=(\omega,\omega)
.

Theorem
5.5
is the integral bound parallel to that of Theorem
5.3
for solutions of the coupled projectively flat theorem (
TFC1
) for negative
c
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
M
be a compact oriented manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
. Suppose the Riemannian metric
h
and the trace-free Codazzi tensor
\omega
solve the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) for
c<0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

(5.12)

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{M}|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}\kappa+c\Lambda_{n,k})|\omega|^{2}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h}.

Estimates of
\Lambda_{n,k}
obtained in Section
5.5
yield Corollary
5.6
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
M
be a compact oriented manifold of dimension
n\geq 3
. Suppose the Riemannian metric
h
and the trace-free Codazzi tensor
\omega
solve the coupled projectively flat equations (
TFC1
) for
c<0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

(5.13)

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{M}|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}\kappa+c(1+\tfrac{(2n+1)(k-1)}{n})|\omega|^{2}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h},

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,k>3.

(5.14)

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{M}|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{n+1}{n(n-1)}\kappa+\tfrac{2n-1}{n}c|\omega|^{2}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h},

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,k=3.

(5.15)

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{M}|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{1}{n-1}\kappa+c|\omega|^{2}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h},

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,k=2.

Example 5.7
.

The
k=2
case of Corollary
5.6
recovers an integral estimate of the scalar curvature of a compact mean curvature zero hypersurface in a round sphere due to J. Simons
[
97
]
.
Applied with
h
the induced metric and
\omega
the second fundamental form of a mean curvature zero compact immersed hypersurface in the
(n+1)
-dimensional round sphere of scalar curvature
n(n+1)
as in Example
4.1.1
, Theorem
5.5
recovers the specialization to such hypersurfaces of a well known theorem of Simons
[
97
, Theorem
5.3.2
]
(which applies to compact submanifolds of arbitrary codimension). Concretely, in this case,
c=-1
and
\mathit{S}-|\omega|^{2}=\kappa=n(n-1)
, so (
5.15
) becomes

(5.16)

\displaystyle 0\geq\int_{M}|\omega|^{2}\left(n-|\omega|^{2}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h}=-n^{2}(n-1)^{2}\int_{M}\left(1-\tfrac{\mathit{S}}{n(n-1)}\right)\left(\tfrac{n-2}{n-1}-\tfrac{\mathit{S}}{n(n-1)}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h}.

which recovers
[
97
, Theorems
5.3.2
and
5.3.3
]
.
From (
5.16
) it follows that either
\mathit{S}=n(n-1)
and
\omega
is identically zero, so that the hypersurface is a totally geodesic hypersphere;
\mathit{S}=n(n-2)
and
|\omega|^{2}=n
, in which case
\omega
is parallel; or
\inf_{M}\mathit{S}<n(n-2)
, which is
[
97
, Corollary
5.3.3
]
.

Example 5.8
.

The
k=3
case of Corollary
5.6
recovers the analogous integral estimate for the scalar curvature of a compact mean curvature zero Lagrangian submanifold of a constant holomorphic sectional curvature Kähler manifold due to B.-Y. Chen and K. Ogiue
[
26
]
.
Applied with
h
the induced metric and
\omega
the twisted second fundamental form of a mean curvature zero compact immersed Lagrangian submanifold in a
2n
-dimensional Kähler manifold of constant holomorphic section curvature
\hat{c}
as in Theorem
4.2
, Theorem
5.5
recovers the specialization to such hypersurfaces of a theorem of Chen-Ogiue
[
26
, Theorem
4.1
]
. Concretely, in this case,
c=-1
,
\kappa=\hat{c}n(n-1)
, and
|\omega|^{2}=\hat{c}n(n-1)-\mathit{S}
, so (
5.14
) becomes

(5.17)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&\geq\int_{M}|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2n-1}\hat{c}-|\omega|^{2}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h}=n^{2}(n-1)^{2}\int_{M}\left(\hat{c}-\tfrac{\mathit{S}}{n(n-1)}\right)\left(\tfrac{\mathit{S}}{n(n-1)}-\tfrac{2n(n-2)}{(2n-1)(n-1)}\hat{c}\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h},\end{aligned}

which recovers
[
26
, Theorem
4.1
]
.
From (
5.17
) it follows that, if
\hat{c}>0
, then either
\mathit{S}=n(n-1)\hat{c}
and
\omega
is identically zero, so that the hypersurface is a totally geodesic hypersphere;
\mathit{S}=\tfrac{2n^{2}(n-2)}{2n-1}\hat{c}
and
|\omega|^{2}=\tfrac{n(n+1)}{2n-1}\hat{c}
, in which case
\omega
is parallel; or
\inf_{M}\mathit{S}<\tfrac{2n^{2}(n-2)}{2n-1}\hat{c}
.

One point here is that these examples are somehow the same result for different values of
k
. The corresponding statement obtained here for
k>3
is plainly not sharp, because certain inequalities for norms of tensors used in intermediate steps that are sharp for
k\leq 3
can be improved when
k>3
. However, even when
k\leq 3
, the method of proof has the virtues, when compared with previous approaches, that it is uniform in
k
and that it does not suppose an immersion in an ambient space.

5.3.
Review of Weitzenböck formulas

The operator
\mathscr{K}
defined in (
3.23
) maps the vector space
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
into
\Gamma(\mathscr{C}^{k+1}(T^{\ast}M))
where

(5.18)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{k+1}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})=\left\{\phi_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}\in\otimes^{k+1}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}:\begin{aligned} &\phi_{iji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=\phi_{[ij]i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=\phi_{ij(i_{1}\dots i_{k-1})},\\
&\phi_{pji_{1}\dots i_{k-2}}\,^{p}=0,\phi_{[iji_{s}]i_{1}\dots\hat{i}_{s}\dots i_{k-1}}=0\,\,\text{for}\,\,1\leq s\leq k-1.\end{aligned}\right\}

comprises trace-free
(k+1)
-tensors having the symmetries determined by the Young projector given by symmetrization over the rows followed by antisymmetrization over the columns of the Young diagram corresponding to the partition
(k,1)
.
The formal adjoint
\mathscr{K}^{\ast}:\Gamma(\mathscr{C}^{k+1}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
satisfies

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{\ast}(\phi)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=-D^{p}\phi_{p(i_{1}\dots i_{k})},

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=-D^{p}\mathscr{K}(\omega)_{p(i_{1}\dots i_{k})}.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
(M,h)
be an
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold.

(1)

The differential operator
\mathscr{L}:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{k+1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
has injective symbol, so

(5.20)

\displaystyle\Gamma(S^{k+1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))=\mathscr{L}(\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)))\oplus(\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\Gamma(S^{k+1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))),

and
\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
is an elliptic operator. If
M
is compact, then
\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}
is nonpositive and
\ker\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}=\ker\mathscr{L}
.

(2)

For any
c\geq\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{2(n+k-3)}
, the operator
\diamond_{c}=(\mathscr{K},\sqrt{c}\operatorname{\delta}):\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(\mathscr{C}^{k+1}(T^{\ast}M)\oplus S^{k-1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
has injective symbol, so

(5.21)

\displaystyle\diamond_{c}^{\ast}\diamond_{c}=-\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}+c\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}

is an elliptic operator. If
M
is compact, then
\diamond_{c}
is nonpositive and
\ker\diamond_{c}=\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
.

(3)

For any
c\geq\tfrac{(k+1)(n+2(k-1))}{2k(n+k-3)}
, the operator
\square_{c}=(\mathscr{K},\sqrt{c}\mathscr{L}):\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(\mathscr{C}^{k+1}(T^{\ast}M)\oplus S^{k+1}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
has injective symbol, so

(5.22)

\displaystyle\square_{c}^{\ast}\square_{c}=-\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}+c\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}

is an elliptic operator. If
M
is compact, then
\square_{c}
is nonpositive and
\ker\square_{c}=\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\mathscr{L}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)
,
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)
, and
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\operatorname{\delta}}(Z)(\phi)
for the symbols of
\mathscr{L}
,
\mathscr{K}
, and
\operatorname{\delta}
applied to the vector
Z^{i}
and
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. Write
(i(Z)\phi)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=Z^{p}\phi_{pi_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}
.
Straightforward computations show

(5.23)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}&=\tfrac{1}{k+1}|Z|^{2}|\phi|^{2}+\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(k+1)(n+2(k-1))}|i(Z)\phi|^{2},\end{aligned}

(5.24)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}&=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(|Z|^{2}|\phi|^{2}-|i(Z)\phi|^{2}\right)-\tfrac{k-1}{2(n+k-3)}|i(Z)\phi|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(|\phi|^{2}-\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}|i(Z)\phi|^{2}\right).\end{aligned}

When
k=1
and
n=2
the coefficient of the pure trace terms in (
5.24
) should be understood in a limiting sense.
By (
5.23
), if
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)=0
for nonzero
Z
, then
\phi=0
, so
\mathscr{L}
has injective symbol. The remaining claims follow from standard elliptic operator theory as in
[
7
, section
4
]
. If
M
is compact, then
\left(\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}\omega,\omega\right)=-\lVert\mathscr{L}(\omega)\rVert^{2}-c\lVert\operatorname{\delta}\omega\rVert^{2}\leq 0
and
\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}\omega=0
if and only if
\mathscr{L}(\omega)=0
.
Combining
|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\operatorname{\delta}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}=|\iota(Z)\phi|^{2}
with (
5.24
) yields

(5.25)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\diamond_{c}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2}|Z|^{2}|\phi|^{2}+\left(c-\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}\right)|i(Z)\phi|^{2},

from which the injectivity of
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\diamond_{c}}(Z)
is apparent. The ellipticity of
\diamond_{c}
follows from standard elliptic operator theory as in
[
7
, section
6
]
. If
M
is compact, then
\left(\diamond_{c}\omega,\omega\right)=-\lVert\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rVert^{2}-c\lVert\operatorname{\delta}\omega\rVert^{2}\leq 0
and
\diamond_{c}\omega=0
if and only if
\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0
and
\operatorname{\delta}\omega=0
.
Combining (
5.23
) and (
5.24
) shows

(5.26)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\diamond_{c}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}=\tfrac{k+1+2c}{2(k+1)}|Z|^{2}|\phi|^{2}+\left(\tfrac{ck(n+2(k-2)}{(k+1)(n+2(k-1))}-\tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}\right)|i(Z)\phi|^{2},

from which the injectivity of
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\square_{c}}(Z)
is apparent. The ellipticity of
\square_{c}
follows from standard elliptic operator theory. If
M
is compact, then
\left(\square_{c}\omega,\omega\right)=-\lVert\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rVert^{2}-c\lVert\mathscr{L}\omega\rVert^{2}\leq 0
and
\square_{c}\omega=0
if and only if
\mathscr{K}(\omega)=0
and
\mathscr{L}(\omega)=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
(M,h)
be a Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
define a formally self-adjoint second order elliptic differential operator
\square_{\alpha}:\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\to\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
by

(5.27)

\displaystyle\square_{\alpha}\omega=\Delta_{h}\omega+\tfrac{\alpha}{k}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega).

(1)

If
\alpha=-1
, then

(5.28)

\displaystyle\square_{-1}=\Delta_{h}\omega-\tfrac{1}{k}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)-2\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega),

is an elliptic operator on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. If
M
is compact, then
\square_{-1}
is nonpositive and
\ker\square_{-1}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))=\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.

(2)

If
\alpha=\tau(k)=\tfrac{k}{n+k-2}
, then

(5.29)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \square_{\tau(k)}\omega=\Delta_{h}\omega+\tfrac{1}{n+k-2}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)&=\tfrac{n+2(k-1)}{n+k-2}\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)-\tfrac{2k(n+k-3)}{(k+1)(n+k-2)}\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega),\end{aligned}

is an elliptic operator on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. If
M
is compact, then
\square_{\tau(k)}
is nonpositive and
\ker\square_{\tau(k)}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))=\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.

(3)

If
-1<\alpha<\tau(k)=\tfrac{k}{n+k-2}
then
\square_{\alpha}
is an elliptic operator on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. If
M
is compact, then
\square_{\alpha}
is nonpositive and
\ker\square_{\alpha}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))=\ker D\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.

Proof.

For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
, straightforward computations using the Ricci identity and (
3.20
) show

(5.30)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)&=\tfrac{1}{2}\{h,\operatorname{\delta}\omega\}-\tfrac{2}{k(n+2(k-2))}\mathsf{X}_{+}(\operatorname{\delta}^{2}(\omega)),\end{aligned}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta_{h}\omega+\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)&=(k+1)\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)-\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{n+2(k-1)}\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega).\end{aligned}

Differentiating (
3.29
) and tracing the result using (
5.30
) and (
5.19
) gives

(5.32)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta_{h}\omega&=\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)+\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)-\tfrac{2k}{k+1}\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega).\end{aligned}

Solving (
5.31
) for
\Delta_{h}\omega
and substituting the result into (
5.32
) yields

(5.33)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\tfrac{1}{k}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)=\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)-\tfrac{(n+k-2)(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)+\tfrac{2}{k+1}\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega).\end{aligned}

Equation (
5.31
) and (
5.33
) are the analogues of the corresponding identities for operators on antisymmetric forms, for example
[
92
, Equations
(2.8)
and
(2.9)
]
. Rewriting (
5.32
) in two different ways using (
5.31
) gives (
5.28
) and (
5.29
). The ellipticity of
\square_{\alpha}
in these cases, and its nonpositivity when
M
is compact, follow from Lemma
5.9
. Being convex combinations of the elliptic operators
\square_{-1}
and
\square_{\tau(k)}
, the operators
\square_{\alpha}
for
-1<\alpha<\tau(k)
are elliptic. If
M
is compact, the same argument shows that
\square_{\alpha}
is nonpositive. By (
5.28
),

(5.34)

\displaystyle\square_{\alpha}\omega=(1+\alpha)\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)+\tfrac{(n+2(k-2))(k-\alpha(n+k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)+\tfrac{2(\alpha-k)}{k+1}\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega),

from which follows
\ker\square_{\alpha}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\supset\ker D\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.
If
M
is compact and
\omega\in\ker\square\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
, integrating the right side of (
5.34
) gives

(5.35)

\displaystyle 0=(1+\alpha)\lVert\mathscr{L}(\omega)\rVert^{2}+\tfrac{(n+2(k-2))(k-\alpha(n+k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\lVert\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)\rVert^{2}+\tfrac{2(k-\alpha)}{k+1}\lVert\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rVert^{2},

and together with (
3.29
) this implies
