\ker\square_{\alpha}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\subset\ker D\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

If
h
is a Riemannian metric on a compact manifold
M
of dimension
n\geq 2
, and
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker(\Delta_{h}-\tfrac{1}{k}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}})
, then
\omega
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
3.14
.

Proof.

Since
M
is compact, and
\Delta_{h}-\frac{1}{k}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}=\square_{-1}
, Lemma
5.10
shows that
\ker(\Delta_{h}-\frac{1}{k}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}})\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))=\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.
∎

Example 5.12
.

Let
M
be compact. Define a functional
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
with arguments a Riemannian metric
h
and a tensor
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
by
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(h,\omega)=-\left(\omega,\square_{\alpha}\omega\right)
. For fixed
h
the first variation of
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(h,\omega)
in
\omega
yields the equation
\square_{\alpha}\omega=0
. Lemma
5.10
implies that for
-1\leq\alpha\leq\tfrac{k}{n+k-2}
the functional
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}(h,\omega)
is nonnegative.

Example 5.13
.

The
Lichnerowicz Laplacian

\diamond_{L}
is the formally self-adjoint operator which acts on an arbitrary rank
k
covariant tensor
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}
by
-\Delta_{h}\omega+\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)
(see
[
75
, page
27
]
for the definition of
\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)
for general tensors
\omega
). The linearization of the Ricci curvature of the metric
h
at the symmetric two-tensor
a_{ij}
is
\tfrac{1}{2}\diamond_{L}a_{ij}+D_{(i}D^{p}a_{j)p}
. On differential forms the Lichnerowicz Laplacian restricts to the usual Hodge Laplacian. The Lichnerowicz operator restricts to
-\square_{-k}
on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
.

Example 5.14
.

The special case of
\square_{-1}=\Delta_{h}-\tfrac{1}{2}\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}
acting on
\Gamma(S^{2}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
was studied by J. Simons in
[
97
]
, and this case of Lemma
5.10
is given in
[
7
, section
6
.c]
.

Example 5.15
.

As is shown in
[
7
]
, an infinitesimal deformation of an Einstein metric
h
on a compact manifold is identified with an
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{2}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
solving
\Delta\omega=\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)-\tfrac{2\mathscr{R}}{n}\omega
. As is summarized in
[
8
, section
12
.H]
(the notations there are different than those here), using this equation in conjunction with the positivity conditions given by integrating (
5.31
) and (
5.28
) gives a proof of the criterion of N. Koiso
[
70
, Theorem
3.3
]
, for the rigidity of an Einstein metric, in particular showing that an Einstein metric of negative sectional curvature is rigid provided
n\geq 3
.

The Weitzenböck formulas of Corollary
5.16
are in
[
43
, Section
6.4
]
, in
[
58
, Section
6
]
(where they are obtained as special cases of the general machinery of
[
93
]
), and in some equivalent form in
[
35
]
.

Corollary 5.16
.

Let
(M,h)
be a Riemannian manifold of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
there hold

(5.36)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2}

\displaystyle=|D\omega|^{2}+(k+1)\langle\omega,\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)\rangle-\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{n+2(k-1)}\langle\omega,\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)\rangle-\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega).

(5.37)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2}&=|D\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{n+2(k-1)}{n+k-2}\langle\omega,\operatorname{\delta}\mathscr{L}(\omega)\rangle-\tfrac{2k(n+k-3)}{(k+1)(n+k-2)}\langle\omega,\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rangle-\tfrac{1}{n+k-2}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega).\end{aligned}

(5.38)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2}

\displaystyle=|D\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}\langle\omega,\mathscr{L}\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)\rangle-2\langle\omega,\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rangle+\tfrac{1}{k}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega).

Proof.

Contracting (
5.31
), (
5.29
), and (
5.28
) with
\omega
yields (
5.36
)-(
5.38
).
∎

5.4.
Refined Kato inequalities and vanishing theorems for conformal Killing and divergence-free Codazzi tensors

The inequality (
5.39
) of Lemma
5.17
generalizes the estimate for the second fundamental form of a minimal hypersurface proved in
[
91
]
. With a
1
in place of
\tfrac{n+k-2}{n+2(k-1)}
, it follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and is known as a
Kato inequality
. The estimates (
5.39
)-(
5.41
) of Lemma
5.17
are
refined Kato inequalities
in the sense of
[
19
]
and
[
14
]
(see also
[
61
]
), and can be deduced from the results in either of those papers. In particular the results of
[
19
, Section
6
]
include Lemma
5.17
, and the discussion at the very end of
[
19
, Section
6
]
gives the explicit constants of (
5.39
)-(
5.41
) for the cases
k=1,2
. (The
k=2
case of (
5.39
) had earlier been stated in
[
11
, section
4
]
.) As considerable work is required to translate general representation theoretic statements into the concrete contexts here, and to keep the exposition self-contained, it is simpler to give here direct proofs following the general procedure described in the introduction of
[
14
]
, and not utilizing general representation theoretic machinery. These proofs were given previously in
[
43
]
.

It is convenient to call a tensor
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))
a
dual Killing tensor
if
\omega^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\in\Gamma(S^{k}(TM))
is a Killing tensor.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
(M,h)
be a Riemannian
n
-manifold with Levi-Civita connection
D
, and let
k\geq 1
.

For a trace-free Codazzi tensor
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
, where
|\phi|^{2}\neq 0
there holds

(5.39)

\displaystyle|d|\phi||^{2}\leq\tfrac{n+k-2}{n+2(k-1)}|D\phi|^{2}.

For a trace-free dual Killing tensor
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
, where
|\phi|^{2}\neq 0
there holds

(5.40)

\displaystyle|d|\phi||^{2}\leq\tfrac{k}{k+1}|D\phi|^{2}.

For
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}
, where
|\phi|^{2}\neq 0
there holds

(5.41)

\displaystyle|d|\phi||^{2}\leq\tfrac{k}{n+2(k-1)}|D\phi|^{2}.

Proof.

Write
\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)
for the symbol of
\mathscr{L}
applied to the vector
Z
and
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
, and similarly for
\mathscr{K}
and
\operatorname{\delta}
. Write
(i(Z)\phi)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}=Z^{p}\phi_{pi_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}
and suppose
Z
has unit norm. The nonnegativity of
|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}
in (
5.24
) yields

(5.42)

\displaystyle\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}|i(Z)\phi|^{2}\leq|\phi|^{2}.

(When
k=1
and
n=2
the coefficient of the pure trace terms in (
5.24
) should be understood in a limiting sense.)
Together (
5.23
) and (
5.42
) give

(5.43)

\displaystyle|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{k+1}|\phi|^{2}+\tfrac{k(n+k-3)}{(k+1)(n+2(k-1))}|\phi|^{2}=\tfrac{n+k-2}{n+2(k-1)}|\phi|^{2}.

Contracting (
3.29
) with
\mathsf{\sigma}_{D}(Z)(\omega)
and using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality shows

(5.44)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\tfrac{1}{2}\iota(Z)d|\phi|^{2}=Z^{i}\phi^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}D_{i}\phi_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\langle\mathsf{\sigma}_{D}(Z)(\phi),D\phi\rangle\\
&=\langle\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi),\mathscr{L}(\phi)\rangle+\tfrac{2k}{k+1}\langle\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi),\mathscr{K}(\phi)\rangle+\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\langle i(Z)\phi,\operatorname{\delta}(\phi)\rangle,\\
&\leq|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)||\mathscr{L}(\phi)|+\tfrac{2k}{k+1}|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)||\mathscr{K}(\phi)|+\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}|i(Z)\phi||\operatorname{\delta}(\phi)|.\end{aligned}

Suppose
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
. By (
3.30
),
|D\phi|^{2}=|\mathscr{L}(\phi)|^{2}
. Substituting this and (
5.43
) into (
5.44
) gives

(5.45)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\phi|^{2}|\iota(Z)d|\phi||^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}|\iota(Z)d|\phi|^{2}|^{2}&\leq|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}|\mathscr{L}(\phi)|^{2}=|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{L}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}\leq\tfrac{n+k-2}{n+2(k-1)}|\phi|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}.\end{aligned}

This holds for all unit norm
Z
, so shows (
5.39
).

Suppose
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
. By (
3.30
),
|D\phi|^{2}=\tfrac{2k}{k+1}|\mathscr{K}(\phi)|^{2}
, and, by (
5.24
),
2|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}\leq|\phi|^{2}
. In (
5.44
) these give

(5.46)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\phi|^{2}|\iota(Z)d|\phi||^{2}=\tfrac{1}{4}|\iota(Z)d|\phi|^{2}|^{2}&\leq(\tfrac{2k}{k+1})^{2}|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}|\mathscr{K}(\phi)|^{2}\\
&=\tfrac{2k}{k+1}|\mathsf{\sigma}_{\mathscr{K}}(Z)(\phi)|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}\leq\tfrac{k}{n+1}|\phi|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}.\end{aligned}

This holds for all unit norm
Z
, so shows (
5.40
).

Suppose
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}
. By (
3.30
),
|D\phi|^{2}=\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}|\operatorname{\delta}(\phi)|^{2}
. With (
5.42
) in (
5.44
) this gives

(5.47)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\phi|^{2}|\iota(Z)d|\phi||^{2}&=\tfrac{1}{4}|\iota(Z)d|\phi|^{2}|^{2}\leq\left(\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\right)^{2}|i(Z)\phi|^{2}|\operatorname{\delta}(\phi)|^{2}\\
&=\tfrac{k(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}|i(Z)\phi|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}\leq\tfrac{k}{n+2(k-1)}|\phi|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}.\end{aligned}

This holds for all unit norm
Z
, so shows (
5.41
).
∎

Remark 5.18
.

When
n=2
the inequalities (
5.39
)-(
5.41
) are in fact equalities
[
44
, Section
3
]
.

Remark 5.19
.

The proof of Lemma
5.17
shows that if
\phi\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
then the largest eigenvalue
\mu
of the symmetric two-tensor
\operatorname{\rho}(\phi\owedge\phi)
satisfies
\mu\leq\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|\phi|^{2}
. By (
5.42
), for any vector field
X
there holds
X^{i}X^{j}\operatorname{\rho}(\phi\owedge\phi)_{ij}=|i(X)\operatorname{\rho}(\phi)|^{2}\leq\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|\phi|^{2}|X|^{2}
, which suffices to show the claim. This means
\operatorname{\rho}(\phi\owedge\phi)\leq\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|\phi|^{2}h
.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
h
be a Riemannian metric on a manifold
M
of dimension
n>2
and let
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. Wherever
\omega\neq 0
there hold

(5.48)

\displaystyle|\omega|^{(n+2(k-1))/(n+k-2)}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{(n-2)/(n+k-2)}\geq\tfrac{n-2}{k(n+k-2)}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega),

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,\omega\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta},

(5.49)

\displaystyle|\omega|^{(k+1)/k}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{(k-1)/k}\geq-\tfrac{2(k-1)}{k+1}\langle\omega,\mathscr{K}^{\ast}\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rangle,

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,\omega\in\ker\mathscr{L}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta},

(5.50)

\displaystyle|\omega|^{(n+2(k-1))/k}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{(2-n)/k}\leq\tfrac{n-2}{k(n+k-2)}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega),

\displaystyle\text{if}\,\,\omega\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\mathscr{L}.

Proof.

Let
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. Wherever
|\omega|>0
there holds

(5.51)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2\lambda}|\omega|^{2(1-\lambda)}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2\lambda}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2}+2(\lambda-1)|d|\omega||^{2}.

Combining (
5.51
) with (
5.17
), (
5.32
), (
5.37
), (
5.38
), and Lemma
5.17
yields (
5.48
)-(
5.50
).
∎

Lemma
5.20
recovers a number of apparently unrelated classical results.

Example 5.21
.

If
h
is flat and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
is harmonic, then
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=D_{i_{1}}\dots D_{i_{k}}f\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
. By Lemma
5.20
, the function
|\omega|^{p}
is subharmonic for all
p\geq(n-2)/(n+k-2)
. For the flat Euclidean connection on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
k=1
this is
[
98
, Theorem
A
]
, and for
k>1
it is
[
20
, Theorem
1
]
. In the opposite direction,
[
99
, Theorem
2(b)
]
shows that on flat Euclidean space the best
p
for which
|\omega|^{p}
is subharmonic is
(n-2)/(n+k-2)
, and
[
99
, Theorem
2(a)
]
shows that on flat Euclidean space, given any section
\omega
of
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
there is around every point a neighborhood
U
and a harmonic function
f\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that on
U
there holds
\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=D_{i_{1}}\dots D_{i_{k}}f
.

For general
h
, if
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
is harmonic then
df\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
, and Lemma
5.20
shows that

(5.52)

\displaystyle(n-1)|df|^{n/(n-1)}\Delta_{h}|df|^{(n-2)/(n-1)}\geq(n-2)\mathit{Ric}(df,df).

If
h
has nonnegative Ricci curvature it follows that
|df|^{p}
is subharmonic for any
p\geq(n-2)/(n-1)
.

The rest of this section records some vanishing theorems for conformal Killing and trace-free Codazzi tensors. These are relevant here for identifying contexts in which the equations (
TFC1
) or (
TFC2
) admit only solutions
(h,\omega)
that are trivial in the sense that
\omega
vanish identically.

These vanishing theorems for conformal Killing tensors and trace and divergence free Codazzi tensors are analogous to the somewhat stronger vanishing theorems for symmetric tensors on Kähler manifolds obtained by S. Kobayashi
[
67
,
68
]
. For compact Riemann surfaces such tensors are the real parts of sections of powers of the canonical bundle so more precise statements are available; see
[
44
]
.

The statements of these vanishing theorems have several subtle aspects. First, the Weitzenböck identities lead naturally to hypotheses involving the definiteness of the quadratic form determined by the curvature operator on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. Different authors define the curvature operator slightly differently; in particular it may or may not involve a term in the Ricci curvature (as it does here). Second, it is desirable to formulate hypotheses in terms of sectional curvature and some extra work is required to relate a condition on sectional curvature with the definiteness of the quadratic form determined by the curvature operator (however defined). Third, when the hypothesis is positivity or negativity of the sectional curvature, this implies the same condition for the Ricci curvature, and implies the definiteness of the curvature operator on trace-free symmetric tensors, whatever the particular definition used for the latter. Finally, when the manifold is not compact the arguments require the Kato inequalities, and the sharpest results require the refined Kato inequalities.

The first vanishing theorem for trace-free Codazzi tensors on compact manifolds is due to M. Berger and D. Ebin
[
7
]
for rank two trace-free Codazzi tensors. Its extension to higher rank Codazzi tensors is due to S. Stepanov
[
100
, Theorem
2
]
. Subsequent refinements can be found in
[
43
,
58
,
94
,
101
]
. A good summary of previous work is in
[
101
]
. The strongest result in this direction is Theorem
5.26
below, which is the slight sharpening of
[
101
, Theorem
5
]
that results from using the refined Kato inequality (
5.39
) in place of the usual Kato inequality. The analogous vanishing theorem for conformal Killing tensors is in
[
43
, Theorem
6.2
]
, while the improved result with a hypothesis on sectional curvature was proved in
[
35
, Theorem
1.6
]
.

It is convenient to say that
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is positive or negative (semi-)definite on
S^{k}(T^{\ast}M)
or
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
if it is positive (semi-)definite or negative (semi-)definite as a quadratic form on
\Gamma(S^{k}(T^{\ast}M))
or
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. Since, by Lemma (
5.1
),
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(h^{\odot k})=0
for any
k\geq 1
,
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is not definite on
\Gamma(S^{2k}(T^{\ast}M))
for any
k\geq 1
.

Theorem 5.22

(
[
43
]
)
.

Let
(M,h)
be a compact Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2
.

(1)

If
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is nonnegative on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
then any
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
is parallel. If moreover
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is at some point of
M
strictly positive on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
then
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}=\{0\}
.

(2)

If
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is nonpositive on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
then any rank
k
conformal Killing tensor is parallel, and if, moreover,
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is at some point of
M
strictly negative on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
, then any rank
k
conformal Killing tensor is identically zero.

Proof.

For a compactly supported
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
, integrating any of (
5.36
)-(
5.38
) by parts against the Riemannian volume
\operatorname{vol}_{h}
and simplifying the result using (
3.30
) yields

(5.53)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{2}{k+1}\lVert|\mathscr{K}(\omega)\rVert^{2}&+\tfrac{(n+k-2)(n+2(k-2))}{(n+k-3)(n+2(k-1))}\lVert\operatorname{\delta}(\omega)\rVert^{2}-\lVert\mathscr{L}(\omega)\rVert^{2}=\tfrac{1}{k}\int_{M}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)\,d\operatorname{vol}_{h}.\end{aligned}

The identity (
5.53
) generalizes the usual integrated Bochner identities for harmonic one-forms and conformal Killing vector fields.
If
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
and
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}\geq 0
then (
5.53
) shows that
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{L}
and from (
3.30
) it follows that
D\omega=0
. If moreover
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is somewhere positive then (
5.38
) shows
\omega=0
. If
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}\leq 0
then (
5.53
) shows that
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
and from (
3.30
) it follows that
D\omega=0
. If, moreover,
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is somewhere negative then (
5.37
) shows
\omega=0
.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

A result very similar to (
1
) of Theorem
5.22
was obtained in
[
100
, Theorem
2
]
. The difference is that the operator
\wideparen{\mathscr{R}}
and corresponding quadratic form
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
considered in
[
100
]
are slightly different (they differ by a term involving the Ricci curvature). The claim here is very slightly more general, but, when the curvature is assumed to have a definite sign, since this sign is inherited by the Ricci tensor, the ambit of application of the claims is the same.

Corollary 5.24
.

Let
h
be a Riemannian metric on a compact manifold
M
of dimension
n>2
.

(1)

(Stepanov,
[
100
, Theorem
2
]
; see also
[
94
, Corollary
1
]
) If
h
has nonnegative sectional curvature, then a trace-free Codazzi tensor
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}
is parallel. If, moreover, the sectional curvature is strictly positive at some point of
M
then
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}=\{0\}
.

(2)

(Dairbekov-Sharafutdinov,
[
35
, Theorem
1.6
]
; see also
[
58
, Proposition
6.6
]
) If
h
has nonpositive sectional curvature, then a rank
k
conformal Killing tensor is parallel, and if the sectional curvature is moreover strictly negative at some point of
M
, then a rank
k
conformal Killing tensor is identically zero.

Proof.

Both claims follow from Theorem
5.22
once it is known that a sign condition on the sectional curvature of
h
implies the same sign condition for
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
on
\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
. When
k=2
this follows from the proof of
[
7
, Proposition
6.1
]
, but that argument (which is direct) does not extend straightforwardly to the case
k>2
. For conformal Killing tensors, claim (
2
) is
[
35
, Theorem
1.6
]
. A proof of the result of Dairbekov-Sharafutdinov for all
k
based on Weitzenböck formulas as in Theorem
5.22
was given as
[
58
, Proposition
6.6
]
; they show via an integration in the fibers argument that the condition (
2
) of Theorem
5.22
follows from the nonpositivity of the sectional curvature. Their argument works equally well assuming nonnegativity of the sectional curvature, and combined with Theorem
5.22
, this yields corollary
5.24
. Alternatively, in the case of trace-free Codazzi tensors, the proof of
[
94
, Corollary
1
]
shows how to deduce the required nonnegativity for
k>2
from the Berger-Ebin argument.
∎

Remark 5.25
.

The
n=2
case of Corollary
5.24
is proved in
[
44
, section
3
]
.

Theorem
5.26
refines
[
101
, Theorem
5
]
, where it is shown there holds the same statement for all
p>q
for some
q>0
that is not determined. The specific
q
obtained here follows from the refined Kato inequality.

Theorem 5.26
.

Let
(M,h)
be a complete Riemannian manifold of dimension
n>2
and let
k\geq 2
. If
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is nonnegative on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
(in particular if
h
has nonnegative sectional curvature) and a trace-free Codazzi tensor
\omega\in\ker\mathscr{K}\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))
is in
L^{p}(M,\operatorname{vol}_{h})
for
p>\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-2}
, then:

(1)

If
\operatorname{vol}_{h}(M)=\infty
then
\omega
vanishes identically.

(2)

If
\operatorname{vol}_{h}(M)<\infty
then
\omega
is parallel and
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)
vanishes identically. In particular if at some point of
M

\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is strictly positive on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
, then
\omega
vanishes identically.

Proof.

Because
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}
is nonnegative on
S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M)
, by Lemma
5.20
,
u=|\omega|^{(n-2)/(n+k-2)}
is a nonnegative subharmonic function on
M
. Because
h
is complete, by a theorem of Yau
[
106
]
, if
u\in L^{q}(M,\operatorname{vol}_{h})
for
q\in(1,\infty)
, then
u
must be constant. Hence if
\omega\in L^{p}(M,\operatorname{vol}_{h})
for
p>\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-2}
, then
|\omega|
is constant. If
\operatorname{vol}_{h}(M)=\infty
then no constant function is in
L^{p}(\operatorname{vol}_{h})
for any
p>0
, so
\omega
vanishes identically. Suppose
\operatorname{vol}_{h}(M)<\infty
. By (
5.38
),
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)=0
and
D\omega=0
.
∎

The result of Theorem
5.26
is sharp in the following sense. For
k\geq 2
, on flat Euclidean space let
P
be a harmonic polynomial homogeneous of degree
k
. Then
\omega=D^{(k)}P
is a parallel trace-free Codazzi tensor. Because it is parallel it descends to any flat torus. Evidently in this case
\omega
is in
L^{p}
for all
p>0
.

5.5.
Bounding norms of Kulkarni-Nomizu products

What follows obtains estimates of the quantities
\Lambda_{n,k}
and
\lambda_{n,k}
defined in (
5.10
).

A tensor
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is
critical
if it is a critical point of the restriction to the unit sphere
\{\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}:|\omega|^{2}=1\}
of the expression (
5.9
). By Lemma
5.27
,
\Lambda_{n,k}
and
\lambda_{n,k}
are eigenvalues of the operator
\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}
determined by a critical
\omega
.

Lemma 5.27
.

A critical
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
satisfies
\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega)=\theta\omega
for some
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Consider a one-parameter family
\omega(t)\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
and let
\dot{\omega}=\tfrac{d}{dt}\omega
. By (
5.7
) applied twice,

(5.54)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{d}{dt}\langle\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega),\omega\rangle&=\tfrac{d}{dt}\left(\binom{k}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+k|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\right)\\
&=4\left(\binom{k}{2}\langle\omega\owedge\omega,\omega\owedge\dot{\omega}\rangle+k\langle\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega),\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\dot{\omega}\rangle\right)=4\langle\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega),\dot{\omega}\rangle,\end{aligned}

while
\tfrac{d}{dt}|\omega|^{4}=4|\omega|^{2}\langle\omega,\dot{\omega}\rangle
. The claim follows.
∎

The entire
O(n)
orbit of a critical
\omega
comprises critical points. It would be interesting to characterize these critical orbits algebraically.

The
k=2
case is particularly simple.

Lemma 5.28
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. Any
\omega\in S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
satisfies

(5.55)

\displaystyle|\omega|^{4}=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega),\omega\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}.

In particular
\Lambda_{n,2}=1=\lambda_{n,2}
for all
n\geq 2
.

Proof.

A straightforward computation using the definition of
\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}
shows that
\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega)=2|\omega|^{2}\omega
. With (
5.7
) this yields (
5.55
).
∎

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. A trace-free symmetric tensor
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is
projectively flat
if
\omega\owedge\omega
is a multiple of
h\owedge h
.

Lemma 5.29
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 3
. For
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
,

(5.56)

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{k}\langle\wideparen{\omega\owedge\omega}(\omega),\omega\rangle=\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\geq\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{4},

with equality if and only if
\omega
is projectively flat. In particular,
\lambda_{n,k}\geq\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}
.

Proof.

By (
3.11
), for
\mathscr{Y}\in\mathscr{MC}(\mathbb{V}^{\ast})
,

(5.57)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\mathscr{Y}|^{2}&=|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\mathscr{Y}|^{2}+\tfrac{4}{n-2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{Y})|^{2}+\tfrac{2}{n(n-1)}(\mathit{S}(\mathscr{Y}))^{2}.\end{aligned}

By (
5.57
), for
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there holds

(5.58)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&=|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{4}{n-2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{2}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{4}_{h}.\end{aligned}

From (
5.58
) and
|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}=|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+n^{-1}|\omega|^{4}
there follows

(5.59)

\displaystyle\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}=\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n-2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{4},

for
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. The equality (
5.59
) implies (
5.56
) and equality holds in (
5.56
) if and only if both
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)=0
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)=0
, which by (
3.11
) imply that
\omega\owedge\omega
is a multiple of
h\owedge h
.
∎

Note that a priori it is not evident that there exist nonzero projectively flat elements of
S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. Examples
5.30
-
5.32
address this for
k\in\{2,3,4\}
.

Example 5.30
.

By Lemma
5.28
,
\lambda_{n,2}=1
for
n\geq 2
, so by Lemma
5.29
no nonzero element of
S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is projectively flat provided
n=\dim\mathbb{V}\geq 3
. A direct proof of this can be given as follows. Suppose
\omega\in S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
were projectively flat so that there be
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\omega\owedge\omega=\kappa h\owedge h
. Let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
be an
h
-orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{V}
with respect to which
\omega
is diagonal. Let
w_{i}=\omega(e_{i},e_{i})
. Then
w_{i}w_{j}=(\omega\owedge\omega)(e_{i},e_{j},e_{i},e_{j})=\kappa
for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
. Because
\omega
is trace free,
0=w_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}=w_{i}^{2}+(n-1)\kappa
, so that
0\geq\kappa=w_{i}w_{j}
and
w_{i}^{2}=(1-n)\kappa
. Were
\kappa<0
then, for distinct
i
and
j
,
w_{i}
and
w_{j}
would be nonzero with opposite signs but equal squares. This is impossible if
n>2
.

Example 5.31
.

It follows from
[
49
, Theorem
1.10
]
that, when
n=\dim\mathbb{V}>1
, in
S^{3}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there is a unique
CO(h)
orbit comprising nonzero projectively flat tensors. An example of a nonzero projectively flat tensor is as follows. Let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
be a basis of
\mathbb{V}
.
Define
h\in S^{2}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
by
h(e_{i},e_{i})=n
and
h(e_{i},e_{j})=-1
for
i\neq j
and extending bilinearly.
By construction the Gram matrix in the given basis is positive definite, so
h
is a Euclidean metric.
Define a commutative nonassociative product on
\mathbb{V}
by
e_{i}\circ e_{i}=(n-1)e_{i}
and
e_{i}\circ e_{j}=-e_{i}-e_{j}
for
i\neq j
and extending bilinearly. Define
\omega\in\otimes^{3}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
by
\omega(x,y,z)=h(x\circ y,z)
, so that
\omega(e_{i},e_{i},e_{i})=n(n-1)
,
\omega(e_{i},e_{i},e_{j})=-(n-1)
, and
\omega(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})=2
for distinct indices
i
,
j
, and
k
. It is straightforward to check that
\omega
is completely symmetric and
h
-trace-free so that
\omega\in S^{3}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. Define
L:\mathbb{V}\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V})
by
L(x)y=x\circ y
. The complete symmetry of
\omega
is equivalent to
h(L(x)u,v))=h(u,L(x)v)
.
It can be checked that
[L(a),L(b)]c=h(a,c)b-h(b,c)a
. It follows that

(5.60)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (\omega\owedge\omega)(a,b,c,d)&=h(c\circ a,b\circ d)-h(c\circ b,a\circ d)=h([L(b),L(a)]c,d)\\
&=h(b,c)h(a,d)-h(a,c)h(b,d)=-(h\owedge h)(a,b,c,d),\end{aligned}

which shows that
\omega\owedge\omega=-h\owedge h
so that
\omega
is projectively flat. There follow
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)=(n-1)h
,
|\omega|^{2}=\mathit{S}(\omega\owedge\omega)=n(n-1)
,
|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}=2n(n-1)
, and
|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}=n(n-1)^{2}
, from which it follows that equality holds in (
5.56
). This shows that
\lambda_{n,3}=\tfrac{n+1}{n(n-1)}
when
n>1
.

Example 5.32
.

Here is an example of a projectively flat tensor in
S^{4}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
when
n=\dim\mathbb{V}>2
. This shows that
\lambda_{n,4}=\tfrac{n+2}{n(n-1)}
when
n>2
. Let
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
be a basis of
\mathbb{V}
. Define a Euclidean metric
h
as in Example (
5.30
). In what follows,
i
,
j
,
k
, and
l
denote distinct indices from
\{1,\dots,n\}
. Define a commutative ternary product
[\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,,\,\cdot\,\,]:\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{V}
by extending multilinearly

(5.61)

\displaystyle[e_{i},e_{i},e_{i}]=(n-1)(n-2)e_{i},

\displaystyle[e_{i},e_{i},e_{j}]=-(n-2)(e_{i}+e_{j}),

\displaystyle[e_{i},e_{j},e_{k}]=2(e_{i}+e_{j}+e_{k}).

Define
\omega\in\otimes^{4}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
by
\omega(a,b,c,d)=h([a,b,c],d)
for
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{V}
, so that
\omega(e_{i},e_{i},e_{i},e_{i})=n(n-1)(n-2)
,
\omega(e_{i},e_{i},e_{i},e_{j})=-(n-1)(n-2)=\omega(e_{i},e_{i},e_{j},e_{j})
,
\omega(e_{i},e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})=2(n-2)
, and
\omega(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k},e_{l})=-6
. It is straightforward to check that
\omega
is completely symmetric and
h
-trace-free, so
\omega\in S^{4}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. Define
L:\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{V}\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V})
by
L(a,b)c=[a,b,c]
. The complete symmetry of
\omega
is equivalent to
h(L(x,y)u,v)=h(u,L(x,y)v)
. Using
(\omega\owedge\omega)(a,b,c,d)=\operatorname{\mathsf{tr}}(L(c,a)L(b,d)-L(c,b)L(a,d))
it is straightforward to calculate that
\omega\owedge\omega=\tfrac{(n+2)(n-2)}{n+1}h\owedge h
, showing that
\omega
is projectively flat. Whether every projectively flat element of
S^{4}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
is in the
CO(h)
orbit of the
\omega
constructed here has not been addressed, although it seems likely this is the case.

For
k>4
, I do not know if there exist projectively flat tensors in
S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
. There is an evident pattern in the construction of such tensors in the
k=3
and
k=4
cases that it appears probable continues for
k>4
, but the direct computations used to check these examples need to be replaced by some more conceptual argument involving
n
-ary operations, and developing this in the detail necessary would not be germane here.

Lemma 5.33
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
\omega\in S^{3}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
,

(5.62)

\displaystyle\tfrac{2n-1}{n}|\omega|^{4}\geq|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}.

so that
\Lambda_{n,3}\leq\frac{2n-1}{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\{e(1),\dots,e(n)\}
be an
h
-orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{V}
. In terms of the endomorphisms
\omega(i)_{j}\,^{k}=e(i)^{p}\omega_{pj}\,^{k}\in\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{V})
,
1\leq i\leq n
,

(5.63)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {}_{kl}&=\omega(i)_{pl}\omega(j)_{k}\,^{p}-\omega(j)_{pl}\omega(i)_{k}\,^{p}=e(i)^{a}e(j)^{b}(\omega_{pla}\omega_{kb}\,^{p}-\omega_{plb}\omega_{ka}\,^{p})\\
&=-2e(i)^{a}e(j)^{b}\omega_{k[a}\,^{p}\omega_{b]lp}=-e(i)^{a}e(j)^{b}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{abkl}.\end{aligned}

(It is because of the last two equalities of (
5.63
) that this proof works only for the
k=3
case.)
By
[
30
, Lemma
1
]
, for symmetric endomorphisms
A_{i}\,^{j}
and
B_{i}\,^{j}
of
\mathbb{V}
there holds
|[A,B]|^{2}\leq 2|A|^{2}|B|^{2}
, and applied with (
5.63
) this yields

(5.64)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|[\omega(i),\omega(j)]|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|[\omega(i),\omega(j)]|^{2}\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2}.\end{aligned}

There holds
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ab}e(i)^{a}e(j)^{b}=\langle\omega(i),\omega(j)\rangle
. Because
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)
is symmetric, the
h
-orthonormal basis
\{e(1),\dots,e(n)\}
can be chosen to be also orthogonal with respect to
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)
, which entails that
\langle\omega(i),\omega(j)\rangle=0
if
i\neq j
. In this case,

(5.65)

\displaystyle|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle\omega(i),\omega(j)\rangle^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{4}.

Combining (
5.64
) and (
5.65
) yields

(5.66)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{4}.\end{aligned}

There hold

(5.67)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{4}&=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{2}\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2}=|\omega|^{4}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2},\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{4}&=\tfrac{1}{2(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(|\omega(i)|^{2}-|\omega(j)|^{2}\right)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2}.\end{aligned}

The observations (
5.67
), which are key to the proof, appear in some equivalent form in the proof of
[
26
, Theorem
4.2
]
.
Summing
\tfrac{2n-1}{n}
times the first equation of (
5.67
) with
\tfrac{1-n}{n}
times the second equation of (
5.67
) yields

(5.68)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{4}+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2}=\tfrac{2n-1}{n}|\omega|^{4}-\tfrac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(|\omega(i)|^{2}-|\omega(j)|^{2}\right)^{2}.

Combining (
5.66
) and (
5.68
) yields

(5.69)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}|\omega(i)|^{2}|\omega(j)|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\omega(i)|^{4}\\
&=\tfrac{2n-1}{n}|\omega|^{4}-\tfrac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j\neq i}\left(|\omega(i)|^{2}-|\omega(j)|^{2}\right)^{2}\leq\tfrac{2n-1}{n}|\omega|^{4},\end{aligned}

which proves (
5.62
).
∎

I do not know the characterization of tensors saturating the bound (
5.62
).

Lemma 5.34
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
k\geq 2
and
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there hold

(5.70)

\displaystyle\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|\omega|^{4}\geq|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{n}|\omega|^{4},

(5.71)

\displaystyle\tfrac{(n-2)(n+k-2)}{n(n+2(k-2))}|\omega|^{4}\geq|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}.

Proof.

For
x_{i}\in\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
, the nonnegativity of the squared norm of

(5.72)

\displaystyle x_{[i}\omega_{j]i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}-\tfrac{1}{n+k-3}\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}h_{i_{s}[i}\omega_{j]i_{1}\dots\hat{i}_{s}\dots i_{k-1}p}x^{p},

(where
\hat{i}_{s}
denotes the omission of the index
i_{s}
) yields

(5.73)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&\leq 2x^{i}\omega^{ji_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}\left(x_{[i}\omega_{j]i_{1}\dots i_{k-1}}-\tfrac{1}{n+k-3}\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}h_{i_{s}[i}\omega_{j]i_{1}\dots\hat{i}_{s}\dots i_{k-1}p}x^{p}\right)\\
&=|x|^{2}|\omega|^{2}-|\iota(x)\omega|^{2}-\tfrac{k-1}{n+k-3}|\iota(x)\omega|^{2}=|x|^{2}|\omega|^{2}-\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n+k-3}|\iota(x)\omega|^{2},\end{aligned}

so that

(5.74)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}=|\iota(x)\omega|^{2}\leq\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|x|^{2}|\omega|^{2}.

This shows
\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|x|^{2}|\omega|^{2}h_{ij}-\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij}
is positive semidefinite. Because
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{ij}
is also positive semidefinite and the endomorphisms
\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|\omega|^{2}\delta_{i}\,^{j}-\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{i}\,^{j}
and
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)_{i}\,^{j}
commute, contracting with
\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)^{ij}
yields

(5.75)

\displaystyle\tfrac{n+k-3}{n+2(k-2)}|\omega|^{4}\geq|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}=|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{n}|\omega|^{4}_{h},

from which the inequalities (
5.70
) and (
5.71
) follow.
∎

Lemma 5.35
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
k\geq 2
and
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there holds

(5.76)

\displaystyle|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}\leq 4|\omega|^{4}.

Proof.

For
\tau_{ij}\in S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
,

(5.77)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&\leq(\tau_{ij}\omega_{klQ}-\tau_{kl}\omega_{ijqQ})(\tau^{ij}\omega^{klQ}-\tau^{kl}\omega^{ijQ})=|\tau|^{2}|\omega|^{2}-|\iota(\tau)\omega|^{2}\\
&=\tau^{ij}\tau^{kl}\left(|\omega|^{2}h_{k(i}h_{j)l}-\omega_{ijQ}\omega_{kl}\,^{Q}\right),\end{aligned}

where
\iota(\tau)\omega
is as in (
3.1
) and
Q
stands for
q_{1}\dots q_{k-2}
. This means that the symmetric quadratic form defined on
S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
by contracting
\tau^{ij}\tau^{kl}
with the tensor
|\omega|^{2}h_{k(i}h_{j)l}-\omega_{ijQ}\omega_{kl}\,^{Q}
is positive semidefinite. Since the quadratic form defined on
S^{2}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
by contracting
\tau^{ij}\tau^{kl}
with
\omega_{ijQ}\omega_{kl}\,^{Q}
is also positive semidefinite and the corresponding endomorphisms commute it follows that

(5.78)

\displaystyle 0\leq(|\omega|^{2}h_{k(i}h_{j)l}-\omega_{ijQ}\omega_{kl}\,^{Q})\omega^{ij}\,_{P}\omega^{kl}\,{}^{P}=|\omega|^{4}-\omega_{ijQ}\omega_{kl}\,^{Q}\omega^{ij}\,_{P}\omega^{kl}\,{}^{P},

(where
P
stands for
p_{1}\dots p_{k-2}
).
Define
\mathcal{M}=\omega_{j}\,^{kQ}\omega_{k}\,^{iP}\omega_{i}\,^{l}\,{}_{Q}\omega_{l}\,^{j}\,{}_{P}
.
From

(5.79)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&\leq 2\omega_{k(i}\,^{P}\omega_{j)lP}\omega^{k(i}\,_{Q}\omega^{j)lQ}=\omega_{kiP}\omega_{jl}\,^{P}\omega^{ki}\,_{Q}\omega^{jlQ}+\mathcal{M}\end{aligned}

it follows that

(5.80)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&=2\omega_{kiP}\omega_{jl}\,^{P}\omega^{ki}\,_{Q}\omega^{jlQ}-2\mathcal{M}\leq 4\omega_{kiP}\omega_{jl}\,^{P}\omega^{ki}\,_{Q}\omega^{jlQ}\leq 4|\omega|^{4},\end{aligned}

the last inequality by (
5.78
). This shows (
5.76
).
∎

Corollary 5.36
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
k\geq 2
and
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there hold

(5.81)

\displaystyle\left(4-\tfrac{2}{n(n-1)}\right)|\omega|^{4}\geq|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{4}{n-2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))|^{2}\geq|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}.

Proof.

From (
5.57
) there follows

(5.82)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&=|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{4}{n-2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega))|^{2}+\tfrac{2}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{4}_{h}\geq\tfrac{2}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{4}.\end{aligned}

Combining (
5.76
) with (
5.82
) yields (
5.81
).
∎

Corollary 5.37
.

Let
(\mathbb{V},h)
be a Euclidean vector space of dimension
n\geq 2
. For
k\geq 4
and
\omega\in S^{k}_{0}\mathbb{V}^{\ast}
there holds

(5.83)

\displaystyle\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\tfrac{(2n+1)(k-1)}{n}\right)|\omega|^{4}.

In particular,
\Lambda_{n,k}\leq 1+\tfrac{(2n+1)(k-1)}{n}
.

Proof.

By (
5.82
), (
5.71
), and (
5.81
),

(5.84)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}&+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}=\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{n+2(k-2)}{n-2}|\operatorname{\mathsf{tf}}\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}+\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{4}\\
&\leq\left(\tfrac{k-1}{2}\left(4-\tfrac{2}{n(n-1)}\right)+\tfrac{n+k-2}{n}+\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}\right)|\omega|^{4}\\
&=\left((k-1)\left(2-\tfrac{1}{n(n-1)}\right)+\tfrac{n+k-2}{n-1}\right)|\omega|^{4}=\left(1+\tfrac{(2n+1)(k-1)}{n}\right)|\omega|^{4},\end{aligned}

which shows the claim.
∎

5.6.
Proofs of Theorems
5.3
and
5.5

Theorem
5.38
is needed in the proof of Theorem
5.3
. Its statement can be found in the form given here, in the context of Hermitian manifolds, as
[
103
, Theorem
4.1
]
.

Theorem 5.38

(S. Y. Cheng and S. T. Yau,
[
29
, Corollary
1
on p.
857
]
,
[
27
, Corollary to Theorem
8
on p.
353
]
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a complete
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with Ricci curvature bounded from below by
-\kappa(n-1)g
for a real constant
\kappa\geq 0
. Suppose
u\in C^{2}(M)
is nonnegative and not identically
0
and satisfies
\Delta u\geq Bu^{1+\sigma}-Au
for constants
B>0
,
\sigma>0
, and
A\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
A\leq 0
, then
u
is identically zero, while, if
A>0
, there holds
\sup_{M}u\leq|A/B|^{1/\sigma}
.

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

By assumption,
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}
solves (
TFC1
) for
c>0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. By (
TFC1
),
\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{R})=c\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)+\tfrac{\kappa}{n}h
,
and
\mathit{S}(\mathscr{R})=c|\omega|^{2}+\kappa
. Together with (
5.7
) these observations yield

(5.85)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{k}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)&=\tfrac{k-1}{2}\langle\mathscr{R},\omega\owedge\omega\rangle+\langle\operatorname{\rho}(\mathscr{R}),\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)\rangle\\
&=c\left(\tfrac{k-1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\right)+\tfrac{\kappa(n+k-2)}{n(n-1)}|\omega|^{2}.\end{aligned}

Since
c>0
, it follows from (
5.85
) and (
5.56
) of Lemma
5.29
that

(5.86)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{k}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)\geq\left(c\lambda_{n,k}|\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{\kappa(n+k-2)}{n(n-1)}\right)|\omega|^{2}\geq\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}\left(c|\omega|^{2}+\kappa\right)|\omega|^{2}.\end{aligned}

Substituting (
5.86
) into (
5.48
) of Lemma
5.20
yields

(5.87)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Delta_{h}|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-2}}&\geq\tfrac{n-2}{k(n+k-2)}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)|\omega|^{-\tfrac{n+2(k-1)}{n+k-2}}\geq\tfrac{n-2}{n(n-1)}\left(c|\omega|^{2}+\kappa\right)|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-2}}\\
&=\tfrac{n-2}{n(n-1)}\left(c\left(|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-2}}\right)^{1+\tfrac{2(n+k-2)}{n-2}}+\kappa|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n+k-2}}\right).\end{aligned}

Because
h
is complete, Theorem
5.38
applies. If
\kappa\geq 0
, it implies
\omega
vanishes. In this case
h
is a metric of constant sectional curvature. If
\kappa<0
, Theorem
5.38
yields
\sup_{M}|\omega|^{2}\leq-\kappa/c
. This implies
\sup_{M}\mathit{S}\leq 0
.

When
k=2
, combining (
5.85
) with (
5.55
) yields

(5.88)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)&=c\left(\tfrac{1}{2}|\omega\owedge\omega|^{2}+|\operatorname{\rho}(\omega\owedge\omega)|^{2}\right)+\tfrac{\kappa}{n-1}|\omega|^{2}=c|\omega|^{4}+\tfrac{\kappa}{n-1}|\omega|^{2}=|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{\kappa}{n-1}+c|\omega|^{2}\right).\end{aligned}

Substituting (
5.88
) into (
5.48
) of Lemma
5.20
yields

(5.89)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{n}{n-2}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n}}&\geq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)|\omega|^{-\tfrac{n+2)}{n}}\geq\left(c|\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{\kappa}{n-1}\right)|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n}}=c\left(|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n}}\right)^{1+\tfrac{2n}{n-2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{n-1}|\omega|^{\tfrac{n-2}{n}}.\end{aligned}

The rest of the argument is as that following (
5.87
), but now yields
\sup_{M}|\omega|^{2}\leq-\tfrac{\kappa}{c(n-1)}
. This implies
\sup_{M}\mathit{S}\leq\tfrac{n-2}{n-1}\kappa<0
.
∎

Remark 5.39
.

Consider a pseudo-Riemannian space form
(N,g)
with constant negative sectional curvature
-c
(in Lorentzian signature such a space form is often called
anti de Sitter space
). Combining the
k=2
case of Theorem
4.1
with Theorem
5.3
shows that the squared-norm
|\mathbb{\Pi}|^{2}
of the second fundamental form of a mean curvature zero spacelike hypersurface complete in the induced metric satisfies
|\mathbb{\Pi}|^{2}\leq-\tfrac{1}{n+1}\mathit{S}_{g}=-nc
. This recovers a theorem of Ishihara
[
64
, Theorem
1.2
]
.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
and Corollary
5.6
.

By assumption
\omega\in\Gamma(S^{k}_{0}(T^{\ast}M))\cap\ker\operatorname{\delta}\cap\ker\mathscr{K}
solves (
TFC1
) for
c<0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. The equation (
5.85
) remains valid. For
k\geq 2
, since
c<0
, it follows from (
5.85
) and (
5.56
) of Lemma
5.29
that

(5.90)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tfrac{1}{k}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)\geq\left(c\Lambda_{n,k}|\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{\kappa(n+k-2)}{n(n-1)}\right)|\omega|^{2}.\end{aligned}

Combining (
5.38
) and (
5.90
) yields

(5.91)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{M}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2}d\operatorname{vol}_{h}=\int_{M}\left(|D\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{k}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h}\\
&\geq\int_{M}\left(|D\omega|^{2}+|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{n+k-2}{n(n-1)}\kappa+c\Lambda_{n,k}|\omega|^{2}\right)\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h},\end{aligned}

which shows Theorem
5.5
.
Taking
k=3
in (
5.91
) and using (
5.62
) of Lemma
5.33
yields (
5.14
). When
k>3
, (
5.83
) of Lemma
5.37
and (
5.91
) yield (
5.13
).

When
k=2
, combining (
5.38
) and (
5.85
) yields

(5.92)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 0&=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{M}\Delta_{h}|\omega|^{2}d\operatorname{vol}_{h}=\int_{M}\left(|D\omega|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{k}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathscr{R}}(\omega)\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h}\\
&\quad=\int_{M}\left(|D\omega|^{2}+|\omega|^{2}\left(\tfrac{\kappa}{n-1}\kappa+c|\omega|^{2}\right)\right)d\operatorname{vol}_{h},\end{aligned}

which shows (
5.15
).
∎
